A checkpoint whose reservation is rejected takes the same path as a storage failure: the runtime resumes, no chunk is written, and the checkpoint falls through to the last successful checkpoint.

**Change (the resume selector and the full-wins fence, spec/10:155).** The shipped selector is scoped to `partial = TRUE` rows and reads the row at `MAX(coordination_generation)`, which identifies a unique row only because `partial_manifest_active_uniq` admits one active partial per `(session_id, slot_id)`. Once the unification puts `partial = false` rows in the same table that uniqueness is gone: `coordination_generation` advances only on coordinator handoff (`spec/04:157`, "incremented on coordinator handoff across gateway replicas"), periodic checkpoints fire every `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`, and the latest-2 rotation deliberately retains two of them (`spec/12:325`), so a session's retained full rows and any later partial normally carry the same generation. Restate the selector on a total order: the newest active row for `(session_id, slot_id)` under `deleted_at IS NULL` ordered by `(coordination_generation DESC, checkpoint_started_at DESC, checkpoint_id DESC)`. A legal-hold session, exempt from rotation, accumulates rows at one generation, so the tiebreaker is what keeps its resume deterministic.

The full-wins fencing sentence in the same passage ("If a full-checkpoint row exists at or above the selected partial's `coordination_generation`, the full checkpoint wins over the partial and the partial-reassembly path below is bypassed") is restated on that order. As written it fires on equality, and under a stable coordinator a session's last full checkpoint and a later deadline-truncated partial carry the same generation, so the older full row would outrank every partial the change produces and the partial-reassembly path would be unreachable for every session that has ever completed a checkpoint under its current coordinator. The clause is inert today only because nothing writes full rows into the table (migration `0062` has no `partial` column). Its purpose is to discriminate staleness, so it becomes: a `partial = false` row wins over the selected partial only when it is **strictly newer** in the total order above, which preserves the CPS-006 guarantee that a late-committed older-generation write cannot beat a fenced newer writer and lets a partial finalised after the last full checkpoint win under a stable coordinator. The threshold arm keeps its NULL-baseline degenerate case verbatim (`threshold = 0` when `baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes IS NULL`, `spec/10:141`, `:155`).

**Change (restore).** `spec/10:155` step (1) currently reads:

```
(1) list objects under `partial_object_key_prefix` via `ListObjectsV2` and verify contiguity — every index `n` in `[0, chunk_count)` must be present; a gap ...
```

Replace the listing with derivation: the coordinator derives the chunk keys from the manifest's `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` and fetches them in ascending index order; a `GetObject` `NotFound` on any index in `[0, chunk_count)` is the contiguity failure and takes the existing non-terminal failure branch. `ListObjectsV2` is not used on the restore path. `blobstore.Store` (`pkg/blobstore/blobstore.go:306-321`) exposes only `Put`, `Get`, and `Stat`, and `spec/10:141` already states the keys are derivable from prefix plus index, so derivation removes an unneeded store capability rather than adding one.

**Change (the staging path, spec/10:155 step (4)).** Step (4) ("Atomicity of extraction failure") names the staging directory and the rename target as the literal `/workspace/current.partial` and `/workspace/current`. That path does not exist on a `maxConcurrentSessions > 1` pool, where the runtime "MUST NOT assume a global `/workspace/current` path" (`spec/06_warm-pod-model.md:392`, layout at `:373`), so the rule as written is unsatisfiable on per-slot pools and contradicts the C1 implementation. Restate step (4) in terms of the resolved workspace root: the decompress-then-untar pipeline writes into a staging directory that is a sibling of the resolved root (`/workspace/current.partial` on a single-session pool, `/workspace/slots/{slotId}/current.partial` on a concurrent pool) and the staging directory is atomically renamed onto that root only after the pipeline completes. The archive's `/sessions` tree stages alongside it and commits in the same rename step. A non-terminal failure deletes both staging trees in their entirety, mutates neither the workspace root nor `/sessions`, and falls back to the last successful full checkpoint. The atomicity guarantee and the `resumeMode: "partial_workspace"` outcome are unchanged.

**Change (the §12.5 GC backstop and the crash-semantics rule).** `ListObjectsV2` has three consumers in the spec today, and all three are replaced by the same derivation so that no surviving passage mandates a store capability `blobstore.Store` does not expose. The backstop's row-selection predicate is rewritten in the same edit, on the two axes the derivation exposes: a soft-deleted row is now the only name a chunk has, and an intent row is now written by every trigger including the seal, whose session is already terminal.

- `spec/12:328` (the GC backstop sweep) currently lists objects under `partial_object_key_prefix` via `ListObjectsV2` and relies on the listing being encoding-agnostic across `partial-{n}.tar` and `partial-{n}.tar.gz`. Rewrite the mechanism: for each swept row the GC job derives the chunk keys from `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding`, issues a per-key `ArtifactStore` soft-delete for every index in `[0, chunk_count)` (a soft-delete on a missing object is a no-op, so a reserved-but-uncommitted index costs nothing), and then soft-deletes the Postgres row under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard. Drop the encoding-agnostic-listing rationale; the manifest's `chunk_encoding` column is the authoritative suffix. The backstop's purpose and its idempotency argument are unchanged. Its row-selection predicate is rewritten on two axes, below.
- **The backstop's row-selection predicate is widened to soft-deleted rows inside the tombstone-retention window.** The shipped predicate is `partial = true AND deleted_at IS NULL` (`spec/12:328`, restated as GC concurrency rule 6 at `spec/12:349` and as the crash-semantics rule at `spec/10:133`). With prefix listing gone, a chunk is named only by its manifest row, so a chunk committed under a row that another writer has already soft-deleted is reachable from no active row. That state is producible: a generation-fenced supersede soft-deletes the victim's row and deletes its committed chunks, and the victim, which per `spec/10:165` still believes it holds the lease, can land the `PutObject` for an index whose counter `UPDATE` committed before the supersede. The §12.5 tombstone hard-prune is "a pure Postgres row removal with no storage-side side effect" (`spec/12:353`, and `hardPrunePartialManifests` at `cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go:1072` issues no object-store call), so once the row is hard-pruned the `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` needed to derive the key are gone and the chunk's bytes stay on the tenant's `storage_bytes_used` permanently. The selection predicate at `spec/12:328` and at `spec/12:349` therefore becomes `partial = true AND (deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now() - (gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds * interval '1 second'))`, and `spec/10:133`'s statement of the same predicate is reworded to match. `spec/10:141` carries a fourth statement of it, `spec/10:175` (BarrierAck rule 5, "cleaned up by the §12.5 GC backstop on the next cycle via the standard `partial = true AND deleted_at IS NULL` sweep") a fifth, and `spec/10:155` a sixth in the identical words ("Generation-stale partial manifests (those not selected by the `MAX(coordination_generation)` filter above) are cleaned up by the §12.5 GC backstop on the next cycle via the standard `partial = true AND deleted_at IS NULL` sweep; they are never consulted by the resume path"); all three are reworded in the same edit, because a spec carrying five widened statements of the predicate and one narrow one would send an implementer following the narrow one to build a sweep that cannot reach a chunk committed under a soft-deleted row. `spec/10:175`'s closing clause becomes "cleaned up by the §12.5 GC backstop on the next cycle, whose row-selection predicate is `partial = true AND (deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now() - gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds)` with the terminal-session arm's `checkpoint_timeout_at` liveness term"; the rest of rule 5, including the fallback to the last successful periodic checkpoint, is unchanged. `spec/10:155`'s sentence takes the same widened predicate, and its "not selected by the `MAX(coordination_generation)` filter" clause is restated on the total order `(coordination_generation DESC, checkpoint_started_at DESC, checkpoint_id DESC)` that S2 makes the selector, so the sentence names the rows the selector did not return rather than a filter the selector no longer applies. `spec/10:141`'s sentence "Cleanup predicates remain reason-agnostic — both the primary path and the backstop sweep key on `partial = true AND deleted_at IS NULL`" becomes "the primary cleanup path keys on `partial = true AND deleted_at IS NULL`; the §12.5 backstop sweep keys on `partial = true AND (deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now() - gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds)`, with the terminal-session arm additionally requiring `checkpoint_timeout_at < now() - gc.cycleIntervalSeconds`", keeping the reason-agnostic claim and the `deleted_at IS NULL` idempotency-guard sentence that follows it. The same paragraph records the column as the literal field `partial: true`, which the unification makes false, so the field entry is restated as the boolean `partial BOOLEAN NOT NULL` column the unified table carries (`true` on an intent row and on a truncated finalisation, `false` on a successful one). The two entry conditions (terminal session, expired resume window) are unchanged, the widened arm is bounded by the tombstone-retention window and therefore terminates (the hard-prune removes the row afterwards), and `deleted_at IS NULL` survives as the idempotency guard on the terminating soft-delete `UPDATE`: a row selected through the widened arm sees `rows_affected == 0` on that `UPDATE` and the pass is chunk-delete-only, which is idempotent because a soft-delete on an already-tombstoned or missing object is a no-op. Rule 6's stale-leader argument survives verbatim on that guard. C4 gives the store the selector this needs; `ListSoftDeletedBefore` is the hard-prune selector and returns the complement of the widened arm.
- **The backstop's terminal-session arm gains a liveness discriminator.** Entry condition (a) is "the session has reached a terminal state", and `Seal` (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/seal.go:91`, reached from `recordSessionCompleted` at `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/usage.go:395` through `Checkpointer.Seal` at `checkpointer.go:210`) runs *after* the session row is terminal. Once the intent-row write is the first durable act of every attempt, the seal's own `partial = true` row satisfies condition (a) from the moment it commits until its finalising `UPDATE`. The backstop is a leader-elected 15-minute GC goroutine that holds none of the upload driver's process-local single-flight lock, so a cycle landing inside the seal window would soft-delete the live row, delete the chunks committed so far, and leave the seal's finalising `UPDATE` to no-op under its own `deleted_at IS NULL` guard. The artifact destroyed is the one `spec/07:212` designates as the terminal session's `final_workspace_ref`, which derive, replay, and `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` resolve. The same race hits any periodic checkpoint still in flight when its session terminates. The terminal-session arm therefore adds `AND checkpoint_timeout_at < now() - (gc.cycleIntervalSeconds * interval '1 second')`, so a row whose attempt has not yet passed its own deadline, plus a margin, is never swept. `checkpoint_timeout_at` is populated on every intent row for exactly this reason (above). The margin covers the detached-context finalisation the driver runs after the deadline fires (S3, C5), and it reuses the existing `gc.cycleIntervalSeconds` knob (default 900s, `spec/12:329`) rather than adding one: 15 minutes exceeds the largest checkpoint deadline (the 90s Stage 2 top tier) by an order of magnitude, and a row still unfinalised a full GC cycle past its own deadline is abandoned by definition. The expired-resume-window arm (b) needs no such term: `maxResumeWindowSeconds` already exceeds every checkpoint deadline by orders of magnitude.
- `spec/10:133` (crash semantics) states that the backstop discovers the chunk objects "via `partial_object_key_prefix`". Reword to discovery via the row's `object_key_prefix` plus `chunk_count`, restate its sweep predicate as the widened one above, and add the reason the counter is reserved ahead of the `PutObject` (S1): the derived key set is a superset of the committed objects even when the gateway dies mid-upload.
- `spec/04:237` (the resume-time cleanup) states the gateway deletes "every chunk object listed under the manifest's `partial_object_key_prefix`". S4 already edits this paragraph; the discovery clause is edited with it to the derived key set.

**Change (artifact-store accounting, spec/12:321, spec/12:328, and spec/10:135).** Production hands the gateway the `cataloging` store (`cmd/lenny-gateway/stores.go:1085-1090`, handed to the binder at `:1842`), whose `Put` inserts one `artifact_store` row per object key at Put time (`pkg/blobstore/cataloging/cataloging.go:143-171`) and whose `SoftDelete` transitions that row to `soft_deleted` and releases the tenant's reserved bytes (`:240-258`). The accounting rule follows that implementation rather than fighting it:

- Every chunk object is one `artifact_store` row, inserted by the `ArtifactStore` as part of the chunk's `Put`, with `artifact_size_bytes` equal to the chunk's byte length. A checkpoint's catalogued footprint is the sum of its chunk rows, and no row is keyed on a bare prefix (a prefix is not a parseable `lenny-blob://` URI, and the `cataloging` GC sweeps skip rows whose URI fails `ParseURI` at `cataloging.go:386-391`, so a prefix-keyed row would be permanently un-GC-able).
- `spec/10:135`'s carve-out (partial-chunk bytes accounted through the manifest row rather than through `artifact_store`) is removed rather than scoped. Partial and full chunks are catalogued identically, so the `workspace_bytes_uploaded` → `chunk_count * chunk_size_bytes` accounting relationship the paragraph describes at delete time goes away, and the paragraph is rewritten to state the surviving invariant: the manifest row exists before the first chunk becomes uploadable, so no chunk object can exist without both a referencing manifest row and its own `artifact_store` row.
- `spec/12:321` gains checkpoint chunks explicitly in the "all MinIO-backed artifacts" list, with the note that a chunked checkpoint contributes `chunk_count` rows.
- The §12.5 checkpoint-storage sizing guidance counts one MinIO object per retained checkpoint, which the unification falsifies: a retained checkpoint is a set of `ceil(workspace_bytes / chunkSizeBytes)` independently committed objects, up to 32 at the 512 MiB `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` with the 16 MiB `chunkSizeBytes` default (`spec/17:919`). `spec/12:336`'s framing sentence ("The per-pod checkpoint-object count depends on the pool's execution mode") is restated so the object count depends on the workspace size and `chunkSizeBytes` as well as on the execution mode, and the two bullets it introduces are restated as counts of retained checkpoints rather than of objects: a pod carries up to `maxConcurrentSessions × 2` retained checkpoints, each stored as `chunk_count` objects, so its steady-state object count is `maxConcurrentSessions × 2 × chunk_count`. `spec/12:338`'s concrete figure ("A pod with `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` therefore carries up to 16 checkpoint objects steady-state") becomes 16 retained checkpoints and up to `16 × chunk_count` objects. `spec/12:325`, the normative latest-2 retention rule, carries a third statement of the same figure ("a pod with `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` may retain up to 16 checkpoint objects (8 slots × 2)") and is restated the same way in the same edit: the limit is per slot, so such a pod retains up to 16 checkpoints, each stored as `chunk_count` independently committed objects, for up to `16 × chunk_count` objects. Leaving it would put two different object counts for one pod configuration two bullets apart, and an operator sizing MinIO object counts and `storageQuotaBytes` reads the retention rule first. The byte-based per-tenant aggregate ceiling and the legal-hold accumulation bullet are unchanged, because both are already stated in bytes.
- Deleting a checkpoint, whether from the latest-2 rotation, from TTL expiry, from terminal-session cleanup, from the resume-time partial cleanup, or from the backstop, derives the chunk keys from the manifest row and issues a per-key `SoftDelete` for every index in `[0, chunk_count)` through the `ArtifactStore`, which tombstones the object, transitions each chunk's `artifact_store` row, and decrements the Redis counter by that row's `artifact_size_bytes`. GC concurrency rules 2 and 5 (`spec/12:345`, `:348`) are unchanged: the `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` guard still makes each chunk's delete and decrement fire at most once, now per chunk row rather than per checkpoint. GC concurrency rule 3 (`spec/12:346`) is restated on the verb this change mandates. It today rests the stale-leader convergence argument on the raw MinIO `DeleteObject` API, states the terminal state as "the object is absent in MinIO", and adds that "no additional coordination, conditional delete, or object tagging is required on the MinIO side", all three of which are false once the rotation and TTL delete is the `ArtifactStore` soft-delete: the object is tombstoned through a `PutObjectTagging` call (`pkg/blobstore/miniostore/miniostore.go:785-804`) and is not absent until the §12.5 tombstone hard-prune. The rule keeps its argument on the new verb: a soft-delete on a missing or already-tombstoned object is a no-op (`miniostore.go:788-793` returns nil on a missing object), so a stale leader that issued the soft-delete before losing the guard race, a new leader that re-issues it after crash recovery, and checkpoint rotation overlapping TTL expiration all converge on the same terminal state, which is a tombstoned object plus an `artifact_store` row soft-deleted with its bytes released exactly once under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard, with physical removal deferred to the tombstone hard-prune. The "no object tagging is required" clause is dropped, because the tombstone is a tag.
- **Every manifest row has a deletion path, including the `partial = false` rows the unification introduces.** In the same step that deletes a checkpoint's derived chunk keys, the deleting path soft-deletes its `checkpoint_manifest` row under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard. The retention bullets at `spec/12:325` (latest-2 rotation) and `spec/12:326` (delete all checkpoints when the session terminates and the resume window expires) are amended to say so, and the §12.5 tombstone hard-prune at `spec/12:353` is made reason-agnostic: its manifest predicate becomes `DELETE FROM checkpoint_manifest WHERE deleted_at IS NOT NULL AND deleted_at < now() - (gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds * interval '1 second')`, dropping the `partial = true` term. The same paragraph closes with a second, independent declaration of the `lenny_gc_tombstones_pruned_total` `table` label's value set ("Metric: `lenny_gc_tombstones_pruned_total` (counter, labeled by `table: artifact_store|partial_manifest`) tracks hard-prune disposition"), and it is edited with the predicate: the value set becomes `artifact_store | checkpoint_manifest`, matching the renamed table and the `spec/16:197` declaration S1 corrects and the Go registration C2 renames (`gatewaymetrics_retention.go:67-73`). Leaving it would have the applied spec declare one counter's label values two different ways and name a table that no longer exists. Without both edits the unification creates a row class nothing retires: a `partial = false` row is not soft-deleted by the read-only restore, and the shipped hard-prune predicate (`partial = true AND deleted_at IS NOT NULL AND …`) structurally excludes it, so every rotated-out and TTL-expired checkpoint would leave a live row naming chunk keys whose objects were already deleted, and every prefix reader (restore, derive, replay, `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace`) resolving such a row would take the contiguity-failure branch instead of finding no artifact. The Go side already keys only on `deleted_at` (`pkg/gateway/checkpoint/partialmanifeststore/pgstore/pgstore.go:216-218`), so the code follows the corrected predicate rather than the current one.
- `spec/12:328` (the partial-manifest retention bullet) is reworded so "partial checkpoint manifest records" reads as `partial = true` rows of `checkpoint_manifest`.

**Change (the delete verb, spec/10:137, spec/10:139, spec/10:157, and spec/04:237).** Four retained sentences specify the chunk delete as a hard bucket delete or the chunk discovery as a prefix listing. `spec/10:137` has the supersede transaction issue "MinIO `DeleteObjects`". `spec/10:157` and `spec/04:237` both state that "MinIO chunk deletion remains a hard-delete batch". `spec/10:139` closes its design rationale with "(b) reassembly is a deterministic concat of keys listed by prefix rather than a multipart listing under a (possibly expired) `UploadId`, and (c) orphan cleanup uses plain `DeleteObject` per key with no dependency on S3 multipart lifecycle rules", which mandates both the raw delete the staged `spec/04:249` text declares "not permitted" and the prefix listing §5 makes a non-goal. Clause (b) becomes "reassembly is a deterministic concat of keys derived from `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` rather than a multipart listing under a (possibly expired) `UploadId`", and clause (c) becomes "orphan cleanup issues a per-key `ArtifactStore` soft-delete over the derived key set with no dependency on S3 multipart lifecycle rules". A hard bucket delete removes the object and touches neither the chunk's `artifact_store` row nor the Redis counter (the only per-key hard delete in `blobstore` is `Tombstoner.HardDeleteObject`, `pkg/blobstore/blobstore.go:381`), so every aborted, superseded, rotated, and TTL-expired checkpoint would leave its bytes on the tenant's `storage_bytes_used` forever, which is the leak the accounting rule above exists to prevent. All four sentences are replaced with the same `ArtifactStore` soft-delete: `cataloging.Store.SoftDelete(u, retention)` (`pkg/blobstore/cataloging/cataloging.go:240-268`) tombstones the object through the inner backend (`miniostore.Store.SoftDelete`, `pkg/blobstore/miniostore/miniostore.go:785`), transitions the chunk's `artifact_store` row, and releases its `artifact_size_bytes`, and the §12.5 tombstone hard-prune physically removes both. The idempotency argument those sentences rest on survives verbatim against the new verb: `miniostore.SoftDelete` returns nil on a missing object (`miniostore.go:788-793`), so a repeated delete and a reserved-but-uncommitted index are both no-ops. This is the single delete verb on the checkpoint path, and C2 implements it.

**Change (the resume-time Cleanup rule is scoped to partial rows, spec/10:157).** The §10.1 Cleanup paragraph opens "Regardless of reassembly outcome, the coordinator deletes every chunk under `partial_object_key_prefix` … then **soft-deletes** the Postgres manifest row". Today the column name is the scope: `partial_object_key_prefix` exists only on partial rows. The rename makes the column `object_key_prefix`, which the unified table carries on every row, and S2 and C2 route full-checkpoint restores through the same reassembly pipeline and the same `SelectForResume` selector, so the sentence as renamed would mandate deleting a full checkpoint's chunks and soft-deleting its row after every restore. That is the behavior C2 forbids (`partial = false` rows are read-only on restore) and the behavior S4's parallel edit to `spec/04:237` forbids, and an implementer following §10.1 would erase the session's full checkpoint, including the `spec/10:155` fallback target, on its first restore. The opening sentence is therefore scoped in the same edit: regardless of reassembly outcome, the coordinator deletes every chunk of a `partial = true` row and soft-deletes that row. A `partial = false` row is read-only on restore; its chunks and its row are retired by the latest-2 rotation, the TTL GC, and terminal-session cleanup, each of which deletes the derived chunk keys and soft-deletes the row in the same step.

**Change (spec/04:235).** Restate checkpoint atomicity as a state transition: no manifest row is ever `partial = false` until the adapter has declared the archive complete with its terminal `CheckpointSummary` frame and every byte it declared has committed. Keep the sentence that a partial manifest "is not a valid checkpoint; it is a recovery aid", and update the chunk-object naming reference to `chunk-{n}.{chunk_encoding}`. The session-file snapshot travels inside the same archive as the workspace (`pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:311-323`, `:352`), so the "either snapshot fails" clause is restated as a single archive stream that either completes or truncates.

**Change (the object-key grammar is unchanged, spec/12:307 and docs/operator-guide/multi-tenancy.md:279-284).** `spec/12:307` fixes the canonical key format at `/{tenant_id}/{object_type}/{session_id}/{filename}`, and the flat `{checkpoint_id}-chunk-{n}.{chunk_encoding}` `PartID` this change adopts keeps every chunk inside that four-segment grammar, so `:307` needs no edit and none is staged. The §5 non-goal records why a five-segment `…/{checkpoint_id}/{chunk}` key is rejected: `blobstore.URI.String()` percent-escapes a slashed `PartID` and `ParseURI` then fails on it, which would make every chunk row permanently un-GC-able through the `cataloging` sweeps.

`docs/operator-guide/multi-tenancy.md:279-284` is the one published statement of the concrete checkpoint key, and it is a single-object key:

```
/{tenant_id}/workspace/{session_id}/snapshot.tar
/{tenant_id}/checkpoint/{session_id}/gen-{n}.tar
/{tenant_id}/eviction/{session_id}/context
```

Replace the checkpoint line with the chunk key this change defines, `/{tenant_id}/checkpoint/{session_id}/{checkpoint_id}-chunk-{n}.{chunk_encoding}`, and state that one checkpoint is a set of `chunk_count` such objects sharing an `object_key_prefix`. The `workspace` and `eviction` lines are unchanged. The page uses the singular object type `checkpoint` while `spec/10:130` and `:139` write the plural `checkpoints` in the partial-chunk prefix; the singular is correct, because it is the value the `blobstore` object-type enum carries (`pkg/blobstore/blobstore.go:151-153`) and the value every other object type in the table uses. S2's `object_key_prefix` definition already writes the singular, and `spec/10:130` and `:139` are corrected to it in the same edit.

**Change (a retained checkpoint is a chunk set everywhere it is counted).** The object-count restatements at `spec/12:325`, `:336`, and `:338` are above. Four further sites count checkpoints as objects and are corrected with them, because a MinIO operator sizing a cluster reads whichever one they reach first.

- `spec/12:337` ("up to 2 retained checkpoints per session × concurrent sessions assigned to the pod") already counts checkpoints rather than objects and is unchanged; it is listed because the bullets around it are not.
- `spec/12:339` (the per-tenant aggregate ceiling, `Σ_pools (active_pods_for_tenant × per_pod_checkpoint_count) × average_checkpoint_size`) is a byte formula and stays correct, because `average_checkpoint_size` is the whole checkpoint's byte total whatever its chunk count. Restate `per_pod_checkpoint_count` as a count of retained checkpoints rather than of objects, and add the object-count corollary an operator sizing MinIO's object namespace needs: the object count is that figure multiplied by `ceil(average_checkpoint_size / chunkSizeBytes)`.
- `spec/12:340` ("A session held for 24 hours can therefore retain ~144 checkpoints instead of 2") is a checkpoint count and stays correct in checkpoints. Add the object corollary in the same terms: at the 512 MiB `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` and the 16 MiB `chunkSizeBytes` default those 144 checkpoints are up to 4,608 objects. The `LegalHoldCheckpointAccumulationProjectedBreach` alert projects bytes and is unchanged.
- `spec/17:900` (the §17.8 quick-reference row "Checkpoint retention | Latest 2 per session") and `spec/19_resolved-decisions.md:7` (resolved decision 1, "Full snapshots with size cap. Keep latest 2 per session. Incrementals deferred.") both frame a checkpoint as one object. Both keep their rule and restate the unit: the latest 2 **checkpoints** are retained per session per slot, and a checkpoint is a set of independently committed chunk objects addressed through its manifest row. The resolved decision itself is not reopened: a chunked checkpoint is still a full snapshot with a size cap, and incrementals remain deferred (§5).

**Change (the legal-hold reconciler's gap detector, spec/12:766 and :768).** `spec/12:768` has the reconciler scan for sessions where `legal_hold = true` and "one or more checkpoints have already been rotated (i.e., there are gaps in the checkpoint sequence recorded in the `artifact_store` table)". That detector presumes one `artifact_store` row per checkpoint, so under the chunked model it reads a sequence of chunk rows and reports a gap for every checkpoint whose chunk set is mid-delete or partially swept, which is a false positive on every rotation. Restate the detector on the `checkpoint_manifest` table, which is the table that knows what a checkpoint is: the reconciler scans for held sessions whose retained `checkpoint_manifest` rows show a rotated-out checkpoint (a soft-deleted `partial = false` row inside the hold window), which is the state the hold was supposed to prevent. `spec/12:766`'s exemption rule (the GC job skips both TTL deletion and the latest-2 rotation for a held session) is unchanged, and C2 already honours it (`checkpointer.go:328-330`).

**Change (GC convergence over a set, spec/12:330, :331, :346, :348, :351).** The §12.5 GC concurrency proof is written over one object per checkpoint, and the convergence claim at `:351` ("a convergent, idempotent, single-writer system") rests on it. The proof survives per chunk rather than per checkpoint, and the passages say so.

- `spec/12:345` and `:348` (GC concurrency rules 2 and 5) keep the `WHERE id = $1 AND deleted_at IS NULL` guard, which now fires once per chunk row rather than once per checkpoint. The guard's purpose is to make each object's delete and each row's quota decrement happen at most once, and it still does. Restate `:348`'s "the MinIO delete" as the per-key soft-delete over the checkpoint's derived key set, and state the property that replaces per-checkpoint atomicity: a checkpoint's deletion is not one transaction, and a delete pass interrupted after `k` of `chunk_count` chunks leaves a partially deleted chunk set that the next pass completes, because every remaining key's soft-delete is a no-op or a delete and the manifest row is soft-deleted only in the same step. A half-deleted chunk set is never a restore source, because the restore path resolves the manifest row first and a soft-deleted row is not selectable (`SelectForResume`, C4).
- `spec/12:346` (rule 3) is restated on the `ArtifactStore` soft-delete, above. Its terminal state ("the object is absent in MinIO") becomes the tombstoned object plus the released `artifact_store` row, per chunk.
- `spec/12:330` ("The GC job is idempotent: it queries Postgres for artifacts past their TTL, deletes them from MinIO, marks the rows as deleted, and only then decrements the Redis storage quota counter") keeps its ordering and its idempotency; the row it names is the chunk's `artifact_store` row and the decrement is that row's `artifact_size_bytes`, which is already what `cataloging.Store.SoftDelete` does (`cataloging.go:240-268`). Restate the granularity in one clause.
- `spec/12:331` ("No reference counting required in v1: each MinIO artifact object is owned by exactly one session") stays true and stays load-bearing: every chunk object is owned by exactly one session, and derive copies each of the parent's chunks into a new session-scoped object rather than sharing one (P1). The sentence is restated per chunk object in the same edit P1 makes.
- `spec/12:351`'s convergence claim is retained, restated over the chunk set: each chunk's delete and each row's decrement are individually convergent and idempotent under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard, and the manifest row's soft-delete is the same, so the whole path converges.
- `spec/12:832` (the §12.8 GDPR `DeleteByUser` step 7, "delete workspace snapshots, checkpoints, uploaded files, session transcripts, and eviction context objects from MinIO for all sessions owned by the user") is prefix-scoped per session and already enumerates objects, so the chunked model changes nothing about it. C4 preserves the `DeleteByUser` and `DeleteByTenant` contract on the manifest table. The site is listed to record that it was checked.
- `spec/12:334` (the configurable 7-day artifact TTL) and `spec/12:326` (delete all checkpoints when the session terminates and the resume window expires) keep their rules; `:326` is amended by S2 above to soft-delete the manifest row alongside the chunks.
- `spec/12:353` (the tombstone hard-prune) is amended above; its per-checkpoint row volume rises to `chunk_count` `artifact_store` rows plus one `checkpoint_manifest` row, which the paragraph states so an operator sizing the prune's batch does not size it per checkpoint.

**Change (bucket versioning and the delete-marker lifecycle, spec/12:289, :292, spec/17:529, :1455, :1461, and the chart job).** `spec/12:289` enables bucket versioning "for checkpoint objects to prevent accidental overwrites", restated at `spec/17:1455` and `spec/17:1461`, and `spec/12:292` sizes the delete-marker lifecycle (a 24-hour expired-object-delete-marker expiry plus a 1-day noncurrent-version expiration, rendered by `charts/lenny/templates/minio-bucket-lifecycle-job.yaml:8-10`, `:117-125`, and preflight-checked at `spec/17:529`) against one delete marker per checkpoint deletion. Under the chunked model one checkpoint deletion issues `chunk_count` deletes and therefore produces up to `chunk_count` delete markers, up to 32 at the 512 MiB `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` with the 16 MiB `chunkSizeBytes` default. The versioning rationale is unchanged and gains a second reason that this change makes concrete: chunk keys are derived and a retry of one chunk index re-`Put`s the same key (C2), so versioning is what keeps a superseded body recoverable. The lifecycle rules keep their durations, and `spec/12:292` states the new marker volume explicitly so an operator reading it knows the sweep's object count scales with `chunkSizeBytes`. The rule that motivates the expiry, delete-marker accumulation degrading `ListObjects`, is restated on the operations that still list: the checkpoint path performs no `ListObjectsV2` at all after this change (every chunk key is derived from the manifest row, §5), so `ListObjects` degradation returns to a background concern for the other object types rather than sitting on the resume hot path. No lifecycle duration, no chart value, and no preflight check changes; the paragraph's arithmetic does.

**Change (MinIO capacity is a request-rate budget and a gateway relay budget, spec/17:1208-1212, :1216, :1221-1225, spec/16:607, docs/reference/configuration.md:337).** The §17 capacity tables size MinIO on checkpoint **bandwidth** and a checkpoint **write rate** (`~0.2/s`, `~1.7/s`, `~17/s` by tier), both of which assume one `PutObject` per checkpoint issued by the pod. Two figures change and one does not.

- The byte figures are unchanged. "Estimated checkpoint upload bandwidth (max 512 MB workspace)" and "Minimum MinIO aggregate throughput (burst, max workspace)" measure bytes, and a chunked checkpoint moves the same bytes.
- The request rate is new. At Tier 3 the ~17 checkpoints/s becomes up to `17 × ceil(workspaceSizeLimitBytes / chunkSizeBytes)` `PutObject` calls per second, which is ~544 PUT/s at the 512 MiB and 16 MiB defaults, and `17 × ceil(100 MB / 16 MiB) = 17 × 6 = ~102 PUT/s` at the 100 MB `capacityPlanning.avgWorkspaceSizeMB` default (`spec/16:607`, `docs/reference/configuration.md:337`). Add a "Minimum MinIO aggregate request rate (checkpoint `PutObject`)" row to the tier table at `spec/17:1208-1212` carrying `checkpoint_write_rate × ceil(avgWorkspaceSizeMB / chunkSizeBytes)`, evaluated per tier from the checkpoint write rates the table already publishes (`~0.2/s`, `~1.7/s`, `~17/s` at `spec/17:1208`): ~1.2 PUT/s at Tier 1, ~10 PUT/s at Tier 2, and ~102 PUT/s at Tier 3. State the worst case at `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` (32 objects per checkpoint, so ~544 PUT/s at Tier 3) in the same row. The `:1216` derivation of the ~17/s checkpoint rate itself (`10,000 / 600`) is unchanged.
- The throughput inequality at `:1221-1225` (`replicas × maxSessionsPerReplica × avg_workspace_bytes / periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds ≤ minio_aggregate_write_throughput × 0.7`) assumed the fleet's pods write to MinIO in parallel. Every byte now flows through a gateway replica, so the same aggregate must also fit the gateway's own egress: add the per-replica corollary `(replicas × maxSessionsPerReplica × avg_workspace_bytes / periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds) / replicas ≤ per_replica_checkpoint_relay_budget`, which at the Tier 3 figures the section already uses (`30 × 400 × 100 MB / 600s = 2.0 GB/s`) is ~67 MB/s sustained per replica. State the drain burst separately, because it is not an average: `spec/10:167` puts every coordinated session's flush on the one draining replica under a single `checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` deadline, so the burst is bounded by `maxSessionsPerReplica × avg_workspace_bytes / checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds`, and the §17.8.2 gateway sizing rows gain a checkpoint-relay line item carrying both figures. This is the capacity consequence of gateway-mediated I/O and it belongs in the spec rather than being discovered in production.
- **A byte budget is not sufficient on its own, because the periodic producer is a serial loop and the sweep has to finish inside its own interval.** `Checkpointer.Sweep` (`pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointer/checkpointer.go:173-178`) iterates every bound session in one goroutine and calls `Checkpoint` synchronously, and `Run` (`:122-133`) fires one `Sweep` per tick. Under this change each iteration archives, relays, slices, and `PutObject`s a whole workspace through the gateway before the next session begins, which the loop can do today only because `Checkpoint` returns `codes.Unimplemented`. The binding constraint is therefore a completion inequality rather than a rate: `maxSessionsPerReplica × checkpoint_duration ≤ periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds × checkpointWorker.concurrency`. At Tier 3 with the §4.4 SLO ceiling (`spec/04:253`, P95 < 2s at ≤ 100 MB) a serialized sweep of 400 sessions needs `400 × 2s = 800s` against a 600s interval, so every session on the replica would carry a last-checkpoint age above `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`, `lenny_checkpoint_stale_sessions` would sit at 400 per replica, and `CheckpointStale` (`spec/16:459`) would fire permanently at the top tier. Add the inequality to `spec/17:1221-1225` beside the relay corollary, and make the sweep a bounded worker pool sized by `gateway.checkpointWorker.concurrency` (C2) so the knob governs it.
- `spec/12:305` reads "Workspace checkpoints (up to 500MB) cause TOAST overhead" while `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` is 512 MiB everywhere else. Correct the figure to 512 MiB in the same pass.

**Change (the chunk-size and recovery-threshold knobs get a real configuration surface).** `partialChunkSizeBytes` (16 MiB) and `gateway.partialRecoveryThresholdFraction` (0.5) are declared in the `spec/17_deployment-topology.md:919-920` quick-reference table. Neither has a key in `charts/lenny/values.yaml`, an entry in `charts/lenny/values.schema.json`, a CRD field, or a gateway flag, so both are unreachable by a deployer today. `gateway.partialRecoveryThresholdFraction` additionally has a `docs/reference/configuration.md:31` row whose definition matches neither `spec/17:920` nor the §10.1 threshold this change makes live: it describes a fraction of "expected manifest entries" and a degraded mode that emits `lenny_gateway_partial_recovery_below_threshold_total`, a metric that appears nowhere in `spec/`, `pkg/`, or `docs/`. That row is rewritten rather than duplicated, on the byte-fraction threshold the restore driver and `SelectForResume` implement (`workspace_bytes_uploaded >= baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes * partialRecoveryThresholdFraction`), and the phantom metric reference is dropped. The page gains rows for `gateway.chunkSizeBytes` and `gateway.checkpointWorker.concurrency`, which have no row today. S1 renames the first to `chunkSizeBytes` because it now governs every checkpoint. Both gain the configuration surface every other gateway knob has: `gateway.chunkSizeBytes` (default `16777216`) and `gateway.partialRecoveryThresholdFraction` (default `0.5`) in `charts/lenny/values.yaml` with their `charts/lenny/values.schema.json` entries, rendered onto the gateway container as `--checkpoint-chunk-size-bytes` and `--partial-recovery-threshold-fraction` in `charts/lenny/templates/gateway-deployment.yaml` alongside the existing `--checkpoint-interval` and `--checkpoint-jitter-fraction` flags (`:599-604`), and read by `cmd/lenny-gateway` into the C2 upload driver and the C4 `SelectForResume` selector. A third key, `gateway.checkpointWorker.concurrency`, bounds the number of concurrent checkpoint uploads one gateway replica drives. It is the knob the relay budget and the sweep-completion inequality above make necessary and the one `docs/runbooks/checkpoint-stale.md:116` already tells operators to reach for, and C2 builds the bounded worker pool it governs: the same semaphore is the single bound shared by the periodic sweep, the seal, the barrier flush, and the eviction producers, so no producer can exceed it by reaching the driver through a different call path. Its default is derived from the inequality rather than chosen: at the Tier 3 figures (`maxSessionsPerReplica: 400`, `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds: 600`) and the §4.4 SLO ceiling of 2s per checkpoint (`spec/04:253`), `400 × 2 / 600 = 1.34`, so concurrency 2 clears the sweep at the SLO, and the default of `4` carries headroom for the 5–10s the same SLO paragraph allows a 512 MB workspace. A deployer whose workspaces sit near `workspaceSizeLimitBytes` raises it against the same inequality, which is what the `spec/17:919-920` row and the `docs/reference/configuration.md` row both state. `spec/17:919-920` gains the two renamed rows and the new concurrency row.

**Change (the two getting-started pages that publish the superseded checkpoint model).** Both pages state the model this change inverts, and both are read before any spec page.

- `docs/getting-started/architecture.md:210` is the published mirror of the `spec/04:235` atomicity sentence: "The metadata record is written only after both artifacts are successfully uploaded. If either upload fails, the entire checkpoint is discarded." After this change the manifest row is the first durable act of every attempt and a truncated attempt deliberately retains its committed chunks under a `partial = true` row, so the page would state the opposite of the applied spec on both counts. Restate the paragraph as the state transition `spec/04:235` now defines: the manifest row is committed before the first chunk `PutObject`, a row becomes `partial = false` only when the terminal `CheckpointSummary` arrived and every declared byte committed, and a truncated attempt finalises `partial = true` and keeps its committed chunks for the resume path. The bullets at `:204-206`, which describe a checkpoint as two separate MinIO artifacts plus a metadata record, are restated as one archive (the workspace root plus `/sessions`) stored as a set of chunk objects under one `object_key_prefix`.
- `docs/getting-started/concepts.md:434-440` is a second published statement of the same model ("A checkpoint is an atomic unit comprising: a workspace snapshot … a session file snapshot … If either snapshot fails, the entire checkpoint is discarded; partial checkpoints are never stored."). After this change a partial checkpoint is stored routinely and is the source of the `partial_workspace` resume the same page's `:98` edit introduces, so the page would contradict both the applied spec and its own edited line. Restate the checkpoint as one archive committed as a chunk set named by a `checkpoint_manifest` row, and replace the "never stored" sentence with the two outcomes the unified path produces: a complete chunk set resumes `full`, and a truncated one above the §10.1 recovery threshold resumes `partial_workspace`.

**Change (the resume-mode enum is restated over chunk contiguity, spec/07:138 and its five doc mirrors).** `spec/07:138` defines `resumeMode` as `full` ("full workspace restored from latest checkpoint"), `conversation_only`, `partial_workspace` ("workspace partially reconstructed from a partial checkpoint manifest"), and `coordinator_handoff`, with `workspaceRecoveryFraction` present on the `partial_workspace` arm. Under the unified model `full` and `partial_workspace` stop being two mechanisms and become two outcomes of one: a complete chunk set versus a truncated one. The enum keeps all four values and their client-visible meaning; the definitions are restated so `full` is a restore from a checkpoint whose chunk set is complete (`partial = false`), `partial_workspace` is a restore from a truncated chunk set above the §10.1 recovery threshold, and `workspaceRecoveryFraction` is meaningful on the ordinary path rather than on an exceptional one, because any trigger's stream can truncate.

The `full` definition also gains the arm the unedited text omits: a session for which `SelectForResume` returns `ErrNotFound`, meaning **no manifest row at all**, takes the §7.3 snapshotless rebuild, materializes an empty workspace from its stored `WorkspacePlan`, and resumes `full` with `workspaceLost: false`. A session that holds manifest rows whose chunk objects are absent is a different state and does not reach this arm: it fails the resume with `WORKSPACE_SNAPSHOT_MISSING` per `spec/25:4095` (C2). That is what the shipped classifier does (`classifyResume` falls through to `ResumeFull`, `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:3227`, on the path `start.go:3089` labels "§7.3 snapshotless resume-rebuild"), it is what C6 and C7 rely on (the eviction-state row wins only when the resume restored nothing, which is exactly this arm), and it is what the `docs/operator-guide/disaster-recovery.md:271-273` edit below states. Defining `full` **only** as a restore from a complete chunk set would leave the applied spec with no arm for a session that restores nothing and would contradict the docs page the same change rewrites, so both are worded from one definition. `spec/10:155`'s fraction formula and `spec/10:130`'s frozen-denominator rule are unchanged, and the freeze is what keeps the fraction well-defined when the prior full checkpoint is itself a chunk set that a later rotation may have deleted: the denominator is the byte count recorded on the intent row rather than a re-read of the parent checkpoint. `spec/10:392` (the `coordinator_handoff` fraction sourced from `session_checkpoint_meta`) is unchanged, and C6 gives it its producer: the restore driver's fraction is written to that column on every restore, so the handoff frame carries a `partial_workspace` recovery forward verbatim instead of the hard-coded `1.0` the shipped frame publishes (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:3264-3269`).

Four published pages already understate the enum and are corrected in the same edit, because after this change a `partial_workspace` resume is a routine outcome that a client must be able to read about:

- `docs/client-guide/wire-format.md:183` lists `session.resumed` with `{ "resumeMode", "workspaceLost" }` and omits `workspaceRecoveryFraction`. Add it, as optional and present on `partial_workspace`.
- `docs/client-guide/streaming.md:64` describes `resumeMode` as "`full` or `conversation_only`". Add `partial_workspace` and `coordinator_handoff`, and add `workspaceRecoveryFraction` on the same terms as the wire-format edit.
- `docs/runtime-author-guide/lifecycle.md:230-236` says "Common values are `full` … and `conversation_only`" and defines `workspaceLost` as "true if the workspace snapshot was unavailable or corrupt". State the full value set, define `workspaceLost` as true when no workspace was materialized (which is the `conversation_only` arm), and add `workspaceRecoveryFraction` to the field list and to the JSON example.
- `docs/getting-started/concepts.md:98` describes `resumeMode` as "either `full` … or `conversation_only`". State the full value set and name `workspaceRecoveryFraction`.
- `docs/operator-guide/disaster-recovery.md:271-273` states that "if no checkpoint exists, the session resumes with `resumeMode: "conversation_only"` and `workspaceLost: true`", which is wrong in the direction this change makes common: a session with no checkpoint at all takes the §7.3 snapshotless rebuild and resumes `full` with `workspaceLost: false` and an empty workspace, which is what the shipped classifier already does (`classifyResume`'s fall-through returns `ResumeFull`, `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:3227`), while `conversation_only` is the arm reached from a `session_eviction_state` minimal-state row. Correct it in the terms the `spec/07:138` restatement above defines for the same arm.

`workspaceRecoveryFraction` appears in no file under `docs/` today, and after this change it is a routine client-visible field on `session.resumed` rather than an exceptional one, so all four client-facing pages carry it: it is an optional number in `[0.0, 1.0]`, present on `partial_workspace` (and carried forward on `coordinator_handoff` per `spec/07:138`, from the `session_checkpoint_meta` row C6 writes on every restore), and omitted when the restored row's `baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes` is NULL. The §6 tier-11 assertion requires the field on every page it names, so an edit that reached only `wire-format.md` would fail the proposal's own gate.

**Change (STORAGE_QUOTA_EXCEEDED mid-checkpoint, spec/15:1026).** The error row already covers "the upload or checkpoint write", and S2's hard stream cap makes a mid-checkpoint rejection a real outcome for the first time. Add the resulting state to the row's description: an attempt rejected on the reservation writes no chunk, and an attempt cut by the stream cap after committing chunks takes the abort sweep, so the tenant's quota counter is unchanged in both cases. The code, its class, its 429 status, and its `details.currentBytes` / `details.limitBytes` fields are unchanged.

**Change (the build sequence and the test matrix).** `spec/18_build-sequence.md:398-415` (Phase 8) names the checkpoint deliverables and its exit criteria. The `pkg/checkpoint` `Trigger` enum (`periodic`, `pre_scale_down`, `eviction`) is unchanged: the agent-pod eviction trigger S3 ships maps onto the existing `eviction` value rather than adding one, which is what keeps the `trigger` metric label's value set closed. The phase description gains the gateway-side upload and restore drivers, the unified `checkpoint_manifest` table, and the `NotifyPodTerminating` signal, and its exit criteria gain the tier-5 node-drain journey §6 names. `TESTING.md:900` (the `ArtifactStore` domain-suite row), `:1025` (`checkpoint_resume`), `:1066` (`pod_lifecycle`), `:1074` (`sandbox_finalizer`), `:1182` (`checkpoint_duration`), and `:1231` (the MinIO-outage core scenario) keep their scenarios and are restated on the gateway as the uploader and on the chunk set as the artifact; `TESTING.md:1067` (`node_drain`) is restated by S3.

**Note for the implementer.** `blobstore.Store.Put` returns `ErrConflict` on overwrite (`pkg/blobstore/blobstore.go:307-312`). A retried checkpoint *attempt* therefore mints a fresh `checkpoint_id` and a fresh prefix under the supersede-on-write rule rather than re-`Put`ting `chunk-00000` under the old one. A retry of one chunk *within* an attempt does re-`Put` the same key, and C2 makes that case idempotent: an `ErrConflict` whose `Stat` reports the byte length the driver is writing is treated as success, and only a length mismatch is an error.

### S3. Reassign the eviction-checkpoint trigger to the gateway, ship the agent-pod eviction trigger, and define the success finalisation UPDATE

**Target:** `spec/04_system-components.md` §4.6 (`:489`, `:491`, `:493`, `:625`) and §4.4 (`:264`, `:266`, `:278`, `:282`, `:284`, `:286`, `:287`); `spec/05_runtime-registry-and-pool-model.md` §5.2 (`:542`, the CRD budget formula); `pkg/controller/poolscaling/poolstoresource.go` (`:65-96` `toConfig`, which mirrors the poolstore row onto `SandboxTemplateSpec` and gains the `TerminationGracePeriodSeconds` mirror; `:89-90` and `:145-159` `sessionPolicyToCRD`, the only renderer of `lennyv1.SessionPolicy`, whose recycle and scrub nil guards keep an ordinary non-recycling pool's `sessionPolicy` off the CRD entirely) with `pkg/gateway/runtime/poolstore`, `pkg/gateway/externalapi/admin/pools.go` (`PoolPayload` `:91-92` and `UpdatePoolRequest` `:237-238`, the §15.1 pool CRUD payloads, which are the deployer's only route to the column), `migrations/` (the `termination_grace_period_seconds` column and its `poolstore.Pool` field, without which the grace period the floor is compared against has no writer), and `tests/tier2_component/migrations/prod_columns_test.go` (the migration-contract row the new column needs, which `scripts/lint-migrations.sh:87-88` requires for every migration number); `pkg/controller/poolscaling/poolstoresource_test.go` (`:263`, `TestPoolStoreSourceLeavesSessionPolicyNilWithoutScrubControl`, which pins the nil render the mirror falsifies); `docs/getting-started/architecture.md` (`:238`); **Anchor and change (spec/04:489, §4.6.1 voluntary-disruption protection).** The sentence currently reads:

```
**Disruption protection for agent pods:** The primary protection against voluntary disruption (node drains, cluster upgrades) is a **preStop hook** on every agent pod that triggers a checkpoint via the runtime adapter's `Checkpoint` RPC before allowing termination.
```

Replace with:

```
**Disruption protection for agent pods:** The primary protection against voluntary disruption (node drains, the kubelet Eviction API, direct pod deletion, and cluster upgrades) is a **preStop hook** on every container of every agent pod. The hooks hold the pod open until the gateway has finished checkpointing every session bound to it. The gateway owns the checkpoint end to end: it mints the `checkpoint_id`, drives the `Checkpoint` stream against the terminating pod's adapter, and writes every chunk through the ArtifactStore ([§10.1](10_gateway-internals.md#101-horizontal-scaling) intent-row-first ordering). The agent pod issues no object-store call, holds no object-store credential, and mints no `checkpoint_id`.

The signal is the adapter's `GatewayControl.NotifyPodTerminating` RPC ([§4.7](#47-runtime-adapter)), sent on the adapter→gateway channel [§13.1](13_security-model.md#131-threat-model) already permits, as the adapter's first act on SIGTERM and before any part of the [§6.1](06_warm-pod-model.md#61-warm-pool-mechanics) teardown sequence. It carries the pod name and the remaining grace budget, and the adapter bounds its own wait so that at least `LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s` of the grace period remains for the demote-and-exit steps that follow. The gateway resolves the sessions bound to the pod from the session store rather than from the request, binds the terminating pod's adapter for every one of them that it is not already bound to, assumes coordination of every one of them through the [§10.1](10_gateway-internals.md#101-horizontal-scaling) coordinator-handoff protocol (taking the coordination lease when it does not already hold it, and advancing `coordination_generation` and fencing the pod in every case, because the replica that drives a session's checkpoints is the one holding its pod binding rather than the one holding its lease), and **reserves each slot's checkpoint in the same transaction that advances `coordination_generation`**, minting its `checkpoint_id` and committing its intent row before it drives any slot. The reservation is what excludes a displaced replica's later attempt: `coordination_generation` is a property of the session row, so a replica that re-reads it presents the advanced value and is admitted by the pod's fence, and only the committed intent row makes its supersede abort. The gateway then takes an **eviction-trigger** checkpoint for each reserved slot, serialized across slots in slot-ID order ([§5.2](05_runtime-registry-and-pool-model.md#52-pool-model) checkpoint granularity), bounded per slot by the [§10.1](10_gateway-internals.md#101-horizontal-scaling) Stage 2 tier cap, and bounded as a whole by the declared budget, which is the `checkpoint_timeout_at` the reserved rows carry. It also removes its own registry binding for the terminating pod for every session, since the pod is destroyed and no other path retires such a binding. The handoff is required rather than opportunistic: the adapter dials the gateway Service, which routes the RPC to an arbitrary replica, and only the coordinating replica may drive a session's `Checkpoint` stream past the pod's generation fence. A pod with no bound session gets an immediate return. The gateway returns when every slot has settled or the declared budget elapses.

The blocking chain is what holds the pod open. The kubelet does not SIGTERM a container until that container's preStop hook returns; neither hook returns until the adapter process exits; the adapter does not begin its teardown until `NotifyPodTerminating` returns or its own deadline fires; and the gateway does not return until every slot has settled or the budget elapses. The two hooks of a sidecar pod are different and only one of them signals. The `adapter` container's hook signals the adapter and waits for it to exit. The `runtime` container's hook is **wait-only**: it blocks until the adapter's listener stops accepting and signals nothing, so the runtime process stays alive to answer the `checkpoint_request` / `checkpoint_ready` quiesce handshake ([§4.7](#47-runtime-adapter)) that a Full-level checkpoint requires. A hook that signalled the runtime container's own PID 1 would terminate the runtime it exists to keep alive, and a hook that assumed a Lenny binary in the image would fail on the third-party runtime images the sidecar model is for ([§4.7](#47-runtime-adapter)); the hook therefore runs a copy of the adapter binary staged into a pod-local volume and reaches the adapter over the pod's shared network namespace. The adapter's intra-pod lifecycle channel and gRPC server are both still up when the gateway opens the `Checkpoint` stream. The pod's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` bounds the whole sequence; the budget rule is below.

A gateway replica draining its own pod takes the same eviction-trigger checkpoint for every session it coordinates ([§10.1](10_gateway-internals.md#101-horizontal-scaling) preStop stage 2 and the CheckpointBarrier flush). The two paths differ in what a failure costs: an agent pod's termination destroys its workspace, so a failed checkpoint falls back to the [§4.4](#44-event--checkpoint-store) minimal-state record; a gateway drain leaves the agent pod running with its workspace intact, so a failed checkpoint falls through to the session's last successful checkpoint.
```

The paragraph's closing sentence (the §7.2 retry mechanism resuming the session on a replacement pod) is unchanged. `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` keeps its role and its value is re-derived in the budget block below.

**Anchor and change (spec/10:30-37, the coordinator handoff may take a lease from a live holder on the termination path).** `spec/10:30` opens the handoff protocol with "When a replica acquires the coordination lease (Redis or Postgres)", and `spec/10:178` describes "a new gateway replica acquiring the coordination lease" after a drain. Both antecedents are an unheld or lapsed lease, and the shipped store implements only that: `leasestore.Store.Acquire` returns `ErrHeld` when a different holder owns the lease (`pkg/gateway/storage/leasestore/leasestore.go:125-141`) and the coordination sweeper skips the session on that error (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordination/coordination.go:132-137`). The `coordination_lease` table is a Postgres mirror of the Redis lease with no compare-and-swap on its `Store` interface (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordlease/coordlease.go:5-9`, `:61-84`), so it is not the thing a takeover CASes. The C7 handler lands on an arbitrary replica and cannot wait for a TTL to lapse, because the pod is already inside its termination grace period.

**Anchor and change (spec/10:36, the fence must terminate the superseded generation's in-flight work).** `spec/10:37` states that "there is no window in which both the old and new coordinator can simultaneously issue accepted RPCs", and §2, S2, C1, and C7 all rest the strictly-higher-generation supersede on a displaced coordinator's in-flight `Checkpoint` stopping at the pod when the fence acks. The only in-flight-cancellation rule the spec carries cannot deliver that. `spec/10:36` fires cancellation ("(a) immediately cancel and discard all in-flight RPCs received after `last_fenced_generation`") only when `new_generation > last_fenced_generation + 1`, and it closes by declaring that "a gap of exactly 1 … is the normal case and requires no special handling" — which is every handoff, because the `spec/10:33` CAS bumps by exactly one. A generation gate on the request (P1, C1) is evaluated once, at RPC entry, so it rejects the *next* request from a displaced coordinator and does nothing to the stream that is already running and already holding the adapter's pod-global op-lock (`pkg/adapter/server.go:328`, `checkpoint.go:177-189`, released by `defer`). The adapter's own comment records that even the gap-case cancellation is unimplemented (`pkg/adapter/coordination.go:62-64`, repeated `:92-95`).

`spec/10:36` is therefore restated so cancellation is a property of **every** accepted fence rather than of a gap: on acknowledging a `CoordinatorFence(new_generation)` the adapter MUST first cancel and discard every in-flight RPC for that session (and, on a concurrent-session pod, for that slot) whose `coordination_generation` is below `new_generation`, releasing any operation lock they hold, and only then record the new generation and acknowledge. The gap-specific clauses (b), (c), and (d) keep their meaning and stay scoped to a gap greater than 1; the sentence declaring a gap of exactly 1 to require no special handling is replaced by the cancellation rule above. Without it the supersede's safety argument has no enforcement point at the pod: the displaced coordinator's `Checkpoint` holds the op-lock for up to its full deadline (`CheckpointTimeout`, 60s, `pkg/checkpoint/checkpoint.go:159-160`) having committed no chunk, and the new coordinator's eviction `Checkpoint` for the same slot blocks in `opLock.Begin` (`pkg/adapter/oplock.go:57-73`) or is coalesced into `codes.Aborted` (`checkpoint.go:180-186`) and drops the slot to the minimal-state fallback. C1 builds the cancellation.

The handoff paragraph also gains one rule: **on the §4.6.1 agent-pod termination path, and only there, the receiving replica MAY take the coordination lease from a live holder.** It overwrites the holder atomically, then executes the existing sequence unchanged (the pre-CAS session read at `:30`, the `coordination_generation` CAS at `:33`, the `CoordinatorFence` precondition at `:35`, and only then any operational RPC at `:37`). The displaced coordinator learns of the takeover on its next sweep, whose `Acquire` (the call that renews a held lease, `coordination.go:10-14`) returns `ErrHeld`, and it then stops renewing the lease and stops mirroring the session; its release is already a no-op for a non-holder (`leasestore.go:170-172`). It does not drop its `podsession.Registry` binding there, because that arm fires for every session another replica happens to hold the lease for rather than only for a displaced one (C7); the binding is retired instead when the checkpoint driver finds the binding naming a Sandbox the session's `pod_assignment` no longer names, or when its `Checkpoint` RPC fails because the pod is gone (C2). The fence is what makes the takeover safe rather than the lease: `:35` already states that the pod accepts the previous generation until the fence acks and rejects it afterwards, and the restated `:36` above makes the ack cancel the displaced coordinator's in-flight RPCs, including its in-flight `Checkpoint` stream, so the displaced writer stops running at the pod on this handoff exactly as on any other. The takeover's effect on a checkpoint the displaced coordinator still has in flight is the strictly-higher-generation arm of the §10.1 supersede predicate (S2): the new attempt supersedes the victim's row, and the victim aborts on its next zero-row `UPDATE`. The same paragraph states what the generation does **not** do, because the number alone reads as a fence and is not one: `coordination_generation` is a property of the session row, so a displaced coordinator's *next* attempt re-reads the advanced value and presents the generation the pod's fence admits and the supersede predicate's equal-generation arm protects. The rule the spec therefore carries is that on the termination path the receiving replica reserves each of the pod's slots (minting the `checkpoint_id` and committing the intent row, §10.1 intent-row-first ordering) **in the same transaction as the CAS**, before it drives any slot, and stamps the declared budget's deadline as each reserved row's `checkpoint_timeout_at`. The committed row is what makes a later attempt from any replica abort on the settled-row term. On every other path (the coordination sweeper, a gateway drain) the lease is still acquired only when it is unheld or lapsed, and `:178` is unchanged. C7 adds the store primitive.

**Anchor and change (spec/06:67, the adapter's SIGTERM sequence).** §6.1 fixes the adapter's SIGTERM handling as an ordered sequence that begins with `DemoteSDK` and sizes the pod's grace period against that sequence alone: "The `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` on the pod spec must be set to at least `LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s` to give the adapter time to complete this sequence before Kubernetes sends SIGKILL." After this change the sequence begins with the blocking `NotifyPodTerminating` RPC, whose deadline can consume most of the grace period exactly when the gateway is slow or unreachable, so both the ordering and the sufficiency claim are false as written. Restate the paragraph: SIGTERM handling is (0) send `GatewayControl.NotifyPodTerminating` and block on it under a bounded deadline, which returns immediately for a pod with no bound session; (1) call `DemoteSDK` with its bounded timeout; (2) force-terminate the SDK process if the demote does not complete; and (3) exit. The demote-and-exit reserve (`LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s`, 10s at the defaults `podspec.go:84-93` carries as `sdkDemoteTimeoutSeconds` and `sdkDemoteGraceMarginSeconds`) is subtracted from the budget the adapter declares rather than from the deadline it applies to a larger declared budget, so the deadline the adapter honours on step 0 and the ceiling the gateway is handed are the same wall clock (P1). The demote-and-exit steps therefore keep the budget §6.1 reserves for them whatever the gateway does, and a gateway that is unreachable or hangs cannot cost the SDK teardown its window. The grace-sizing sentence is restated against the S3 agent-pod floor below, which carries the demote-and-exit reserve as an additive term rather than subsuming it: the reserve is serial with the gateway's checkpoint window, not concurrent with it. `LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` is deployer-configurable and the admission webhook cannot read it (it is an adapter env var rather than a `SandboxTemplate` field), so the staged `spec/06:67` text states the deployer's obligation directly: raising `LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` above its 5s default requires raising the pool's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` by the same amount, because the floor the webhook enforces is computed against the default. The same sizing sentence is mirrored in the code comments C7 edits (`pkg/controller/sandbox/podspec/podspec.go:1203-1212`, `pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/sandboxtemplate_types.go:174`), which are corrected with it. The preConnect grace floor in `terminationGrace` (`podspec.go:1213-1218`) is unchanged and still takes precedence over the `maxTerminationGracePeriodSeconds` clamp.

**Anchor and change (the grace-period budget, spec/04:489, spec/04:264 clause (b), spec/04:278, spec/05:542, and spec/10:116-122).** The budget arithmetic does not close today and this change is what makes it binding, so every site that states it is corrected together. There are two budgets on two different workloads, and the defect is that one rule is stated over both.

`spec/04:489` sets the agent pod's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` default at 120s (implemented as `defaultTerminationGraceSeconds` in `pkg/controller/sandbox/podspec/podspec.go:75`, with `terminationGrace` at `:1193` and a `preStopDrainMarginSeconds` of 10s at `:82`). `spec/04:264` clause (b) justifies the 30-second eviction MinIO retry budget by citing "`terminationGracePeriodSeconds` (240s at Tier 1/2, 300s at Tier 3)", and `spec/04:278` justifies the 60-second Postgres fallback budget against the same figures. Those figures are the **gateway** pod's (`spec/17:966-967`) rather than the agent pod's. S1 already reattributes clause (b) to the gateway pod for the gateway-drain path. For the agent-pod path the correct referent is the agent pod's own grace period, and against 120s the arithmetic fails: a single slot at the 90s cap, plus the 60s Postgres fallback, plus the 10s preStop drain margin, plus the 10s the adapter reserves for its demote-and-exit sequence (`podspec.go:84-93`), needs 170s.

**The agent-pod rule (the webhook's rule).** The `SandboxWarmPool` admission webhook validates the `SandboxTemplate` / `SandboxWarmPool` `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` field, which is the **agent** pod's grace period (`pkg/admission/pool_config_validator/validator.go:645-650`, `charts/lenny/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml:225-234`). Its rule becomes two arms, one per execution mode, because only one of the two modes runs the work the checkpoint terms budget:

```
session mode:
  maxConcurrentSessions × (max(max_tiered_checkpoint_cap, 90) + postgresFallbackBudgetSeconds)
    + preStopDrainMarginSeconds
    + sdkDemoteReserveSeconds
    ≤ terminationGracePeriodSeconds

service mode:
  preStopDrainMarginSeconds + sdkDemoteReserveSeconds
    ≤ terminationGracePeriodSeconds
```

**The checkpoint terms apply to the pool class that runs the checkpoints.** A service-mode pool "creates no `SandboxClaim`, and materializes no workspace" (`spec/05:391`), its session is claimless in code (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/create.go:326`), and it therefore carries no `sessions.pod_assignment` row for C7's `ListActiveByPod` to resolve, so `NotifyPodTerminating` returns for a terminating service pod with nothing to checkpoint. Charging such a pod `maxConcurrent × (90 + 60)` would budget a per-slot eviction checkpoint and a §4.4 Postgres minimal-state fallback that the pool never performs: a shipped `executionMode: service, maxConcurrent: 4` template with no grace period, which is admitted today, would be rejected against a 620s floor (1220s at `maxConcurrent: 8`), above the 600s node-drain warning threshold, for a pod that takes no checkpoint. The rejection would not be bypassable, because `spec/04:625` and `spec/10:116` apply rule set 1 to PoolScalingController SSA applies with no `userInfo` exemption, so every reconcile of such a pool would be denied and the pool would enter the §4.6.3 stuck-pool path. No spec line supports a service-mode checkpoint budget: `spec/05:542` states the rule over `maxConcurrentSessions`, a session-mode field. The service-mode arm therefore keeps only the two terms that are true of any terminating pod, the kubelet's drain margin and the adapter's demote-and-exit reserve, which sum to 20s and are cleared by the 180s default with no deployer action.

**The session-mode multiplier has to be readable by the webhook, so `sessionPolicy.maxConcurrentSessions` becomes a CRD field.** The shipped multiplier is `effectiveMaxConcurrent` (`validator.go:459-462`), which returns `spec.maxConcurrent` for service mode and **1 for every session-mode pool**, and its own doc comment concedes why: "The session-mode maxConcurrentSessions multiplier lives on the gateway-side sessionPolicy mirror" (`validator.go:452-454`). The `SessionPolicy` CRD type carries only `Recycle` (`pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/sandboxtemplate_types.go:28-34`, rendered at `charts/lenny/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml:176-186`), so the value the floor multiplies is invisible at admission and a rule whose multiplier collapses to 1 never fires for the class it exists to budget: a `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` pool would be admitted against the 170s floor while its serialized worst case is 1220s, render pods at the 180s default, and be SIGKILLed part-way through its second slot's checkpoint.

`SessionPolicy` therefore gains `MaxConcurrentSessions *int32` (`+optional`, defaulting to 1 when unset or below 1), which is the field `spec/04:416` **already** requires the CRD to validate ("Key rules: … `sessionPolicy.maxConcurrentSessions >= 1`") and which the CRD type simply omits. This is a §4.6.3-consistent addition rather than a new surface: `SandboxTemplate.spec.*` is owned by the PoolScalingController and "reconciled from Postgres pool definitions" (`spec/04:592`), and the PSC already reads `sessionPolicy.maxConcurrentSessions` off the poolstore row for its own `mode_factor` derivation (`pkg/controller/poolscaling/poolstoresource.go:124-130`). The poolstore stays the gateway-side source of truth; the CRD field is the mirror the webhook reads. `effectiveMaxConcurrent` then returns `sessionPolicy.maxConcurrentSessions` for a session-mode pool and keeps `spec.maxConcurrent` for a service-mode one (where it selects the mode's own two-term floor rather than a per-slot checkpoint term), and its doc comment's "lives on the gateway-side sessionPolicy mirror" clause is deleted. `spec/04:416` (the CEL rule) and `spec/04:592` (the §4.6.3 ownership row that gives the PoolScalingController `SandboxTemplate.spec.*`) are **confirmed unchanged**: both already cover the field, and the CRD type is what omits it. The chart CRD and the embedded copy are regenerated from the Go type, as they are for the field-doc edits below.

**The mirror is written by `sessionPolicyToCRD`, and its nil guards are what the edit has to remove.** `poolstoresource.go:124-130` assigns `cfg.MaxConcurrentSessions` on the controller's internal `PoolConfig` (the scaling struct) and never touches `SandboxTemplateSpec`, so it is not the CRD write path. The only code that renders `lennyv1.SessionPolicy` onto the CRD spec is `sessionPolicyToCRD` (`poolstoresource.go:145-159`), called at `:89-90`, and it returns nil when the store row carries no recycle block or when the recycle block carries neither a scrub profile nor the microvm acknowledgment. An ordinary non-recycling `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` pool, which is the default configuration, therefore renders `SandboxTemplate.spec.sessionPolicy = nil` no matter what is added to the Go type, the webhook reads no multiplier, and the pool is admitted at the collapsed 170s floor. The edit is therefore at `:89-90` and `:145-159`: `sessionPolicyToCRD` emits a non-nil `lennyv1.SessionPolicy` carrying `MaxConcurrentSessions` whenever the store row sets it, independently of the recycle and scrub guards, and keeps the scrub-control rendering it performs today. Its doc comment, which today says the CRD "does not model the session-mode recycle sizing knobs", is restated: the CRD models `maxConcurrentSessions` because the pool-config webhook evaluates the §5.2 grace floor against it, and the remaining recycle sizing knobs still reach the gateway through the poolstore alone.

**The grace period the floor is compared against needs a writer, and today it has none.** No component in the repository ever sets `SandboxTemplate.spec.terminationGracePeriodSeconds`. The PoolScalingController is the sole permitted writer of `SandboxTemplate.spec` (`validator.go:200-215`, `spec/04:592`), its `SandboxTemplateSpec` builder never populates the field (`pkg/controller/poolscaling/poolstoresource.go:65-96`), `poolstore.Pool` carries no grace-period column and no migration defines one, and `syncTemplate` replaces the whole spec wholesale on every reconcile (`pkg/controller/poolscaling/controller.go:595-597`, `:611-613`), so a value set by hand is wiped on the next tick. A deployer-facing `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` is nevertheless already published in the pool configuration (`docs/operator-guide/configuration.md:285`) and `spec/05:542` already tells deployers to "set `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` accordingly", so the knob is documented and unplumbed. Without a writer, dropping the `!= nil` guard below would make the effective grace period always the 180s default, and every session-mode pool with `maxConcurrentSessions ≥ 2` would be rejected on every PSC apply with no remediation path, which is the §4.6.3 stuck-pool state rather than a configuration error the deployer can fix.

The writer path is therefore built with the multiplier's, on the same channel and with no new surface: a `termination_grace_period_seconds` column on the pools table (`migrations/`), the field on `poolstore.Pool` and its pgstore, the admin-API pool create and update payloads that already carry the pool's other budget fields, and the `PoolStoreSource.toConfig` mirror onto `SandboxTemplateSpec.TerminationGracePeriodSeconds` (`poolstoresource.go:65-96`). The column is nullable and the PSC leaves the CRD field nil when it is unset, so an unset pool keeps the 180s effective default, which clears the session-mode floor at `maxConcurrentSessions: 1` and the service-mode floor at every `maxConcurrent`. A pool that needs more sets the column, and the webhook's rejection message names it. The floor keeps a single evaluator: the PSC mirrors a value and computes no floor.
