The per-slot checkpoint term is `max(max_tiered_checkpoint_cap, 90)` rather than the pool's own tier cap. `max_tiered_checkpoint_cap` is derived from `spec.workspaceSizeLimitBytes` (30s at ≤ 100 MB, 60s at ≤ 300 MB, 90s above; `validator.go:549-566`, `spec/10:104-106`), but the deadline the gateway actually applies is the **90s maximum tier** for any session with no prior checkpoint, whatever the pool's size limit (`spec/10:108`, implemented at `pkg/gateway/podlifecycle/prestop/prestop.go:93-96` and `:378-384` as `MaxTierBudget` on the `postgres_null` and `cache_miss` paths). Every session's first checkpoint is in that state. A rule keyed on the pool's tier cap would admit a `workspaceSizeLimitBytes: 100Mi` pool at `1 × (30 + 60) + 10 + 10 = 110s` while the real worst case on the eviction path is 170s, and the kubelet would SIGKILL the pod with the first checkpoint of every fresh session unfinalised. The tier table tops out at 90s, so the term evaluates to 90s for every pool today; it is written as a `max` so that a later tier addition cannot silently drop below the fallback the gateway must select.

`postgresFallbackBudgetSeconds` is the 60s of [§4.4](#44-event--checkpoint-store), `preStopDrainMarginSeconds` is the 10s the podspec already reserves for the kubelet to reap the container **after the adapter exits** (`podspec.go:77-82`), and `sdkDemoteReserveSeconds` is the `LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s` the adapter spends demoting the SDK and exiting after the gateway returns (`podspec.go:84-93`, 10s at the defaults). All three claims are **serial** on one grace period: the drain margin runs after the adapter exits, the demote-and-exit runs after `NotifyPodTerminating` returns, and the per-slot checkpoints run inside the RPC. The demote reserve is therefore an additive term rather than one the checkpoint window subsumes; omitting it would hand the gateway a window 10s shorter than the floor budgets, and the 10s would come off the tail of the 60s Postgres fallback, which is the write that separates a `conversation_only` resume from the §4.4 total-loss path. Both the checkpoint and the fallback run **serialized per slot**, which `spec/05:542` already states, so the per-slot term is summed rather than maxed. The default agent-pod `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` rises from 120s to **180s**, which clears the `maxConcurrentSessions: 1` floor (`1 × (90 + 60) + 10 + 10 = 170s`) with a 10s margin. The floor is a floor rather than a target: the webhook rejects a value strictly below it (`validator.go:643`), so a session-mode pool admitted exactly at the floor must still leave the gateway `maxConcurrentSessions × (max(cap, 90) + 60)` of usable window, which the additive demote term is what guarantees.

**The rule is enforced against the *effective* grace period, so an unset field cannot escape it.** The shipped webhook rejects only a value the deployer supplied: `decideTerminationBudget` guards its floor comparison with `if spec.TerminationGracePeriodSeconds != nil && *spec.TerminationGracePeriodSeconds < floor` (`pkg/admission/pool_config_validator/validator.go:644`). The field is `+optional` with no CRD default (`pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/sandboxtemplate_types.go:167-178`; `charts/lenny/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml:225-237` carries no `default:` key), no defaulting webhook exists, and when it is unset the reconciler passes nil through (`pkg/controller/sandbox/controller.go:332`) and the podspec renders the flat `defaultTerminationGraceSeconds` constant, which carries no `maxConcurrentSessions` term (`podspec.go:1193-1197`, `:75`). A `maxConcurrentSessions: 2` session-mode pool that omits the field would therefore be admitted with no rejection and no warning (its floor of `2 × (90 + 60) + 10 + 10 = 320s` sits under the 600s node-drain warning threshold) and would render pods with 180s of grace, and the kubelet would SIGKILL the pod part-way through the second slot's serialized eviction checkpoint. That hole is inert today, because `Checkpoint` returns `codes.Unimplemented` and nothing runs during the agent pod's grace period; C7 makes that grace period load-bearing for the first time. The `!= nil` guard is therefore dropped: the webhook compares the **effective** grace period (the deployer's value when set, and `defaultTerminationGraceSeconds` when unset) against the floor and rejects with `422 INVALID_POOL_CONFIGURATION` when it is below, naming the pool's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` and the floor it fell short of. That rejection is actionable only because the writer path above makes the field settable: without it the value is nil on every template the PoolScalingController applies, the effective grace period is always the 180s default, and the rejection has no remediation. The rule is stated in that form at `spec/04:489`, `spec/05:542`, `spec/10:116-122`, and in the `TerminationGracePeriodSeconds` field doc, all of which say that a pool whose effective grace period is below the floor is rejected whether or not the field is set, and all of which state the floor as two arms, one per execution mode. The default stays a single constant rather than a formula the podspec evaluates, so the podspec keeps one grace-period source and the webhook keeps one rule.

**Anchor and change (spec/04:625, the webhook's rule-set 1 rationale).** §4.6.3 states the semantic/budget rules that apply to every write including PoolScalingController SSA applies, and it justifies rule 1 on the wrong workload: "the PoolScalingController is rejected if it attempts to write a configuration that violates the preStop budget, because any such configuration would guarantee SIGKILL during **gateway drain** regardless of the writer's identity." The budget rule bounds the **agent** pod's own termination sequence (its per-slot eviction checkpoints, its Postgres fallback, and its preStop drain margin), and after the split above the gateway pod's 210s drain budget is not an admission rule at all. Reword the rationale so a violating configuration guarantees SIGKILL of the **agent pod during its own termination** (a node drain, the kubelet Eviction API, a direct pod delete, or a cluster upgrade) before its per-slot eviction checkpoints and its Postgres fallback complete. The `checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` floor rule stays as the second, separate rule of the same set, and the rule set's `userInfo`-bypass exemption is unchanged.

**Anchor and change (spec/16:132, the budget-exceeded counter row).** The §16.1 inventory row for `lenny_pool_termination_budget_exceeded_total` does not merely name the counter: it inlines the rejection predicate as the superseded inequality ("increments each time the `lenny-pool-config-validator` admission webhook rejects a `SandboxTemplate`/`SandboxWarmPool` write because `max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + 30 > terminationGracePeriodSeconds`") and labels its cross-reference "CRD validation rule — tiered cap + BarrierAck budget". Left unedited, the applied spec would carry the agent-pod formula at `spec/04:489`, `spec/05:542`, `spec/10:116-122` and the CRD field docs, and a mutually exclusive statement of the retired one here. Restate the increment condition on the agent-pod floor (`maxConcurrentSessions × (max(max_tiered_checkpoint_cap, 90) + postgresFallbackBudgetSeconds) + preStopDrainMarginSeconds + sdkDemoteReserveSeconds > terminationGracePeriodSeconds` on a session-mode pool, and `preStopDrainMarginSeconds + sdkDemoteReserveSeconds > terminationGracePeriodSeconds` on a service-mode pool, both evaluated against the pool's effective grace period) and relabel the cross-reference "CRD validation rule — agent-pod termination budget". The counter's name, type, and `pool` label are unchanged.

**`checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` leaves the agent-pod floor.** The shipped webhook computes `floor := maxConcurrent*tierCap + barrierAck + minStreamDrainSeconds` (`validator.go:635`), and `checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` is the wall-clock the **gateway** replica waits for `CheckpointBarrierAck` from every pod it coordinates while draining its own pod (`spec/10:167`). It bounds a gateway drain and consumes none of the agent pod's grace period, and the agent pod's own eviction sequence dispatches no barrier. Keeping it would put the `maxConcurrentSessions: 1` floor at `1 × (90 + 60) + 90 + 10 + 10 = 260s`, above the 180s default this change sets, for a deadline that does not run on the pod being sized. The term is therefore dropped from the agent-pod floor, and the `30` stream-drain constant is replaced by the `preStopDrainMarginSeconds` the podspec actually reserves. The BarrierAck-**floor** rule (`checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds ≥ max_tiered_checkpoint_cap`) keeps its rejection and is unchanged: it is the check at `validator.go:628-634`, documented at `validator.go:609-612` and specified at `spec/10:124`, it governs the same field for a different reason, and S1 re-argues its rationale.

`decideTerminationBudget`'s own doc comment is part of the edit rather than a passage left behind it. The comment block at `validator.go:591-600` states the retired floor formula verbatim (`floor = maxConcurrent * max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + minStreamDrainSeconds`) and its rejection-rule bullets at `:602-612` state the retired guard ("When spec.terminationGracePeriodSeconds is set and below the floor"). Both are false under this change, and the tier-11 assertion that the budget rule is stated identically at `decideTerminationBudget` would fail on them, so C7's edit range for the file starts at `:585` rather than at `:618` and the comment is restated on the agent-pod floor, the additive demote reserve, and the effective-grace comparison.

**The gateway-pod budget keeps the 210s arithmetic, stated as a Helm value rather than a CRD rule.** `spec/10:116-122` states the webhook rule as `max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + 30 > terminationGracePeriodSeconds` and concludes that "`terminationGracePeriodSeconds` must be set to at least 210s **on gateway pods**". That conclusion is about the gateway pod and the rule is enforced against the agent pod's CRD field, which is the contradiction. Split them: `:116-122` states the agent-pod webhook rule above (keeping its `422 INVALID_POOL_CONFIGURATION` rejection, its `lenny_pool_termination_budget_exceeded_total` metric, whose §16.1 row is restated on the new inequality above, and its scope over every `SandboxTemplate.spec` / `SandboxWarmPool.spec` mutation including PoolScalingController SSA applies, now evaluated against the pool's effective grace period rather than only against an explicitly set one), and states the gateway pod's own drain budget (`max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + 30 = 210s at defaults`) as the budget the chart's `gateway.terminationGracePeriodSeconds` value covers at 240s (`charts/lenny/values.yaml:2402-2408`, `spec/17:967`), which is not an admission rule and is not enforced by any webhook, because the gateway pod carries no `SandboxTemplate`. `spec/10:167` ("This is why the CRD validation formula (below) adds `max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + 30`") and `spec/10:177` (the same figure, as "the CRD-validated … budget") both restate the old conflation and are reworded in the same edit to name the gateway pod's drain budget rather than a CRD rule.

`spec/05:542` already carries the webhook's formula as `maxConcurrentSessions × max_tiered_checkpoint_cap + checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds + 30 ≤ terminationGracePeriodSeconds`, and the same bullet already tells deployers, as advice, to "use `max(max_tiered_checkpoint_cap, 60)` when computing `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` manually if MinIO degradation is a concern". The advice becomes the rule and `:542` carries the agent-pod formula verbatim in both of its arms, including the session-mode `maxConcurrentSessions` multiplier, the `sdkDemoteReserveSeconds` term, and the service-mode arm that budgets only the drain margin and the demote reserve, with the advisory sentence deleted rather than left contradicting the formula above it. The bullet is a §5.2 checkpoint-granularity rule and its subject is `maxConcurrentSessions`, so the split is what keeps it stating a rule about the pools it describes. The rejection code, the >600s node-drain-timeout warning, the `maxTerminationGracePeriodSeconds` hard-ceiling rejection, and the `lenny-preflight` check are unchanged, and they are what keep a high-`maxConcurrentSessions` pool from silently configuring a grace period past the cluster's drain timeout: at `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` the floor is `8 × 150 + 10 + 10 = 1220s`, well past the 600s warning threshold and past most cluster drain timeouts, exactly as the existing warning says. The same bullet also points deployers at "a helper formula in `values.yaml` comments"; no such comment exists (`charts/lenny/values.yaml` carries one `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` key and it is the gateway Deployment's, at `:2402-2408`, and no chart template renders an agent-pod grace period), so the sentence is corrected to point at the `SandboxTemplate` field docs, which is where the formula is published.

**The CRD field docs are edited at their controller-gen source.** `charts/lenny/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml` is generated (`Makefile:78` runs `controller-gen crd paths=./pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/... output:crd:dir=charts/lenny/crds`), so the formula in its field docs is rendered from the Go doc comments and a YAML-only edit is reverted by the next `make generate`. The edits therefore land in `pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/sandboxtemplate_types.go`: `TerminationGracePeriodSeconds` (`:167-178`, which carries the old formula and the "§4.6.1 120s default base"), `MaxTerminationGracePeriodSeconds` (`:160-165`), `WorkspaceSizeLimitBytes` (`:180-190`, which describes the tier cap fanned across slots), and `CheckpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` (`:192-202`, whose "The pool-config webhook adds it to the per-pod `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` floor" clause is deleted, leaving the BarrierAck-floor rule it still governs). `charts/lenny/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml` is regenerated from them, and `pkg/embedded/crds/lenny.dev_sandboxtemplates.yaml` is re-copied, because a shipped test requires the two to stay byte-identical (`pkg/embedded/crds/crds_test.go:80`, `TestEmbeddedCRDsAreCopiesOfChartManifests_spec_10_437`).

`spec/17:976-979`'s `worst_case_single_node_drain_time = pods_on_node × terminationGracePeriodSeconds` formula is **confirmed unchanged**: it is scoped to co-located **gateway** pods and the gateway pod's grace period (`spec/17:973` ties it to the gateway Deployment's `maxUnavailable: 1` PDB, and `:979` evaluates it at the gateway's Tier-3 300s), which this change does not touch. The agent pod's node-drain consequence of the new 180s default is stated where the agent-pod grace period is stated (`spec/04:489`), not there.

The 30s MinIO retry budget and the 60s Postgres fallback budget themselves are unchanged. What changes is the figure they are argued against: on the agent-pod path it is the agent pod's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`, and `spec/04:264` clause (b) and `spec/04:278` say so, each naming the pod class its arm applies to.

**Anchor and change (the agent-pod eviction fallback arm goes live, spec/04:266, :278, :284, :286, :287).** S1 scopes the §4.4 minimal-state record, its 60-second Postgres retry budget, and the total-loss path to the agent-pod eviction class. That class now has a trigger, so those paragraphs stop describing an arm with no producer and start describing the arm C7 builds. Their normative content is unchanged: a failed agent-pod eviction checkpoint writes the `session_eviction_state` minimal-state row, increments `lenny_checkpoint_eviction_fallback_total`, and the session resumes `conversation_only` with `workspaceLost: true`; exhausting the Postgres budget as well takes the total-loss path, publishes `session.lost` with `reason: "eviction_total_loss"`, and increments `lenny_session_eviction_total_loss_total`. Two edits attach to them. `spec/04:286` step 1 has the gateway publish `session.lost` "before the preStop hook exits", which is true on the arms that fire while the pod's grace period still holds the slot's `postgresFallbackBudgetSeconds`: a slot whose MinIO retries are exhausted, and a slot whose coordination handoff cannot complete. It is unsatisfiable on the third arm, where the pod's declared `budget_ms` elapses with the slot unsettled, because `budget_ms` already excludes the drain margin and the demote-and-exit reserve (P1) and the gateway therefore has no grace period left to run a 60-second Postgres write in. The step is restated with both cases named: the gateway publishes `session.lost` before it returns `NotifyPodTerminating` when the fallback runs inside the RPC, and when the pod's budget elapsed first it returns at `budget_ms` and completes the minimal-state record and the publish on a gateway-owned context detached from the pod's lifetime, under the §4.4 60-second Postgres budget of `:278`. The record and the event are durable either way; only their ordering against the pod's exit differs, and the record does not depend on the pod. `spec/04:287`'s `lenny_session_eviction_total_loss_total` label set (`pool`, `had_prior_checkpoint`) is the one `spec/16:274` is corrected to (S1).

**Anchor and change (the drain-readiness webhook, spec/12:296, :298, :303).** S3's rewrite of `spec/12:296` (the simultaneous MinIO-outage-and-node-drain risk) replaces the pod-side preStop upload contention with the real mechanism, which is now a funnel rather than a fan-out: the agent pods on the drained node signal one gateway, and that gateway drives their checkpoints against an unavailable MinIO, each exhausting its 30-second retry budget and then its 60-second Postgres fallback, serialized per slot. The three mitigations are unchanged and the first two become more load-bearing: the `CheckpointStale` alert, staggered node drains, and separated maintenance windows. The pre-drain MinIO health check (`spec/12:298`, the gateway's `GET /internal/drain-readiness` performing an S3 `HeadBucket`) and its `lenny-drain-readiness` `ValidatingAdmissionWebhook` on `pods/eviction` (`spec/12:303`, rendered by `charts/lenny/templates/admission-policies/drain-readiness-webhook.yaml`) are unchanged in mechanism and gain one sentence stating why they now matter more: a gateway-mediated eviction checkpoint cannot run at all if the gateway itself is unreachable, so the webhook is what keeps a drain from proceeding into a state where no checkpoint can be taken.

**Anchor and change (the gateway-initiated pod release, spec/06:216-220).** The `maxSuspendedPodHoldSeconds` graceful-release path already has the gateway initiate the checkpoint and already refuses to release the pod when the checkpoint fails. It routes through the same `Checkpointer` and therefore through the C2 upload driver with no change, and it keeps `checkpoint.TriggerPeriodic`: the pod is released rather than terminated under a grace period, so no eviction budget applies. The site is listed to record that it was checked and that it needs no edit.

**Anchor and change (the pages that describe the preStop checkpoint).** Published pages describe the agent pod's preStop hook taking a checkpoint. They stay true in outcome and change in actor, and each is corrected to the gateway-driven form: `docs/getting-started/architecture.md:238` ("checkpoint active sessions before eviction via preStop hook"), `docs/runbooks/minio-failure.md:106` ("When a preStop checkpoint cannot reach MinIO, the gateway writes a minimal row to `session_eviction_state`", whose mixed subject the signal now formalizes), `docs/runbooks/session-eviction-loss.md:31` (the total-loss paragraph), and `TESTING.md:1067` ("`kubectl drain` triggers preStop checkpoint; session resumes on new pod; workspace intact"), which becomes the scenario the tier-5 test in §6 actually asserts. `docs/runbooks/node-drain-during-minio-outage.md` names no actor and describes no fallback mechanism; its edit is its front-matter symptom (`:11`) and the page summary that repeats it (`:19`), both of which state unqualified that "checkpoints landed in minimal state". That outcome holds for the agent-pod eviction class alone, so both lines are scoped to it in the same terms as the staged `docs/runbooks/minio-unavailable.md:11` and `:19` edit, and the rest of the page is confirmed unchanged. `docs/runbooks/stuck-finalizer.md` is confirmed unchanged: it contains no preStop text, no checkpoint actor, and no eviction-fallback prose. `spec/17:11` is restated by the block below. `docs/runbooks/pod-kill-during-session.md` carries no such passage and is confirmed unchanged: `:29` attributes the final snapshot to "the §4.4 checkpoint pipeline" with no actor and stays true verbatim, and `:56` names `preStop` as a kubelet event reason inside a `kubectl get events` search hint rather than as a checkpoint author.

**Anchor and change (the passages that describe the preStop hook as the checkpoint's author).** After the rewrite above the agent pod's preStop hook still triggers the checkpoint and still holds the pod open for it, but the hook signals rather than uploads. Three passages describe it as the author and are restated on the signal:

- `spec/04:491` refers to "the preStop-based protection on active session pods" when explaining that the warm-pod PDB selector excludes active pods. The protection is real and stays; reword to "the preStop-signalled, gateway-driven eviction checkpoint on active session pods". The PDB argument, the `maxUnavailable: 1` rule, and the recycle-window carve-out are unchanged.
- `spec/04:493` (the `lenny.dev/session-cleanup` finalizer) releases the finalizer only after "(a) no session references the pod, or (b) the session has been successfully checkpointed and the gateway has been notified". Condition (b) is now satisfiable on every voluntary-disruption path, because the gateway drives the checkpoint before the pod exits. It is still unsatisfiable on an involuntary one (a node crash runs no preStop hook), where the session has neither a fresh checkpoint nor a notification, and the `Sandbox` would sit in `Terminating` until the `FinalizerStuck` alert. Restate condition (b): the session has a durable checkpoint recorded (the last successful checkpoint of any trigger) or a §4.4 eviction-fallback minimal-state record, and the gateway has been notified so it can resume the session on a replacement pod.
- `spec/17:11` lists "preStop checkpoint hook for active pods" as an agent-pod deployment property. Reword to "preStop hook that signals the gateway and holds the pod open until the gateway-driven eviction checkpoint completes, for active pods".

**Anchor and change (spec/10:132).** Step 3 is titled **Finalisation on deadline fire** and defines the finalisation `UPDATE` for the timeout case alone. Retitle it to **Finalisation** and add the success arm, reusing the same UPDATE:

```
   The gateway issues the same finalisation `UPDATE` with `partial = false`, the committed `chunk_count`, and `workspace_bytes_uploaded` when, and only when, the adapter's terminal `CheckpointSummary` frame arrived within the deadline and `workspace_bytes_uploaded` equals `CheckpointSummary.size_bytes`. A stream that reaches EOF without a summary, or whose committed byte count disagrees with the summary, carries a truncated archive and finalises `partial = true` with `manifest_reason = "timeout"`, exactly as a deadline fire does. Stream termination alone is not a completion signal: an adapter can end a server stream with an OK status after a truncated archive, and a `partial = false` row over an incomplete chunk set is indistinguishable from a valid checkpoint on every later read. Every finalisation writes the committed `chunk_count`. Because the counter is reserved before the `PutObject` it names, an attempt cut between a reservation and the completion of that `PutObject` leaves at most one index whose outcome the gateway did not observe and whose object may nonetheless have committed server-side. Before the finalising `UPDATE` the gateway MUST attempt to resolve that index by `Stat`ing its derived key, and MUST take one of three arms. An object present at the byte length the gateway was writing is counted (`workspace_bytes_uploaded` advances by that length and the reserved `chunk_count` stands). An object the `Stat` reports as definitely absent, or definitely present at a different byte length, is soft-deleted through the `ArtifactStore` and the count is lowered. A `Stat` that fails for any other reason leaves the index **unresolved**, and an unresolved index MUST keep the reserved `chunk_count` and MUST NOT be soft-deleted: the object may have committed server-side, and only the reserved count keeps it inside the derived key set every later sweep enumerates. A `chunk_count` lowered past a committed object would put that object outside `[0, chunk_count)` for every manifest-derived path and outside the `artifact_store` catalog (the catalog row is inserted only on a successful `Put`), leaving it permanently orphaned and its bytes on the tenant's counter. The unresolved arm is the common one on the failure this rule exists for, because an object store that is unreachable during the upload is also unreachable to the resolving `Stat`. The unresolved index is always the highest index of the row, since the chunk `Put`s of one attempt are serial, and an attempt with an unresolved index always finalises `partial = true`, because `workspace_bytes_uploaded` advances only on a `Put` the gateway observed. This UPDATE is the atomicity boundary: no manifest row is `partial = false` until the adapter has declared the archive complete and every byte it declared has committed. The finalising UPDATE carries the `checkpoint_id = $id AND deleted_at IS NULL` guard, and `rows_affected == 0` is an abort rather than a success: a newer coordinator has superseded the attempt (soft-deleting its row and deleting its chunks) after its last chunk committed, so there is no further counter `UPDATE` left to observe the supersede. The superseded writer therefore writes no `partial = false` row, performs no session-record stamp and no retention-catalog INSERT, releases the remainder of its reservation, and takes the abort sweep over its own derived key set. Without that rule the losing writer would stamp `workspace_snapshot_ref`, `last_successful_checkpoint_at`, and `last_checkpoint_workspace_bytes` and insert a retention row naming a chunk set the new coordinator has already deleted, so derive, replay, and `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` would resolve absent objects and the latest-2 rotation would evict a real checkpoint in the phantom's favour. The finalising UPDATE, the `chunk_count == 0` soft-delete, the abort sweep, and the storage-quota release run on a **gateway-owned** context detached from the deadline that cut the attempt, under a bounded budget of the gateway's own; a deadline that cancels the upload context must not also cancel the Postgres write that records what the upload achieved. That budget is not drawn from the terminating pod's grace period. On the gateway replica's own drain it comes out of the gateway pod's remaining `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` ([§17.8](17_deployment-topology.md)). On the agent-pod eviction path the gateway is not the terminating workload, and the pod's declared budget is by construction zero at the instant it elapses, so the bookkeeping runs on the gateway's own clock after the gateway returns `NotifyPodTerminating`, independently of whether the pod still exists. On a `partial = false` finalisation only, the retention-catalog INSERT plus latest-2 rotation ([§12.5](12_storage-architecture.md#125-artifact-store)) and the `sessions.last_successful_checkpoint_at` and `last_checkpoint_workspace_bytes` stamps ([§4.4](04_system-components.md#44-event--checkpoint-store)) follow the finalising UPDATE and remain best-effort; a catalog or rotation failure does not unwind a committed checkpoint. A `partial = true` finalisation updates neither the session record nor the retention catalog: a partial manifest "is not a valid checkpoint; it is a recovery aid" ([§4.4](04_system-components.md#44-event--checkpoint-store)), the `last_successful_checkpoint_at` stamp is defined on every **successful** checkpoint, and `last_checkpoint_workspace_bytes` is the frozen `baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes` denominator and the Stage 2 tier-cap input, so a truncated byte count there would corrupt the next attempt's threshold and its cap tier.
```

The `chunk_count == 0` soft-delete arm keeps its soft-delete and its CPS-010 rule ("an empty partial manifest is never exposed to the resume path"), and its fall-through target is corrected. It currently routes to "the `CheckpointStorageUnavailable` eviction path ([§4.4])", which is the arm that writes the Postgres minimal-state record and resumes the session `conversation_only`. On the gateway-drain trigger the pod and its workspace survive and no such record is written (C6), so a zero-chunk attempt falls through to the session's last successful checkpoint (`spec/10:180`) and increments `lenny_checkpoint_storage_failure_total{reason="no_chunks_committed"}` (S1, which declares that value); the minimal-state record applies to the agent-pod eviction class (S3, C7), where the pod and its workspace do not survive. The BarrierAck-handler sentence that closes the step keeps its mechanism (the handler reads the intent row directly from Postgres rather than polling an unresponsive pod) and is edited twice: its "or the adapter otherwise stops uploading" clause is dropped, because the gateway is the uploader and the trigger is the end of the adapter's archive stream or the deadline; and it names `object_key_prefix` rather than the renamed `partial_object_key_prefix` (S2).

### S4. Remove the phantom snapshot-close manifest producer

**Target:** `spec/07_session-lifecycle.md` §7.2 (`:214`); `spec/04_system-components.md` §4.2 (`:157`) and §4.4 (`:237`); `spec/10_gateway-internals.md` §10.1 (`:130`, `:141`, `:157`); `spec/16_observability.md` §16.1 (`:195`).

The spec mandates a manifest row that its own cleanup rules make impossible. `spec/07:214` requires that "Any partial-checkpoint manifest row written by the aborted snapshot replay MUST be tagged ... `manifest_reason = 'terminated_during_resume'`", but the replay path writes no manifest row and commits no chunks: `spec/10:155` is a read-then-delete pipeline and `spec/04:237` deletes the row it consumed. The antecedent is never satisfied. The one row in hand at collapse time is the row being restored, and under S2 that row is usually a `partial = false` full checkpoint, which `spec/07:214` step 2 itself designates as the session's `final_workspace_ref`. Retagging it `partial = true` would invalidate the artifact the same paragraph relies on.

**Anchor and change (spec/07:214).** Delete the sentence beginning "Any partial-checkpoint manifest row written by the aborted snapshot replay MUST be tagged". Replace with:

```
The aborted replay writes no manifest row and commits no chunks. Any manifest row the replay was consuming is cleaned up on the abort path exactly as on the success and failure branches of the [§4.4](04_system-components.md#44-event--checkpoint-store) partial-checkpoint cleanup: the gateway deletes every chunk under `object_key_prefix` and soft-deletes the row under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard. A row that escapes this path is reclaimed by the [§12.5](12_storage-architecture.md#125-artifact-store) GC backstop. The durable record of an intentional mid-resume collapse is the terminal session state plus the `coordination_generation` bump written in the same transaction.
```

Steps (a) and (b) of the generation bookkeeping are unchanged; they are already implemented at `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:3397`.

**Anchor and change (spec/04:157).** Delete the final sentence of the paragraph, which restates the same mandate ("The partial-checkpoint manifest row written by the aborted replay carries both counter values plus `manifest_reason = 'terminated_during_resume'` ..."). The preceding bump-and-freeze semantics stand and are already built.

**Anchor and change (spec/04:237).** Widen the cleanup trigger from "On resume — whether the partial reconstruction succeeds or fails" to "On resume — whether the partial reconstruction succeeds, fails, or is aborted by a mid-resume terminal collapse ([§7.2](07_session-lifecycle.md#72-interactive-session-model))". Scope the paragraph explicitly to `partial = true` rows: a `partial = false` row is read-only on restore, and its chunks are owned by the latest-2 rotation and the §12.5 GC. Replace the discovery clause "every chunk object listed under the manifest's `partial_object_key_prefix`" with the S2 derivation: every chunk key derived from the row's `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` for `n` in `[0, chunk_count)`.

**Anchor and change (spec/10:130).** Drop the trailing clause "; `terminated_during_resume` for the §7.2 snapshot-close path", leaving `manifest_reason` populated by the upload that authored the chunks.

**Anchor and change (spec/10:141, :157 and spec/16:195).** Remove `terminated_during_resume` from the `manifest_reason` value set and `resume_snapshot_close` from the `trigger` label set on `lenny_checkpoint_partial_total`. `timeout` is the sole remaining `manifest_reason` value; keep the column single-valued for forward compatibility and say so in `:141`.

Rescope the `trigger` label in the same edit rather than only shrinking it. Both surfaces define the label as "which deadline finalised the row" over the closed set `tier_cap | barrier_ack_timeout | resume_snapshot_close`, and they scope the counter's increment condition to those deadline paths. After S2 every trigger commits an intent row and any stream that reaches EOF without a terminal `CheckpointSummary` finalises `partial = true`, so a periodic checkpoint (the most frequent trigger, one per active session per `periodicCheckpointIntervalSeconds`) and a `Seal` checkpoint can each author a partial row, and neither carries a Stage 2 tier cap or a BarrierAck deadline. As the label stands, that row class has no legal value: an implementer must emit an out-of-enum value or emit nothing, and a session that later resumes `partial_workspace` from a truncated periodic checkpoint would never appear in the counter and its `recovered` flip would be unattributable. `:157` and `spec/16:195` therefore restate the label as the checkpoint path that authored the row, over the set `tier_cap | barrier_ack_timeout | periodic`, and restate the increment condition as once per manifest row finalised `partial = true`. `periodic` covers the deadline-less classes, matching the `checkpoint.TriggerPeriodic` value C5 threads through `snapshotWithTrigger` for both the periodic ticker and `Seal`. One value is added and no metric is introduced; the tier-cap and BarrierAck deadline classes keep the distinction `:157` asks operators to make. `pre_scale_down` gains its value when its trigger ships; it stays a non-goal here (§5).

### P1. Widen Checkpoint to server-streaming and Resume to client-streaming on the existing Adapter service, and add NotifyPodTerminating to GatewayControl

**Target:** `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto` (`service Adapter`, the `Checkpoint` RPC at `:103` and the `Resume` RPC at `:120` with their doc comments at `:100-103` and `:116-119`; `CheckpointRequest` / `CheckpointResponse` `:1021-1029`, which gain `slot_id`; `ResumeRequest` `:1048-1107`, which becomes `ResumeStart` and gains `slot_id`; `ResumeResponse` `:1108-1122`; `CheckpointBarrierRequest` and `CheckpointBarrierResponse` `:1174-1179` and its doc comment `:1169-1172`; `CoordinatorFenceRequest` `:1129-1135`, which gains `slot_id`; the `CheckpointBarrier` RPC doc comment `:136-141`; `service GatewayControl` at `:215`); the regenerated `pkg/proto/adapter/v1` (`make generate-proto`); `spec/04_system-components.md` §4.5 (`:312`) and §4.7 (the contract-table rows `:655`, `:656`, and `:661`, the `CheckpointBarrierAck` event row `:686`, the operation-lock paragraph `:672`, and the `GatewayControl` RPC table); `spec/07_session-lifecycle.md` §7.1 (`:81`, `:92`) and §7.2 (`:212`, `:222`); `spec/12_storage-architecture.md` (`:331`, `:938`); `spec/15_external-api-surface.md` §15.5 (`:1056`) and §15.4.3 (`:2291-2300`); `schemas/lifecycle-events.schema.json` (`:68-77`, `:80-101`); `schemas/examples/lifecycle.checkpoint_request.json`, `lifecycle.checkpoint_ready.json`, `lifecycle.checkpoint_complete.json`; `docs/api/internal.md` (`:89-90`, the `service RuntimeAdapter` block's `Checkpoint` entry; `:210-226`, `:331-360`); `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md` (`:67`, `:68`, `:73`, `:86` the operation-lock paragraph, which is the published mirror of `spec/04:672` and states the retired per-session lock, `:96`, `:540`, `:552`, `:627-633`); `docs/reference/error-catalog.md` (`:150`); `docs/runtime-author-guide/lifecycle.md` (`:183-198`); `docs/runtime-author-guide/integration-levels.md` (`:156`, `:165`, `:170`, the three lifecycle frames' `checkpointId` examples). `sdks/runtime/go/runtime/lifecycle.go` (`:65-70`, `:191-213`), `sdks/runtime/typescript/src/lifecycle.ts` (`:35-37`, `:176-190`), and `sdks/runtime/python/lenny_runtime/lifecycle.py` (`:51-53`, `:192-202`) are **confirmed unchanged**: their doc comments already describe the handshake as quiesce-only.

An inbound byte channel exists (`PrepareWorkspace`, `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:38`, `:645`), but its staging semantics land the whole archive on the same emptyDir the restore must fit inside, which collides with the `expected_workspace_bytes` / `workspace_size_limit_bytes` pre-extraction guard already on `ResumeRequest` (`:1088-1105`), and it routes extraction through `FinalizeWorkspace`'s `WorkspacePlan` and the §13.4 `ArchivePolicy` ceilings (`:661-699`), which do not apply to a verbatim checkpoint restore. The only outbound byte path (`ExportPaths`, `ExportedFile.content` at `:1211`) is unary and bounded by `fileExportLimits`. No streaming archive channel exists in either direction, and the restore direction is already spec-mandated ("Stream the chunks to the adapter", `spec/10_gateway-internals.md:155`).

**Change (Checkpoint).** `rpc Checkpoint(CheckpointRequest) returns (stream CheckpointFrame)`. `CheckpointRequest` gains `string checkpoint_id = 3` (gateway-minted, per `spec/10:130`), `string chunk_encoding = 4`, `SlotId slot_id = 5`, and `int64 coordination_generation = 6`, and keeps `session_id` and `deadline_ms`.

**`CheckpointRequest` must carry `coordination_generation`, because the §10.1 fence the supersede's safety argument rests on does not reach this RPC today.** `spec/10:37` states that no window exists in which the old and the new coordinator can both issue accepted RPCs to the pod, and §2, S2, and C7 all rest the strictly-higher-generation supersede on that: a displaced coordinator's in-flight `Checkpoint` must stop at the pod when the fence acks. Two mechanisms are needed, and neither exists. The field on the request is the first, and it is what lets the adapter reject a displaced coordinator's *later* requests; the fence-driven cancellation of the stream that is *already running* is the second, which S3 states at `spec/10:36` and C1 builds, because an entry-time gate is evaluated once and cannot reach a stream that passed it before the fence advanced. `CheckpointRequest` carries no generation (`schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:1021-1024`), the handler's only identity check is `s.checkSession(sessionID)` (`pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:149`), and the generation gate lives on `CheckpointBarrier` alone (`pkg/adapter/coordination.go:143-159`); the client sends only `SessionId` and `DeadlineMs` (`pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient/client.go:466-471`), while `CheckpointBarrier` sends `CoordinationGeneration` (`:499-502`). The gap is inert today only because `Checkpoint` returns `codes.Unimplemented`; C1 and C2 make it the RPC that moves every checkpoint byte. The concrete harm is on the C7 termination path: the adapter serializes checkpoints behind one pod-global op-lock (`ops opLock`, `pkg/adapter/server.go:328`; `s.ops.Begin(ctx, opCheckpoint)` at `checkpoint.go:178`, returning `codes.Aborted` "checkpoint coalesced into an in-flight operation; retry" at `:180-186`), so a displaced coordinator's still-running `Checkpoint` stream would hold that lock and the new coordinator's eviction `Checkpoint` would either queue behind it, burning the slot's Stage 2 tier cap, or fail with `Aborted` and take the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback against a healthy object store and a quiescable pod. The adapter's other stale-coordinator defence cannot help: gap detection fires only when `new_generation > lastFenced + 1` while the handoff CAS bumps by exactly one (`coordination.go:91`), and the §10.1 in-flight-RPC cancellation is explicitly unimplemented (`coordination.go:62-64`). The field is therefore added and C1 validates it, which is the same gate `CheckpointBarrier` already carries rather than a new mechanism. The field alone is not sufficient, because it is checked at RPC entry and the victim's stream is in flight only because it already passed that check: S3 restates `spec/10:36` so an accepted `CoordinatorFence` cancels every in-flight RPC of a superseded generation before it acks, and C1 builds that cancellation on the same per-slot fence state, which is what actually releases the op-lock.

**`CheckpointRequest` must name a slot, because a checkpoint is per-slot and the pod-global identity cannot answer for one.** `spec/05:542` mandates per-slot checkpoints, C7 drives one `Checkpoint` stream per slot in slot-ID order, and the shipped message carries no slot (`schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:1021-1024`), so the contract is inexpressible on the wire as it stands. Two adapter-side consequences follow from the same gap and are fixed with it (C1). The handler gates on the pod-global session identity (`s.checkSession(sessionID)`, `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:149`), which the code documents as unreachable on a concurrent pod ("a concurrent pod sets only the per-slot st.sessionID, so checkSession below can never pass for it", `pkg/adapter/session.go:234-236`), so every `Checkpoint` against a `maxConcurrentSessions > 1` pod fails with `FailedPrecondition: pod has no assigned session` (`session.go:351-353`). And `checkpointRoots()` (`checkpoint.go:311-323`) archives the pod-global `s.WorkspaceRoot`, which on a concurrent pod is not the slot's tree. `SlotId` is the field every other slot-scoped message already carries (`StartSessionRequest` `:415`, `ShutdownRequest`, `AttachRequest`), so this reuses the existing convention rather than adding one. `CheckpointFrame` is a `oneof` of a mandatory leading `CheckpointStart { int64 probed_bytes = 1; }`, zero or more `bytes archive_chunk` frames, and a terminal `CheckpointSummary { int64 size_bytes = 1; }`. `probed_bytes` is the result of the adapter's existing pre-checkpoint workspace-size probe (`spec/04:255`), which the gateway needs before the first chunk `Put` to run the §11.2 storage-quota reservation; the gateway rejects a stream whose first frame is not `CheckpointStart`. `CheckpointResponse` (`:1026-1029`) is deleted, and the `checkpoint_id` field it carried is gone because the gateway already holds the value it minted.

`docs/api/internal.md` publishes the RPC signature twice, and both sites change. The message blocks at `:210-226` are regenerated from the corrected proto, and so is the `service RuntimeAdapter` block's entry at `:89-90` (`rpc Checkpoint(CheckpointRequest) returns (CheckpointResponse);`), which sits outside every other edited range and would otherwise keep advertising a unary RPC returning a message type the proto no longer has, on the one page whose stated audience is runtime adapter authors (`docs/api/internal.md:11`). It becomes `rpc Checkpoint(CheckpointRequest) returns (stream CheckpointFrame);` with a doc comment stating that the adapter archives the workspace and streams it to the gateway, which performs the object-store write. The page is authored rather than generated, so `make generate` does not correct it.

`chunk_encoding` travels on the request rather than on `CheckpointStart`, because the gateway commits it on the intent row before it opens the stream (`spec/10:130`, and on the barrier path the row is committed before the barrier is even dispatched), it is the suffix of every derived chunk key, and it is the decode selector on the restore, GC, supersede, and abort sweeps. `spec/10:139` states that "A fixed `chunk_encoding` is chosen once at intent-row INSERT time" and that "The suffix therefore reflects the actual on-disk payload", and an adapter-reported value arrives strictly after the row has been INSERTed and after keys have been derived from it, so it is either dead or authoritative too late. The gateway is the sole authority: it selects `tar.gz`, the only encoding `workspace.ArchiveTree` produces (`pkg/adapter/workspace/tree.go:45-57`, "ArchiveTree writes a gzip-compressed tar"), and the adapter MUST stream an archive in the encoding the request names or fail the stream. The `Checkpoint` RPC doc comment and `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md:67` state that obligation so a third-party adapter author cannot pick a different encoding.

The terminal `CheckpointSummary` is mandatory and is the completion gate: the gateway finalises `partial = false` only when the summary arrived inside the deadline and `workspace_bytes_uploaded` equals `CheckpointSummary.size_bytes` (S3, C2). The RPC doc comment and `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md:67` say so, because an adapter that ends the stream with an OK status after a truncated archive would otherwise produce a `partial = false` row over an incomplete chunk set.

**Change (Resume).** `rpc Resume(stream ResumeFrame) returns (ResumeResponse)`. `ResumeFrame` is a `oneof` of a mandatory first `ResumeStart` frame (the current `ResumeRequest` field set, including `checkpoint_id`, `expected_workspace_bytes`, `workspace_size_limit_bytes`, and `expected_workspace_root`, **plus `SlotId slot_id` and `int64 coordination_generation`**) and subsequent `bytes archive_chunk` frames. The generation travels on `ResumeStart` for the same reason it travels on `CheckpointRequest`: `spec/10:37` makes the fence a precondition of every gateway→pod RPC, and a restore driven by a displaced coordinator would otherwise overwrite a workspace the new coordinator owns. The adapter validates it exactly as it validates the fence today, so a slot whose fence state is uninitialized (a fresh replacement pod, which is the dominant resume case) accepts the first generation it is handed and a slot already fenced at a higher generation rejects the stale one. `ResumeResponse` is unchanged. The adapter rejects a stream whose first frame is not `ResumeStart` with `InvalidArgument`, and rejects a second `ResumeStart` the same way.

**`ResumeStart` must name a slot for the same reason `CheckpointRequest` must.** The shipped `ResumeRequest` carries no slot (`schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:1048-1107`) and the handler is pod-global on both counts: it calls `s.claimSession(sessionID)` (`pkg/adapter/resume.go:49`), which sets the pod-global `s.sessionID` and returns `codes.Unavailable, "pod is not idle: session %s is already assigned"` for a second slot's resume (`pkg/adapter/session.go:363-368`), and it extracts against the pod-global `s.WorkspaceRoot` (`resume.go:40`), which it also compares against `expected_workspace_root` and rejects on mismatch (`:63`). The adapter has no session-to-slot reverse lookup: `pkg/adapter/slot.go` exposes `useSlot(slotID)`, `resolveSlotPaths(slotID)`, and `workspaceRootForSlot(slotID)`, all keyed on a caller-supplied slot. Without the field, the staged `spec/10:155` step (4) rule that extraction stages beside the **resolved** workspace root (`/workspace/slots/{slotId}/current.partial` on a concurrent pool, S2) is unsatisfiable on the wire, the §6 tier-1 staging assertion cannot be implemented, and the whole per-slot machinery this change builds (the `slot_id` in the `checkpoint_manifest` key, `SelectForResume(tenant, session, slot)`, C7's serialized per-slot eviction checkpoints) writes per-slot checkpoints that no restore path can consume. The gateway already holds the value it must send: `resumeOnPod` calls `SelectForResume(tenantID, sessionID, slotID)` (C4). The slot therefore travels on `ResumeStart`, on `podsession.ResumeRequest`, and on `adapterclient`'s resume parameters, and C1 routes `Resume` through the same `useSlot(slotID)` / `checkSlotSession(sessionID, slotID)` / `workspaceRootForSlot(slotID)` pattern it applies to `Checkpoint`, replacing the pod-global `claimSession` and `s.WorkspaceRoot` on the concurrent-pod branch, including the `expected_workspace_root` comparison, which is made against the resolved slot root.

**Change (CheckpointBarrier and its ack).** `CheckpointBarrierResponse.checkpoint_ref` (`:1177`) currently documents "the storage reference of the best-effort checkpoint flushed during the barrier (empty when ... checkpoint sink unwired)". The pod can no longer mint a storage reference, and the doc comment cites the interface C1 deletes. `CheckpointBarrierRequest` gains `string checkpoint_id`: the gateway mints the id and commits the intent row for the session before it dispatches the barrier, so the ack has something to correlate against and the `chunk_count == 0` arm at `spec/10:173` (row present, no chunks, soft-delete and fall through) covers a pod that never acks. `checkpoint_ref` is redefined as that gateway-minted `checkpoint_id` echoed back for correlation, and the doc comments at `:1169-1172` and `:136-141` are rewritten so the adapter quiesces and acknowledges immediately while the gateway drives the flush. A new RPC is not added: one field on the existing barrier message carries the correlation. Update the §4.7 `CheckpointBarrierAck` event-field row (`spec/04:686`) to match.

**Change (CoordinatorFence and CheckpointBarrier become slot-aware).** The §10.1 handoff C7 runs for every session on a terminating pod cannot complete on a concurrent-session pod as the fence stands, so the fence is fixed in the same change rather than assumed. `Server.CoordinatorFence` gates on the pod-global `checkSession(sessionID)` (`pkg/adapter/coordination.go:72`), which never passes on a concurrent pod (`pkg/adapter/session.go:234-236`), so every fence returns `FailedPrecondition` before it reaches the generation check and, by C7's own failed-slot rule, every slot of every terminating concurrent pod would take the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback, which is the outcome `leasestore.Steal` was added to prevent. `CoordinatorFenceRequest` (`:1129-1135`) also carries no slot, and the adapter's fence state is a single pod-global `lastFenced` requiring strictly increasing generations (`coordination.go:26-34`, `:82-89`) while `coordination_generation` is a per-session counter, so even a slot-aware fence would reject the second and later sessions of a pod whose sessions sit at equal or lower generations.

`CoordinatorFenceRequest` and `CheckpointBarrierRequest` therefore gain `SlotId slot_id`, and C1 routes `CoordinatorFence` (`coordination.go:72`) and `CheckpointBarrier` (`coordination.go:137`) through the `useSlot(slotID)` / `checkSlotSession(sessionID, slotID)` pattern the adapter already uses on every other slot-scoped RPC (`pkg/adapter/attach.go:42`, `pkg/adapter/session.go:186`, `checkSlotSession` at `pkg/adapter/slotsession.go:116`). `lastFenced` and `initialized` move from the pod-global `coordinationState` onto the per-slot state, so each session's generation is fenced independently and the pod-global state stays the single-session path. The §10.1 handoff protocol text gains one sentence stating that on a concurrent-session pod the fence is per `(session_id, slot_id)` (S3's `spec/10:30-37` edit). `pkg/adapter/coordination.go` is in C1's and C7's target lists for this.

**The fence's sender path carries the slot too, and it is a separate file family from the barrier's.** A `slot_id` on `CoordinatorFenceRequest` that no caller populates leaves the field empty on the wire, and `useSlot` is exactly `slotID != ""` (`pkg/adapter/slot.go:39-41`), so every fence would take the adapter's pod-global branch, land on `checkSession(sessionID)`, and fail on a concurrent pod for the reason the paragraph above gives; the per-slot `lastFenced` state C1 introduces would never be written, and C7's handoff would drop every slot of every terminating concurrent pod to the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback. The barrier's sender is already staged end to end (`Client.CheckpointBarrier` in `client.go`, fed by `barrier.Target.SlotID`, C2); the fence's is not, and it lives in three files that no list named. `adapterclient.Client.CoordinatorFence` (`pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient/coordinatorfence.go:48-54`) builds the request with `SessionId` and `CoordinationGeneration` alone; `coordfence.FenceClient` (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordfence/coordfence.go:62-64`) and `Fencer.Fence` / `Fencer.fence` (`:137`, `:143`) carry no slot; and the two gateway callers, `sessionserver.CoordinationFencer` with `fenceResumedPod` (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/sessionserver.go:822`, `start.go:3176-3180`), pass none. All three widen to carry the session's slot, which the callers already hold (`resumeOnPod`'s selected row, and the `ListActiveByPod` row on the C7 handler), and `Client.CoordinatorFence` stamps it on `CoordinatorFenceRequest.slot_id`. The value is empty on a single-session pool, which is the same convention every other slot-scoped message uses and which keeps `useSlot` on the pod-global path there. `pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient/coordinatorfence.go`, `pkg/gateway/coordination/coordfence/coordfence.go`, and `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/sessionserver.go` with `start.go` are in C1's and C7's target lists for this, and the two shipped tests that implement the pre-widening signatures (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordfence/coordfence_test.go:24`, `fakeFenceClient`, and `pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient/coordinatorfence_test.go`) are in §10's rewrite ledger.

**Change (WorkspaceSnapshot.Ref contract).** With `CheckpointResponse` gone, `sessions.WorkspaceSnapshot.Ref` (assigned `result.CheckpointID` at `pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointer/checkpointer.go:265`) becomes the `object_key_prefix`, and `checkpointretention.Record.Ref` (`pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointretention/checkpointretention.go:54-58`) likewise. Both doc comments stop calling it a MinIO object reference. Three readers assume a single object and must be reworked in the same change, because a prefix cannot be addressed as one key:

- `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/derive.go:318`, `:326`, `:375` mints a fresh prefix (`derivedSnapshotRef`) and copies the parent snapshot with a single-object `blobstore.Copier`. Because chunk keys are derivable only from a `checkpoint_manifest` row keyed on `(tenant_id, checkpoint_id)`, a derived prefix with no manifest row behind it has no chunk count, no encoding, and no way to enumerate. Derive therefore resolves the parent's manifest row, mints a new `checkpoint_id` for the derived session, copies each chunk of the parent's derived key set to the new prefix, and inserts a `partial = false` `checkpoint_manifest` row for the derived session (copying `chunk_count`, `chunk_size_bytes`, `chunk_encoding`, and `workspace_bytes_uploaded`) in the same transaction as the copy. `DERIVE_SNAPSHOT_UNAVAILABLE` stays the error for a missing chunk, and its two client-facing definitions are restated on the chunk-set model in the same edit. `spec/15:1056` defines the code as "the referenced workspace snapshot **object** was not found in object storage" and defines the detail field as "`details.snapshotRef` includes the missing object path", and `docs/reference/error-catalog.md:150` mirrors it. After P1 the ref is a prefix that names no object, and the missing artifact is one chunk of the parent's derived key set, so both rows are restated: derive failed because a chunk of the referenced checkpoint could not be read from object storage. The emitter (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/derive.go:343`, `map[string]any{"snapshotRef": source.WorkspaceSnapshot.Ref}`) keeps putting the parent checkpoint's `object_key_prefix` in `details.snapshotRef`, and both rows describe the field as that prefix rather than as the path of the missing object. The code, its class, and its 503 status are unchanged.
- `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/replay.go:117`, `:178`.
- `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/session_subresources.go:283-294` with `parseSnapshotRef` at `:338-357`, which serves `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace` ("Download workspace snapshot (tar.gz)", `spec/15_external-api-surface.md:671`). The endpoint resolves the manifest row, concatenates the derived chunk keys in ascending index order, and streams the result.

Five spec passages define the derive copy itself as a single-object copy and are restated in the same edit, because an implementer following them would copy one object and leave the derived prefix with no manifest row, which is the unenumerable state above and which breaks `POST /v1/sessions/{id}/derive` and the derived session's `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/workspace`.

- `spec/07:81` ("Derive copy semantics") has derive "read the parent's MinIO object and write a new independent object under the derived session's own path". Restate it as the chunk-set copy: derive resolves the parent's `checkpoint_manifest` row, copies every chunk in `[0, chunk_count)` from the parent's `object_key_prefix` into a new prefix under the derived session's own path (`/{tenant_id}/{object_type}/{new_session_id}/...`), and inserts the derived session's `partial = false` manifest row in the same transaction, so the derived prefix is enumerable. The no-sharing rule, the `parent_workspace_ref` lineage-pointer rule, and the GC argument are unchanged.
- `spec/07:92` argues derive-lock safety on the reference resolving to "a stable, immutable MinIO object key that cannot be mutated by any concurrent checkpoint", and its partial-copy rule deletes "any partially written destination object". Restate both on the (prefix + manifest row) pair: the reference resolves to an immutable chunk set named by a manifest row that no concurrent checkpoint mutates (a new attempt mints a new `checkpoint_id` and a new prefix), and a failed copy deletes every chunk it wrote for the derived session and the derived manifest row.
- `spec/12:331` ("No reference counting required in v1") and `spec/12:938` ("Workspace deduplication") both state that derive "always copies the parent's workspace snapshot into a new session-scoped MinIO object". Restate the no-sharing argument per chunk object: every chunk object is owned by exactly one session, and derive copies each of the parent's chunks into a new session-scoped object, so no chunk object is shared across sessions.
- `spec/04:312` ("Workspace lineage") has the gateway copy "the parent's workspace snapshot bytes into a new MinIO object scoped to the derived session's own path (`/{tenant_id}/{object_type}/{new_session_id}/...`)". This is the fifth statement of the single-object copy, and it sits in §4.5 rather than in §7.1 or §12.5, so the three edits above do not reach it. Restate it as the same chunk-set copy: the gateway resolves the parent's `checkpoint_manifest` row, copies every chunk in `[0, chunk_count)` from the parent's `object_key_prefix` into a new prefix under the derived session's own path, and inserts the derived session's `partial = false` manifest row in the same transaction, so the derived session owns its chunk objects independently. The paragraph's opening clause ("Each workspace snapshot is immutable and identified by a content-addressed hash (SHA-256 of the tar archive)") is restated in the same edit, because a checkpoint is a chunk set rather than one tar object: a checkpoint snapshot is immutable and is identified by its gateway-minted `checkpoint_id`, from which the `object_key_prefix` and every chunk key derive. The `parent_workspace_ref` lineage-pointer rule, the independent-GC argument, and the lineage-query rationale are unchanged.

A fifth site addresses a checkpoint as one object, in the spec rather than in code. `spec/07:212` sets a terminal session's `final_workspace_ref` to "this checkpoint's object key", and `spec/07:222` repeats the assignment for the pre-attach collapse. After S2 no checkpoint has an object key: it is a set of independently committed chunk objects addressed through the manifest row's `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding`. Both sentences are restated so `final_workspace_ref` carries the checkpoint's `object_key_prefix`, whose chunk keys are derivable from the `checkpoint_manifest` row, and `workspaceSnapshotSource` stays `"checkpoint"`. `spec/07:212` sits two lines above the sentence S4 rewrites at `:214`, so leaving it would put the new chunk-set model and the deleted single-object model in one numbered list.

The invariant these readers rest on: every readable `WorkspaceSnapshot.Ref` prefix has a `checkpoint_manifest` row behind it, so `chunk_count` and `chunk_encoding` are always available to the reader. C2 states the same invariant on the write side.

**Change (NotifyPodTerminating on the existing GatewayControl service).** The agent-pod eviction trigger needs one message from the pod to the gateway, and `GatewayControl` (`schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:215`) is the seam it belongs on: it is the adapter→gateway service, it exists for exactly this class of call ("decisions the pod cannot make itself"), the adapter already holds the client, and the call travels on TCP 50051, which `allow-pod-egress-base` already permits. No new service, no new port, and no pod RBAC. On the gateway the RPC lands on the one registered `GatewayControlServer`, which is `leasecontrol.Service` (`cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go:700`), so the handler is a method on that type rather than a free-standing component (C7).

```protobuf
// NotifyPodTerminating tells the gateway that this pod has entered its
// termination grace period, so the gateway can take a §4.4 eviction
// checkpoint for every session bound to the pod before the pod exits.
// The adapter sends it from its SIGTERM path, before it stops serving,
// and blocks on the response: the gateway drives the checkpoints back
// over this pod's own Adapter.Checkpoint stream, which is only reachable
// while the adapter's gRPC server is still up. spec: §4.6.1.
rpc NotifyPodTerminating(NotifyPodTerminatingRequest)
    returns (NotifyPodTerminatingResponse) {}

message NotifyPodTerminatingRequest {
  // pod_name identifies the terminating pod. It is the value the adapter
  // already reads from its Downward API POD_NAME env, and it is the key
  // the gateway resolves the bound sessions on (sessions.pod_assignment).
  // The gateway resolves those sessions itself rather than trusting a
  // session list from the pod. The namespace is not carried: agent pods
  // live in the agent namespace the gateway is already configured with.
  string pod_name = 1;
  // budget_ms is the wall-clock the adapter will actually hold the pod
  // open, and it is the deadline the adapter applies to this call:
  // terminationGracePeriodSeconds, minus preStopDrainMarginSeconds (the
  // kubelet's reaping margin after the adapter exits), minus the
  // demote-and-exit reserve the §6.1 teardown runs after this call
  // returns (LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s), minus what the adapter
  // has already spent since SIGTERM. It is an upper bound the gateway
  // MUST NOT exceed; the gateway applies its own §10.1 Stage 2 tier cap
  // per slot inside it. Every term is subtracted before the value is
  // declared, so the ceiling the gateway is handed is the wall clock it
  // gets: the adapter does not abandon the call earlier than budget_ms.
  int64 budget_ms = 2;
}

message NotifyPodTerminatingResponse {
  // slots_checkpointed and slots_failed report what the gateway achieved,
  // for the adapter to log. The adapter takes no action on either: the
  // gateway owns the fallback.
  int32 slots_checkpointed = 1;
  int32 slots_failed = 2;
}
```

The gateway is the authority on which sessions are bound to the pod, which is why the request carries no session list: a pod that under-reported its slots could otherwise skip a slot's checkpoint. The gateway resolves them from `sessions.pod_assignment`, the column the `idx_sessions_active_by_pod` partial index already serves for `GetActiveSlotsByPod` (`migrations/0080_sessions_active_by_pod_index.up.sql`, `pkg/gateway/session/sessionstore/pgstore/pgstore.go:692`), extended with a `ListActiveByPod` selector that returns the rows rather than their count (C7). The process-local `podsession.Registry` is not the source, because the RPC lands on an arbitrary replica.

`budget_ms` travels from the pod because the pod is the party that knows how much of its grace period the SIGTERM path has already spent; the gateway treats it as a ceiling and never as a floor. The adapter subtracts every serial claim on the grace period before it declares the value (the kubelet's drain margin, the demote-and-exit reserve, and the elapsed time since SIGTERM) and then applies the declared value as its own RPC deadline, so the declared ceiling and the adapter's actual abandonment point are one number. A `budget_ms` computed as `grace − preStopDrainMarginSeconds − elapsed` while the adapter cancelled the call a demote reserve earlier would hand the gateway a window it never gets, and a gateway that planned its serialized slot loop against the declared ceiling would be cut before its last slot's Postgres fallback completed. The S3 floor sizes `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` so that the value that survives all three subtractions still covers `maxConcurrentSessions × (max(cap, 90) + postgresFallbackBudgetSeconds)` on a session-mode pool, which is the only class whose terminating pod runs a checkpoint at all. The adapter is given the grace period itself the same way the gateway pod is: the podspec renders `LENNY_TERMINATION_GRACE_SECONDS` onto the adapter container, mirroring `charts/lenny/templates/gateway-deployment.yaml:742-743` and the value `cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go:2500` already reads for the gateway's own preStop budget (C7). Without it the adapter has no source for the value: the pod's containers carry only `POD_NAME` from the Downward API (`podspec.go:551`, `:638`, `:804-816`), `automountServiceAccountToken` is false (`:890`), and the computed preStop timeout is passed only as `--timeout` to the short-lived `lenny-adapter prestop` exec (`podspec.go:1230`), whose contract with the adapter stays "signal it, then wait for it to exit".

The response is advisory. The adapter proceeds to its teardown whatever it says, because the pod is terminating regardless and the gateway has already recorded the outcome, written the minimal-state fallback where it applies, and published `session.lost` where it does not.

Who blocks on what, end to end: the kubelet holds each container's `SIGTERM` until that container's preStop hook returns; `lenny-adapter prestop` does not return until the adapter process exits or its timeout elapses; the adapter does not begin its teardown until `NotifyPodTerminating` returns or its own deadline fires; the gateway does not return until every slot's checkpoint has settled or `budget_ms` has elapsed. Every link is bounded, and the outermost bound is `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`, which the kubelet enforces with SIGKILL. The §4.7 contract table gains the RPC row, and `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md` gains its published mirror.

**Change (ResumeResponse.mode stops being the adapter's classification).** `ResumeResponse` (`:1108-1122`) documents `mode` as the adapter reporting "`full` when the named checkpoint was extracted intact, `partial_workspace` when only a partial manifest was available". The adapter cannot make that judgment once the gateway fetches the chunks: it sees one concatenated byte stream and does not know whether the chunk set was complete, which row was selected, or what the recovery threshold was. `restored_bytes` stays, and it is the numerator the gateway divides by the frozen `baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes` (C2). `mode` is redefined as the adapter's report of what it did with the stream it was given (`extracted` when the decompress-then-untar pipeline committed onto the workspace root, `failed` when it did not), and the §7.2 `resumeMode` classification becomes the gateway's, sourced from the restore driver's `RestoreMode` (C2, C6). `ResumeRequest.checkpoint_id` (`:1056`) stops being a pointer the adapter dereferences and becomes correlation only; its doc comment says so.

**Change (`checkpoint_id` provenance, one format, one minter).** The repository states three different origins and three different formats for `checkpoint_id`, and the gateway becoming the sole minter (S2, `spec/10:130`) is what settles all three.

- `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:1021-1029` has `CheckpointRequest` carry no `checkpoint_id` at all and `CheckpointResponse` return one, so the adapter mints it. The Checkpoint change above inverts this: the id travels down on `CheckpointRequest` and `CheckpointResponse` is deleted.
- `docs/api/internal.md:210-214` already documents `CheckpointRequest.checkpoint_id` as "gateway-assigned", which the proto contradicts today. The doc is right about the minter and stale in every other respect: it declares a `consistency` field on the request and `bool success` / `error_code` / `error_message` on the response (`:220-226`) that the proto has never had. Regenerate the whole block from the corrected proto rather than patching the minter line, and do the same for the three lifecycle frames at `:331-360`, whose headings ("checkpoint_request (gateway to runtime)", "checkpoint_complete (gateway to runtime)") contradict the schema's own "Adapter → runtime" framing (`schemas/lifecycle-events.schema.json:68`). The lifecycle channel is intra-pod: the adapter sends these frames to the runtime, carrying the gateway's id. State that.
- The id format appears as a ULID (`schemas/examples/lifecycle.checkpoint_request.json:3`, `ckpt_01HX9F0YWXKK0V7QZ7G6P3R5JM`), as `chk_42` (`docs/reference/adapter-contract.md:540`, `:552`, `:627-633`; `docs/runtime-author-guide/lifecycle.md:183-198`; and `docs/runtime-author-guide/integration-levels.md:156`, `:165`, `:170`, whose three lifecycle frames carry the same value and which S1 already rewrites at `:145-175`, so both edits land in one pass), and as a UUID (`spec/10:130`, "the gateway mints the `checkpoint_id` UUID"). The UUID is the one the manifest row's primary key carries (C4) and the one that appears in every derived chunk key, so it is authoritative. Restate the three `schemas/examples/` files and the three doc pages on a UUID example. The lifecycle-event schema's `checkpointId` stays `{"type": "string", "minLength": 1}` and gains no format constraint, because it is an opaque relay value to the runtime; only the examples change.
- `schemas/lifecycle-events.schema.json:68-77` describes `checkpoint_request` as "Adapter → runtime. Asks the runtime to quiesce so the adapter can take a consistent snapshot". The direction is right and the reason is wrong: the adapter quiesces the runtime so it can archive a consistent tree and stream it to the gateway. Restate the description, and restate `checkpoint_complete` (`:80-101`, "Confirms the snapshot upload result") as the archive-capture result (S1). `checkpoint_ready` is unchanged.
- `spec/15:2291-2300` (the §15.4.3 runtime-author pseudocode) echoes `checkpointId` back on `checkpoint_ready` and asserts `checkpoint_complete`. The handshake is unchanged and the pseudocode is unchanged; it is listed because the tier-11 assertion compares the id's stated origin across it.

**Change (the runtime SDKs are confirmed unchanged).** The `checkpoint` capability stays a runtime capability meaning quiesce-and-acknowledge, and the SDK surface is unchanged in every language: `onCheckpoint(checkpointID)` fires on `checkpoint_request`, the SDK replies `checkpoint_ready`, and the adapter signals `checkpoint_complete` (`sdks/runtime/go/runtime/lifecycle.go:65-70`, `:191-213`; `sdks/runtime/typescript/src/lifecycle.ts:35-37`, `:176-190`; `sdks/runtime/python/lenny_runtime/lifecycle.py:51-53`, `:192-202`). Their doc comments already describe the handshake as quiesce-only and name no snapshot, no upload, and no artifact store: the Go comment reads "The callback quiesces runtime output; a non-nil error is logged and the SDK still replies so the adapter is not left waiting" (`lifecycle.go:65-70`), and the TypeScript and Python comments carry the same wording. None of the three contains an object-store client, a `PutObject`, or an upload helper, which is what makes `spec/15:2531` (S1) a spec-only claim and what makes the tier-10 and tier-11 no-object-store-client assertions pass. No file under `sdks/runtime/` is edited by this proposal. The three **client** SDKs are edited, in one field each: their session types gain the `failureReason` the §15.1 envelope gains (C2).

**Change (spec/04 §4.7 contract table and the published adapter contract).** The `Checkpoint` row (`:655`, "Export recoverable session state") and the `Resume` row (`:661`, "Restore from checkpoint on a replacement pod") both stop being unary. Reword `:655` so the adapter streams the workspace archive to the gateway, which owns the object-store write, and `:661` so the gateway streams the checkpoint chunks to the adapter, which extracts them. The `CheckpointBarrier` row (`:656`) is the normative source `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md:68` mirrors, and it carries the same "flushes a best-effort checkpoint" mandate, which the adapter can no longer satisfy once C1 removes the adapter-side flush. Reword it so the adapter validates `coordination_generation`, quiesces tool-call dispatch, and replies via `CheckpointBarrierAck` carrying the gateway-minted `checkpoint_id`, with the gateway driving the flush over the `Checkpoint` stream. Its single wall-clock `checkpointBarrierAckTimeoutSeconds` deadline across all coordinated pods is unchanged.

**Anchor and change (spec/04:672, the operation lock is pod-global).** The paragraph states that "The adapter maintains a **per-session** operation lock that serializes `Checkpoint` and `Interrupt` RPCs", and the shipped lock is one `ops opLock` field on `Server` (`pkg/adapter/server.go:328`), which is the pod rather than the session: it admits one running operation plus one queued and coalesces or rejects everything beyond that (`pkg/adapter/oplock.go:37-40`), which `Checkpoint` maps to `codes.Aborted` (`pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:178-186`). The disagreement is inert today because `Checkpoint` returns `codes.Unimplemented`, and this change makes it load-bearing: every argument P1, C1, and C7 make about a displaced coordinator's stream holding the lock against the new coordinator's checkpoint reads it as pod-global, and the gateway-side serialization S1 states at `spec/05:542` and C2 and C7 implement is what keeps two slots of one pod from contending for it. The spec is the party that is wrong, and a per-slot lock inside the adapter would be a second serializer for a concern the gateway already owns. Restate `:672` so the lock is **pod-global**: the adapter serializes `Checkpoint` and `Interrupt` across the whole pod, a second operation is queued and a third is coalesced or rejected, and the gateway drives at most one slot's `Checkpoint` per pod at a time (`spec/05:542`), so the slots of a concurrent-session pod never contend for it. The Interrupt-during-checkpoint and checkpoint-during-interrupt ordering bullets keep their semantics, which the pod-global reading only widens from one session to the pod.

`docs/reference/adapter-contract.md` is the published mirror of that table and of the lifecycle-channel event table, and it is the page adapter authors implement against, so the same four rows are rewritten there: `:67` (`Checkpoint`) and `:73` (`Resume`) to the streaming directions above, `:68` (`CheckpointBarrier`, which today says the adapter "flushes a best-effort checkpoint") and `:96` (`CheckpointBarrierAck`, "after quiescence and checkpoint flush") to quiesce-and-acknowledge with a gateway-driven flush and a `checkpoint_ref` carrying the gateway-minted `checkpoint_id`. Left as they are, an adapter author would implement exactly the adapter-side flush C1 removes.

The same page carries the operation lock's published mirror, and it is corrected in the same edit. `docs/reference/adapter-contract.md:86` states the retired contract verbatim ("**Checkpoint and Interrupt are mutually exclusive.** The adapter maintains a per-session operation lock. Only one of these operations may execute at a time; the other is queued until the first completes."), and it sits outside the four rows above. Leaving it would apply the proposal with the normative spec saying the lock is pod-global and the page third-party adapter authors build against saying it is per-session, which is the spec-versus-docs disagreement S1 refuses to create at `spec/16:44` and `docs/reference/metrics.md:40` (`:177`). The disagreement is load-bearing: an author who builds the per-session lock the page still advertises admits one concurrent `Checkpoint` stream per slot, which is the state the gateway-side serialization exists to prevent and which every displaced-coordinator argument in P1, C1, and C7 reads as impossible. Restate `:86` in the staged `spec/04:672` terms: the adapter maintains one pod-global operation lock that serializes `Checkpoint` and `Interrupt` across every slot of the pod, and the gateway drives at most one slot's `Checkpoint` per pod at a time in slot-ID order (`spec/05:542`), so two slots never contend for it.

### C1. Implement the adapter's streaming Checkpoint and Resume handlers and delete the nil-sink interface family

**Target:** `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go` (the `CheckpointSink` / `CheckpointAborter` interfaces `:20-48`; the pod-global session gate `:149`; the `Unimplemented` branch `:161-168`; `checkpointRoots` `:311-323`; `archiveAndStore` `:325-358`; `runAbortCleanup` `:297-298`; `recordStorageFailure` `:380-400`); `pkg/adapter/resume.go` (the `CheckpointSource` interface `:19-24`; the `Unimplemented` branch `:44-47`; the pod-global `claimSession` gate `:49`, which returns `codes.Unavailable` for a second slot's resume; the pod-global `s.WorkspaceRoot` extraction target and its `expected_workspace_root` comparison `:40`, `:63`); `pkg/adapter/server.go` (`:214-220`, `:232-245`, `:257-259`); `pkg/adapter/slot.go` (`useSlot` `:39`, `workspaceRootForSlot` `:119`) and `pkg/adapter/slotsession.go` (`checkSlotSession` `:116`), which the checkpoint, resume, fence, and barrier handlers now route through; `pkg/adapter/coordination.go` (`CoordinatorFence`'s session gate `:72`, the pod-global `coordinationState` `:26-34`, its unimplemented-cancellation comment `:62-64` and `:92-95`, `CheckpointBarrier`'s session gate `:137`, `flushBarrierCheckpoint` `:193-222`);
