**Change (`PartialCleaner`'s row predicate).** `PartialCleaner` is already wired in production (`cmd/lenny-gateway/sessionsrv.go:217`) and `CleanupAfterResume` runs on **every** resume (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:2786`). It selects its row with `LatestActive` (`partialcleanup.go:149`), whose SQL predicate is `deleted_at IS NULL` alone (`pgstore.go:165-171`), and then hands it to `CleanupPartialManifest`, which deletes the chunks (`partialcleanup.go:94`) and soft-deletes the row (`:101`). Once the unification puts `partial = false` rows in the same table, `LatestActive` returns the session's newest **full** checkpoint, and filling the nil `Deleter` with the C2 per-key delete surface would then make every resume delete every chunk of that full checkpoint and soft-delete its manifest row. C2's `partial = false` read-only rule lives in the new restore driver and does not cover this separate, pre-wired path. `CleanupAfterResume` therefore selects through the `partial = TRUE AND deleted_at IS NULL` predicate (a `LatestActivePartial` selector), no-ops when no active partial row exists, and `CleanupPartialManifest` refuses a row whose `partial` is false. `LatestActive` is re-scoped to `partial = TRUE` rather than kept alongside it, so no caller can reach the wrong predicate.

**Change (the backstop sweep and its selector).** The §12.5 backstop sweep has no implementation today. The only partial-manifest worker is the leader-gated tombstone hard-prune (`cmd/lenny-gateway/workers.go:803-841`, calling `hardPrunePartialManifests` at `:831`), which reads through `ListSoftDeletedBefore` (`partialmanifeststore.go:140`) on the predicate `deleted_at < cutoff`, the complement of what the backstop needs, and physically removes the row without issuing an object-store call. No sweep over `partial = true` rows exists anywhere in `pkg/` or `cmd/`. That is load-bearing after this change rather than a latent gap: the `partial = true` intent row is the first durable act of every checkpoint of every trigger, prefix listing is gone, and a chunk is named only by its manifest row, so crash-abandoned rows and chunks are routine and the hard-prune destroys the only name a chunk has once `gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds` elapses. Three of the four orphan invariants in §3 and the residual-window closure in S2 rest on this sweep, and three of the §6 assertions exercise it, so it is built here rather than assumed.

The `Store` interface, `MemoryStore`, and `pgstore` gain `ListSweepable(ctx, tombstoneCutoff, deadlineCutoff time.Time) ([]Record, error)`, returning every `partial = TRUE` row whose `deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > tombstoneCutoff` and whose `checkpoint_timeout_at < deadlineCutoff`, with `created_at` on the returned `Record`. The two cutoffs are what make the sweep reach a chunk committed under a soft-deleted row while leaving an in-flight seal or periodic attempt alone (S2).

**Entry condition (b) is a predicate on the manifest row, not on session state.** `spec/12:328` states the two conditions as "(a) the session has reached a terminal state, or (b) the row's `created_at` timestamp is older than `maxResumeWindowSeconds`", and `spec/12:349` (GC concurrency rule 6) writes the same predicate as `partial = true AND (terminal_state OR created_at < now() - maxResumeWindowSeconds) AND deleted_at IS NULL`. Only (a) is session state. Reading (b) as one too, and evaluating both through a join against the session record, would collapse the sweep to arm (a): a live, healthy session has no expired resume window, so no crash-abandoned intent row and no supersede-soft-deleted row of a still-running session would ever be swept, and the §12.5 tombstone hard-prune would then remove those soft-deleted rows after `gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds` and destroy the `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` needed to derive their chunk keys. That is exactly the permanent orphan S2's widened predicate exists to prevent, and supersedes happen only on live sessions, so the widened arm would be inert for the case it was added for. `ListSweepable` therefore evaluates (b) itself off the row's `created_at`, and the session-record join is scoped to arm (a) alone.

The caller is a leader-gated sweep goroutine registered alongside the existing `tombstone-hard-prune` entry in the same `leaderGate` block (`cmd/lenny-gateway/workers.go:803-841`), running on the existing `gc.cycleIntervalSeconds` ticker rather than on a new knob. Each cycle calls `ListSweepable(now − gc.tombstoneRetentionSeconds, now − gc.cycleIntervalSeconds)` and evaluates the two §12.5 entry conditions on each returned row. Condition (b), "the row's `created_at` timestamp is older than `maxResumeWindowSeconds`" (`spec/12:328`, `spec/12:349`), is read straight off the `Record` and needs no session read; it is the arm that reclaims a crash-abandoned intent row and a supersede-soft-deleted row of a session that is still running. Condition (a), "the session has reached a terminal state", is the only one that needs a session-record join, and the sweep performs that join only for rows that fail (b). For each row that satisfies either condition the sweep derives the chunk keys from `object_key_prefix`, `chunk_count`, and `chunk_encoding` and issues a per-key `SoftDelete` for every index in `[0, chunk_count)` through the tenant-scoped `ArtifactStore` (the C2 delete surface), then soft-deletes the row under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard. On a row selected through the widened soft-deleted arm that terminating `UPDATE` affects zero rows and the pass is chunk-delete-only, which is idempotent because a soft-delete on an already-tombstoned or missing object is a no-op. `ListSweepable` deliberately does not evaluate either entry condition: (a) is session state the store does not hold, and (b) is a row predicate the sweep evaluates against its own clock so the store surface carries no time-of-day policy. A failed chunk delete increments `lenny_checkpoint_orphaned_objects_total` and leaves the row for the next cycle.

**Change (the resume selectors).** The `Store` interface, `MemoryStore`, and `pgstore` gain two methods. `SelectForResume(ctx, tenantID, sessionID, slotID string) (Record, error)` is the single `spec/10:155` entry-condition selector the C2 restore path reads. It returns the newest active row for `(tenant_id, session_id, slot_id)` under `deleted_at IS NULL` in the total order `(coordination_generation DESC, checkpoint_started_at DESC, checkpoint_id DESC)`, with the S2-restated fence applied on the same order: a `partial = false` row wins over the highest active `partial = true` row only when it is strictly newer in that order. It also applies the §10.1 restorability gates to a winning partial (`chunk_count > 0`, `workspace_bytes_uploaded > 0`, and `workspace_bytes_uploaded >= baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes * gateway.partialRecoveryThresholdFraction` when the baseline is non-NULL), and returns the newest active `partial = false` row instead when the partial fails them, which is the fallback `spec/10:155` mandates. It returns `ErrNotFound` only when the session has neither a restorable partial nor an active full row. A selector that returned the non-restorable partial and left the caller to branch would route the resume to the snapshotless rebuild while a live full checkpoint sat active in the table, because the intent row of every in-flight or crash-abandoned attempt is a `partial = true` row that outranks the session's last full row under a stable coordinator (`spec/04:157`).

`SelectLatestFullBefore(ctx, tenantID, sessionID, slotID, excludeCheckpointID string) (Record, error)` returns the newest active `partial = false` row for the same key that is **strictly below** `excludeCheckpointID` in the same total order, and `ErrNotFound` when none exists. It is the fallback target `spec/10:155` names on the reassembly-failure path: the restore driver calls it when the row it was handed fails contiguity or its reassembly fails, so the session restores the next full checkpoint down rather than failing the resume. The exclusion is load-bearing rather than defensive: S2 makes a `partial = false` row a chunk set that can itself fail contiguity, and `SelectForResume` returns such a row directly in the dominant case, so a selector that returned the newest full row unconditionally would hand the driver back the row it just failed on, leave the second retained checkpoint of the latest-2 rotation unreachable, and never terminate (C2). Without the selector at all, the driver holds one `checkpoint_id` and has no way to reach any other row. An "at or above" comparison on `coordination_generation` alone would be satisfied by equality, and since the counter advances only on coordinator handoff (`spec/04:157`) a session's last full checkpoint and a later truncated partial normally carry the same generation, so the older full row would outrank every partial the change produces and the partial-reassembly path would be unreachable under a stable coordinator. The existing `LatestActive` (`partialmanifeststore.go:125`) selects on `deleted_at IS NULL` alone and admits no slot, so once `partial = false` rows share the table it would return a full checkpoint to a caller asking for a partial and would key on the wrong tuple; it is re-scoped to `partial = TRUE` for `PartialCleaner` and `SelectForResume` carries the restore predicate. `SelectForResume` is what the `(tenant_id, session_id, slot_id, coordination_generation DESC, checkpoint_started_at DESC)` index above exists to serve.

`SelectForResume` has exactly one caller, `resumeOnPod`, which uses it to choose between the restore path and the snapshotless rebuild and to name the row on `podsession.ResumeRequest`. The C2 restore driver loads that named row by `checkpoint_id` and calls `SelectLatestFullBefore` only on the contiguity or reassembly-failure arm, passing the `checkpoint_id` that failed. The resume classification is an output of the restore driver rather than a third read of the table (C6), so no consumer can disagree with another about which row a resume reads.

### C5. Build the eviction trigger seam and delete the phantom triggerForSource

**Target:** `pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointer/checkpointer.go` (`snapshot` `:236-291`; `triggerForSource` `:337-352`); `pkg/gateway/podlifecycle/prestop/prestop.go` (`CheckpointFn` `:207-212`; `Checkpoint` `:239-242`; `run` `:341-380`) and `prestop/checkpoint_adapter.go` (`CheckpointFnFor` `:18-31`); `cmd/lenny-gateway/httpsurface.go` (`:559-561`, `:617`); `pkg/checkpoint/checkpoint.go` (`RetryBudgetFor` `:118-122`).

**Change.** Add `func (c *Checkpointer) snapshotWithTrigger(ctx context.Context, tenantID, sessionID string, source sessionstore.WorkspaceSnapshotSource, trigger checkpoint.Trigger) error`, and thread `trigger` into the C2 upload driver (which selects the retry budget from `checkpoint.RetryBudgetFor(trigger)`) and into `observeDuration` (which labels `lenny_checkpoint_duration_seconds`). Delete `triggerForSource` (`:343-352`) and its dead switch; the periodic ticker and the `Seal` caller pass `checkpoint.TriggerPeriodic` explicitly. This closes T-4.4.20.

Route the gateway preStop hook's per-session checkpoint through `snapshotWithTrigger(…, checkpoint.TriggerEviction)`. `prestop.CheckpointFn` is already documented as "the per-session eviction-checkpoint surface" that "runs the §4.4 eviction-trigger checkpoint synchronously" (`prestop.go:207-212`), `run` already fires it for every session the replica coordinates under the §10.1 Stage 2 tier cap (`prestop.go:341-380`), and production already binds it to the `Checkpointer` through `CheckpointFnFor` (`prestop/checkpoint_adapter.go:18-31`, `cmd/lenny-gateway/httpsurface.go:559-561`, `:617`). Today that call lands in `Checkpointer.Checkpoint` and is therefore labeled `trigger="periodic"` and retried on the periodic budget `{200ms, 5s, 5s}`. After the change it selects `RetryBudgetFor(TriggerEviction)` (`{500ms, 5s, 30s}`), emits `lenny_checkpoint_duration_seconds{trigger="eviction"}`, and passes the hook's per-session tier-cap budget into the C2 upload driver as the row's `checkpoint_timeout_at`. That gives `checkpoint.RetryBudgetFor(TriggerEviction)` its first non-test caller and closes T-4.4.14 for the gateway-drain half.

**The two budgets on this path, and which one cuts first.** The hook bounds each per-session checkpoint by the §10.1 Stage 2 tier cap: `run` wraps the call in `context.WithTimeout(ctx, budget)` (`prestop.go:403-405`) and `CheckpointFnFor` wraps it again (`checkpoint_adapter.go:28-30`), where `budget` is 30s for a workspace at or under 100 MB, 60s to 300 MB, and 90s above that (`prestop.go:88-90`). The eviction retry budget is 30 seconds total (`pkg/checkpoint/checkpoint.go:118-122`), so at the default tier the cap and the retry budget expire together and neither leaves headroom for the other. Both are ceilings on the same attempt and whichever expires first ends it: the driver finalises `partial = true` with `manifest_reason = 'timeout'` and retains the committed chunks, or soft-deletes the row and falls through to the last successful checkpoint when `chunk_count == 0`. Neither ceiling can cut an in-flight chunk through the shipped `blobstore.Store`, whose verbs take no context and whose MinIO implementation sleeps through its own 36-second ladder on `context.Background()` (`pkg/blobstore/miniostore/miniostore.go:44`, `:259`). The context-carrying chunk-I/O surface C2 adds, and the driver owning the retry ladder instead of `miniostore`, are what make `RetryBudgetFor(TriggerEviction)` the budget that actually governs a MinIO retry and the tier cap the deadline that actually ends the attempt. The gateway-drain path writes no §4.4 minimal-state record, so no 60-second Postgres budget runs inside its per-session cap. The agent-pod eviction path does write one, and C7 runs that write on a context detached from the per-session cap and bounded by the remaining grace period (`spec/04:278`), because 30s of MinIO retries plus a 60s Postgres write cannot fit inside a 30s cap.

**Both ceilings bound the upload alone, and the bookkeeping runs on a detached context.** `run` wraps the per-session call in `context.WithTimeout(ctx, budget)` (`prestop.go:403-405`) and `CheckpointFnFor` wraps it again (`checkpoint_adapter.go:28-30`), so the deadline that ends the attempt is the deadline of the Go context the upload driver is running on. Every Postgres, object-store, and Redis call the driver must still make after that deadline fires would be issued on an already-cancelled context and return `context.DeadlineExceeded` immediately: the finalising `UPDATE` would never commit (leaving `manifest_reason` NULL and the reserved rather than the committed `chunk_count`), the `chunk_count == 0` soft-delete would never run (so `spec/10:132`'s CPS-010 arm would never fire), the abort sweep's chunk deletes would never issue, and the reservation of `probed_bytes` would never be released. The `manifest_reason = 'timeout'` finalisation and the `lenny_checkpoint_partial_total{trigger="tier_cap"}` observation this change exists to produce would be unreachable on the dominant drain path, and the leaked reservation is reconciled only on a Redis down-to-up edge (`pkg/gateway/quota/storagequota/recovery.go:29-40`), so every gateway rolling update would add up to one full `probed_bytes` per drained session to the tenant's `storage_bytes_used` permanently.

The tier cap and the retry budget therefore bound the archive stream and the chunk `Put`s alone. The finalising UPDATE, the `chunk_count == 0` soft-delete, the abort sweep, and the quota release run on a context derived with `context.WithoutCancel` from the cancelled one, under a bounded budget the **gateway** owns rather than one carved out of a terminating pod's grace period. On the gateway's own drain that budget comes out of the gateway pod's remaining `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` (240s at Tier 1/2, `spec/17:966-967`), and the driver reserves it inside the cap by shrinking the upload ceiling by it, so the two do not sum past the cap. On the agent-pod eviction path the gateway is not terminating and the pod's `budget_ms` leaves zero grace period at the instant it elapses (C7), so the bookkeeping runs on the gateway's own clock after the `NotifyPodTerminating` return, which is what keeps a slot cut at `budget_ms` from leaving an unfinalised row, undeleted chunks, and a leaked `probed_bytes` reservation that only a Redis down-to-up edge reconciles (`pkg/gateway/quota/storagequota/recovery.go:29-40`). The same rule applies on the barrier path, where `prestop.fireBarrier` bounds the whole fan-out by `bctx` (`prestop.go:475`): the BarrierAck-timeout finalisation runs on a context detached from `bctx` for the same reason.

The unhealthy-threshold drain (`slotbinder.go:598` `DrainSandbox`) is not the eviction seam and is left alone. It stamps `lenny.dev/drain-request` and does not terminate a pod carrying live sessions (`spec/06_warm-pod-model.md:178`: existing slots run to completion); its only caller is the slot-bind failure handler (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go:2192-2193`), whose session has already released its slot reservation (`start.go:2168`) and has no registry binding for `Checkpointer` to find (`checkpointer.go:237-239` returns `ErrNoBinding`); it carries a pod name with no pod-to-sessions enumeration; it is reachable only on `maxConcurrentSessions > 1` pools (`start.go:1985`); and it runs on an unrelated client's `POST /v1/sessions` request context, so a 60s `checkpoint.CheckpointTimeout` plus a 30s eviction retry budget per live slot would sit on that request and be cancelled on caller disconnect.

`checkpoint.TriggerPreScaleDown` stays unimplemented in this change and is recorded as such in §5. `pkg/controller/warmpool` is a separate binary with no checkpointer and no gateway channel, and no spec section defines a pre-scale-down checkpoint driver, so wiring it means designing a controller-to-gateway surface. That surface is a new seam rather than an existing one, unlike the agent-pod preStop hook C7 uses, which every pod already runs. A pre-scale-down release that terminates a pod carrying live sessions runs that pod's preStop hook and is therefore already covered by the C7 signal as an agent-pod eviction; the unbuilt case is a controller that wants a checkpoint *before* it decides to release.

### C6. Classify the resume from what the restore did, and hold the eviction-fallback writer for the trigger that loses a workspace

**Target:** `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/sessionserver.go` (the `PartialManifestLookup` interface `:667-673`, the server field `:273`, the `Options` field `:1258-1262`, and the constructor assignment `:1671`) and `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/start.go` (`classifyResume` `:3214-3227`, `classifyResumeWithAdapter` `:2802` and `:2899`, `emitResumedEvent` `:3247-3256` and `resumedEventPayload` `:3236-3239`, `handoffResumedFrame` `:3264-3269`, whose `full := 1.0` is the coordinator-handoff frame's only fraction, and the cleanup ordering at `:2710` and `:2786`) with `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/events.go` (`synthesizeHandoffReattach` `:285`, the frame's only caller, which already holds the context and the session row); `pkg/gateway/session/sessioncheckpointmeta/sessioncheckpointmeta.go` and its `pgstore` (the `Store` interface and `MemoryStore`, which gain the narrow `SetWorkspaceRecoveryFraction` writer the restore path needs, since the shipped `Upsert` replaces the whole row); `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/events_test.go` (`:446`, which pins `"workspaceRecoveryFraction":1` on the synthesized handoff frame and keeps that expectation for a session with no recorded fraction).

**The failed checkpoint on a gateway drain is not an eviction fallback.** Two triggers select `checkpoint.TriggerEviction`, and only one of them destroys a workspace. The C5 trigger is the gateway replica's own preStop hook. Draining a replica does not terminate the agent pod and does not destroy the workspace: the sessions the replica coordinated are re-adopted by another replica through a coordinator handoff, which "re-adopts the still-running pod without a checkpoint restore: the workspace stays intact and the pod re-executes nothing" (`spec/10_gateway-internals.md:178`, cold-start behaviour at `:112`), and a session whose barrier flush did not complete "falls back to the last successful periodic checkpoint" (`spec/10:180`). The §4.4 minimal-state record describes the opposite state: `workspace_lost` is "always `true` for this record" (`spec/04_system-components.md:274`), a resume from it "skips workspace materialization and the pod starts with an empty workspace" (`:276`), and `evictionfallback.Writer` enforces that semantic in code (`WorkspaceLost is forced true by construction`, `pkg/gateway/storage/evictionfallback/evictionfallback.go:234-236`), with a total-loss arm that publishes `session.lost` with `reason: "eviction_total_loss"` (`evictionfallback.go:26-30`).

Hanging the writer off the C5 path would therefore make a transient MinIO blip during a routine gateway rolling update write a `workspace_lost = true` row for every session the draining replica coordinates, whose pods keep running with intact workspaces, and publish `session.lost` for sessions that were never lost. That is strictly less reliable than what ships today, where the drain checkpoint returns `Unimplemented` instantly and the session keeps its last snapshot. On the C5 path a failed checkpoint falls through to the last successful checkpoint, writes no `session_eviction_state` row, and publishes nothing; the session's resume classification is unaffected and the coordinator handoff proceeds.

**Why the lookup is removed rather than widened.** `sessionserver.PartialManifestLookup` is `HasActivePartialManifest(ctx, tenantID, sessionID) (bool, error)` (`sessionserver.go:672`), a bare presence check with no slot, no `coordination_generation`, no `chunk_count`, and no byte count, and `classifyResume` returns `ResumePartialWorkspace` on that boolean alone (`start.go:3221-3226`). Four problems make it unwirable as it stands, and the fourth rules out any store-reading replacement for it.

It does not compile against the C4 store: `partialmanifeststore.Store` carries `Put`, `Get`, `LatestActive`, `SoftDelete`, `ListSoftDeletedBefore`, `HardDelete`, `DeleteByUser`, and `DeleteByTenant` (`partialmanifeststore.go:95-153`) and no `HasActivePartialManifest`; the only implementations in the tree are the interface declaration, the `classifyResume` call site, and a test fake (`resumed_event_test.go:58`).

Its predicate is wrong under S2. Every successful checkpoint now writes a `partial = false` row into the same table, and a presence check keyed on `deleted_at IS NULL` alone (which is what `LatestActive` and the shipped `partial_manifest_active_uniq` index at `migrations/0150_partial_manifest_active_uniq.up.sql:24-26` both use) returns true for every session that has ever checkpointed, so every resume after a clean full checkpoint would classify `partial_workspace`.

Its predicate is also incomplete against `spec/10:155` as S2 restates it, which gates the partial restore on three things a boolean cannot express: selection of the newest active row for `(session_id, slot_id)` in the total order `(coordination_generation DESC, checkpoint_started_at DESC, checkpoint_id DESC)`; a strictly newer `partial = false` row winning over the selected partial; and the reassembly threshold `workspace_bytes_uploaded > 0 AND chunk_count > 0 AND (baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes IS NULL OR workspace_bytes_uploaded >= baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes * gateway.partialRecoveryThresholdFraction)`, below which the session falls back to the last successful full checkpoint. S2 makes those gates the common case rather than the exception: the intent row is committed at the start of every attempt of every trigger, so a session resumed while a periodic checkpoint is in flight, or after a gateway crash mid-upload (which leaves a `partial = true, deleted_at IS NULL, chunk_count = 0` row), would classify `partial_workspace` with `workspaceLost` and `workspaceRecoveryFraction` semantics off a zero-chunk row while the C2 restore driver in fact restores `full` from the last successful checkpoint. That exposes the empty manifest `spec/10:132` says is "never exposed to the resume path (see CPS-010)" and makes `session.resumed` disagree with the restore.

A fourth problem rules out re-reading the row at classification time at all, which is what a widened `PartialManifestLookup` would do. Both cleanup paths run before the classifier, inside the same `handleResume` call. `resumeOnPod` runs first (`start.go:2710`) and reaches `s.podBinder.Resume` (`start.go:3126`), the seam C2 hangs the restore driver on, and C2 has that driver delete the chunks and soft-delete the row of a `partial = true` restore. `PartialCleaner.CleanupAfterResume` then fires on every resume (`start.go:2786`) and soft-deletes the row again (`partialcleanup.go:100`). Only afterwards does `classifyResumeWithAdapter` run (`start.go:2802`), reaching `classifyResume` (`start.go:3214`) and its lookup (`:3221`). Any selector scoped to `deleted_at IS NULL` therefore cannot see the row the resume just reassembled: it returns the older `partial = false` row or `ErrNotFound`, both of which degrade to `ResumeFull`. `ResumePartialWorkspace` would be unreachable on every production resume, `lenny_checkpoint_partial_total{recovered}` could never flip to `true`, and a session that in fact reassembled a partial workspace would emit `session.resumed` with `resumeMode: "full"` and no `workspaceRecoveryFraction`.

**Change (the classification is an output of the restore).** The §10.1 selection, fencing, and threshold predicate lives once, on the C4 store surface and in the C2 restore driver, which is the code that acts on it. The driver reports the mode it actually took on `podsession.ResumeResult.RestoreMode` (C2): `partial_workspace` when it reassembled a `partial = true` row above the threshold, and `full` on every outcome that restored a `partial = false` row, which covers a newest row that is already full, a below-threshold partial, a zero-chunk row left by an in-flight or crash-abandoned attempt, a partial outranked by a strictly newer `partial = false` row, and a contiguity failure. In each of those arms the session restores the bytes of a full checkpoint (`SelectForResume`'s fall-through, or `SelectLatestFullBefore` on the contiguity arm, which excludes the row that failed), so "reports `full`" means a full checkpoint was restored rather than that nothing was restored. A contiguity failure with no fall-back row reports no mode at all: it fails the resume with `WORKSPACE_SNAPSHOT_MISSING` and the session transitions to `failed` (C2, `spec/25:4095`), so the classifier is never reached. It reports the recovery fraction on the same result (`RecoveryFraction`, C2), which is the only place the numerator and the denominator are both in hand. `resumeOnPod` carries both values up, `classifyResumeWithAdapter` (`start.go:2802`) classifies from the mode rather than re-querying the store, and `emitResumedEvent` (`start.go:3247`) publishes the fraction on the `partial_workspace` arm. `sessionserver.PartialManifestLookup` (`sessionserver.go:667-673`) and the `partialManifestLookup` arm of `classifyResume` (`start.go:3221-3226`) are deleted with it: `classifyResume` has no other consumer of the interface, and a second reading of the manifest row is a second implementation of a predicate the restore driver already evaluated. `Options.PartialManifestLookup` is removed with it, together with the server field it feeds (`sessionserver.go:273`) and the constructor assignment that reads it (`:1671`); all four sites are in `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/sessionserver.go`. `cmd/lenny-gateway` needs no edit for it, because the field is assigned nowhere in `cmd/` today, which is why the lookup is nil in production (§1(E)). One shipped test does construct the field and is rewritten with the interface: `TestResumeEmitsPartialWorkspaceWhenPartialManifestPresent` (`pkg/gateway/sessionserver/resumed_event_test.go:119-145`) builds `sessionserver.Options{PartialManifestLookup: partial}` with the `fakePartialLookup` fake (`:52-63`) and asserts `resumeMode: "partial_workspace"` off the bare presence check, which is the behaviour this change moves onto the restore driver's `RestoreMode`. It is rewritten onto that report, and `TestResumeEmitsConversationOnlyWhenEvictionStatePresent` (`:92`) is rewritten against the C7 precedence rule.

The NULL-baseline arm is not an omission the driver may collapse: `spec/10:155` sets `threshold = 0` when `baseline_full_checkpoint_bytes IS NULL` and `spec/10:141` spells out that the check then degenerates to "any non-zero `workspace_bytes_uploaded` is eligible", which is the state of every session whose first-ever checkpoint truncates. Reaching that arm at all requires the C2 change to the resume entry condition, because such a session has no `WorkspaceSnapshot.Ref` and the shipped gate at `start.go:3084` would send it down the snapshotless rebuild path with an empty workspace.

C4 gives the partial-manifest path a producer for the first time, so the arm becomes live and a deadline-truncated checkpoint above the recovery threshold resumes `partial_workspace` instead of claiming `full`.

`evictionfallback.Writer` and `sessionserver.Options.EvictionStateLookup` are wired by C7, on the agent-pod eviction trigger, which is the termination class that destroys a workspace. Two corrections travel with that wiring and are built there rather than assumed, because wiring the lookup as it stands would be wrong:

- **Precedence.** `classifyResume` treats the presence of a `session_eviction_state` row as decisive (`start.go:3214-3219`: `HasEvictionState` → `ResumeConversationOnly`, ahead of the partial-manifest and full arms) with no comparison against the session's checkpoints. `spec/04:264` requires the opposite: "If a previous successful full checkpoint exists, the session is resumed from that checkpoint … If only the minimal state record exists" does the `conversation_only` resume apply. Because the classification is now an output of the restore, the arm reduces to what the restore did: the eviction row wins only when the resume restored nothing, which is exactly the snapshotless-rebuild path `SelectForResume`'s `ErrNotFound` selects. The classifier gains that predicate before the lookup is wired (C7).
- **Clearing.** Nothing deletes the row after a later successful checkpoint; `evictionstatestore.Delete` (`evictionstatestore.go:234`) has no non-test caller, and `spec/04:282` cleans the table only at terminal state. Without a clear-on-success path, one fallback write would make every subsequent resume of that session `conversation_only` with `workspaceLost: true` forever, discarding a durable workspace. The checkpoint driver deletes the session's row when a later checkpoint finalises `partial = false` (C7).

The C6 wiring needs no new table, no new metric label, and no spec edit: the restore driver implements the `spec/10:155` predicate the spec already states, and the classifier reports what that driver did. It deletes the `PartialManifestLookup` interface and its `Options` field, adds `RestoreMode` and `RecoveryFraction` to `podsession.ResumeResult`, widens `emitResumedEvent` (`start.go:3247`) to populate the `workspaceRecoveryFraction` field `resumedEventPayload` already declares (`start.go:3238`) and that only the coordinator-handoff frame writes today (`start.go:3264-3269`), and puts the `SelectForResume` and `SelectLatestFullBefore` selectors on the C4 store, which is where the predicate lives.

**Change (the fraction is persisted, because the coordinator-handoff frame carries it forward).** Publishing the fraction in memory is enough for the resume that computed it and not for the next coordinator handoff, which synthesizes its own `session.resumed`. Three retained statements make `session_checkpoint_meta` the source of that frame's fraction: `spec/07:138` (a prior `partial_workspace` resume's fraction "is still authoritative in `session_checkpoint_meta` … the value is carried forward verbatim"), `spec/10:392` ("`workspaceRecoveryFraction` sourced from `session_checkpoint_meta`"), and `spec/15:1421` (the same, "when present"). The column has no producer that can satisfy them: its only writer is the barrier ack path (`pkg/gateway/coordination/barrier/barrier.go:203`), whose `Ack` carries no fraction (`wiring.go:58-60`) because `CheckpointBarrierResponse` has no such field (`schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:1176-1180`), so `workspace_recovery_fraction` (`migrations/0148_session_checkpoint_meta.up.sql:44`) is always NULL and `handoffResumedFrame` hard-codes `1.0` (`start.go:3264-3269`). That is harmless only while the `partial_workspace` arm is unreachable (§1(E)); C4 and C6 make it live, and a session that reassembled 40% of its workspace and published `workspaceRecoveryFraction: 0.4` would tell the client on its next handoff that the workspace is intact.

C6 therefore persists what the restore reported. On every restore, `resumeOnPod` writes the driver's `RecoveryFraction` to the session's `session_checkpoint_meta` row: the value on a `partial_workspace` restore, and NULL on a `full` one, so a stale fraction cannot outlive the workspace it described. The shipped `Store.Upsert` (`pkg/gateway/session/sessioncheckpointmeta/sessioncheckpointmeta.go`) replaces the whole `(tenant, session)` row and would clobber the barrier's `barrier_id` and `checkpoint_ref`, so the store gains one narrow writer, `SetWorkspaceRecoveryFraction(ctx, tenantID, sessionID string, fraction *float64) error`, which writes that column alone (an INSERT with an `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` on the fraction and `updated_at`) on `MemoryStore` and on the pgstore. `handoffResumedFrame` (`start.go:3264-3269`) then reads the row rather than hard-coding: it carries the recorded fraction forward verbatim when the row holds one, and keeps `1.0` when there is no row or the column is NULL, which is the state of every session that has never resumed `partial_workspace` and is the value the frame publishes today. `synthesizeHandoffReattach` (`events.go:285`) already holds the context and the session row the read needs, and `workspaceLost: false` on the handoff arm is unchanged. `spec/07:138`, `spec/10:392`, and `spec/15:1421` are therefore confirmed unchanged: this is the producer they name, given to them for the first time.

### C7. Wire the agent-pod eviction trigger, the eviction-fallback writer, the resume precedence rule, and the eviction-state supersede

**Target:** `cmd/lenny-adapter/prestop.go` (`preStopConfig` `:18-27`, `preStopDeps` `:31-36`, `runPreStop` `:67-93`, all unchanged) plus two new `cmd/lenny-adapter` subcommands (`prestop-wait`, the wait-only hook the sidecar `runtime` container runs, and `install-drain-wait`, the initContainer that stages the binary) and `cmd/lenny-adapter/main.go` (the SIGTERM path at `:396-401`); `pkg/adapter` (the gateway-control client the adapter already holds); `pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient`; `pkg/gateway/mcpfabric/delegationtree/leasecontrol/leasecontrol.go` (`Service` `:390-395`, the **only** registered `adapterv1.GatewayControlServer` in the tree, which embeds `UnimplementedGatewayControlServer` and is therefore where `NotifyPodTerminating` must land) with `cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go` (`newGatewayControlServer` `:582`, the registration at `:700`) and `cmd/lenny-gateway/controlserver.go` (`:161`, the composition site); `pkg/gateway/storage/leasestore/leasestore.go` (the new `Steal` primitive beside `Acquire` `:125-141`, on the `LeaseStore` interface `:58-66`, whose three implementations all carry compile-time satisfaction assertions), `pkg/gateway/storage/leasestore/failover.go` (`:39`, the `*Failover` production wires) and `pkg/gateway/storage/leasestore/pgstore/pgstore.go` (`:50`, the §12.4 advisory-lock fallback); `pkg/gateway/coordination/coordlease` and the §10.1 handoff path the handler reuses, including its fence sender (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordfence/coordfence.go` `:62-64`, `:137`, `:143` and `pkg/gateway/runtime/adapterclient/coordinatorfence.go:48-54`, both of which gain the slot the handler fences each session on, P1); `pkg/gateway/coordination/coordination/coordination.go` (`:132-137`, the sweeper's `ErrHeld` stand-down arm, **unchanged**: the arm fires for every session whose lease another replica happens to hold rather than only for a displaced one, so it cannot carry a registry unbind); `pkg/gateway/session/sessionstore` (a `ListActiveByPod` selector beside the existing `GetActiveSlotsByPod`, `pgstore/pgstore.go:692`); `pkg/gateway/podlifecycle/podsession/binder.go` (a new exported `AdoptForCheckpoint`, built on the `resolveSandbox` (`:1908-1917`) and `DialAdapter` (`:56-58`) pair the unexported `reconnect` (`:1096-1100`) already uses, which is the only way the handler can reach the terminating pod's adapter) and `pkg/gateway/podlifecycle/podsession/registry.go` (`Put` and `Remove`, which the handler pairs so the bindings it creates do not outlive the RPC); `pkg/gateway/checkpoint/checkpointer/checkpointer.go` (`snapshotWithTrigger`, C5; the `partial = false` finalisation arm); `pkg/gateway/storage/evictionfallback/evictionfallback.go` (`Writer`, `:234-236`; the total-loss arm `:26-30`); `pkg/gateway/storage/evictionstatestore/evictionstatestore.go` (`Delete` `:234`); `pkg/gateway/sessionserver/sessionserver.go` (`Options.EvictionStateLookup` `:1256`) and `start.go` (`classifyResume` `:3214-3227`); `pkg/controller/sandbox/podspec/podspec.go` (`defaultTerminationGraceSeconds` `:75`, `terminationGrace` `:1193`, `preStopDrainHook` `:1229` and its two call sites at `:560` and `:645`, which are unchanged; the sidecar `runtime` container at `:562-573`, which gains the wait-only hook and the read-only `drain-bin` mount; `runtimeMounts` `:509-518`, which is the sidecar-only mount literal (the embedded container's is a separate literal at `:596`); the sidecar builder's volume assembly at `:493` (`volumes := podVolumes()`), which is where the `drain-bin` `emptyDir` is appended, because `podVolumes()` (`:823-859`) takes no `Inputs` and is called by both layouts, so adding the volume inside it would render it on embedded pods too; a new initContainer on the sidecar layout; and the container env at `:551` and `:638`); `pkg/admission/pool_config_validator/validator.go` (`decideTerminationBudget` `:585-660`, whose doc-comment formula at `:591-600`, whose rejection-rule bullets at `:602-612`, whose floor at `:635`, and whose `!= nil` guard at `:644` all change, plus `effectiveMaxConcurrent` `:452-462`) and `pkg/admission/webhook` (`pool_config_validator_test.go:191`, whose service-mode admitted-with-warning case the mode split turns into an admit with no warning, and `:215`, whose service-mode ceiling-breach rejection the mode split turns into an admit, both re-keyed onto session-mode templates in §6); `pkg/apis/lenny/v1alpha1/sandboxtemplate_types.go` (the CRD field docs and the new `SessionPolicy.MaxConcurrentSessions` field, S3) with `pkg/controller/poolscaling/poolstoresource.go` (`:65-96` and `:89-90` / `:145-159`, the poolstore→CRD mirror for both `maxConcurrentSessions` and `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`) and `poolstoresource_test.go` (`:263`); `pkg/gateway/runtime/poolstore`, `pkg/gateway/externalapi/admin/pools.go`, and `migrations/` (the `termination_grace_period_seconds` pool column and the admin-API payloads that set it, which are the only remediation for the fail-closed rejection the dropped guard produces); `docs/reference/configuration.md` (the Pool configuration table `:70-79`, which carries no `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` row, and the `sessionPolicy.maxConcurrentSessions` row `:87`, whose Validation cell states `acknowledgeProcessLevelIsolation` as the complete requirement for a concurrent pool) and `docs/api/admin.md` (`:252-268`, the `POST /v1/admin/pools` request body, and `:289`, `PUT /v1/admin/pools/{name}`); `cmd/lenny-gateway/httpsurface.go` and `sessionsrv.go` (the eviction-fallback writer and the eviction-state lookup).

**Change (where the handler lives).** gRPC admits one implementation per service name, and the gateway registers exactly one: `adapterv1.RegisterGatewayControlServer(gs, svc)` (`cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go:700`), where `svc` is the `*leasecontrol.Service` built by `newGatewayControlServer` (`main.go:582`) and composed at `cmd/lenny-gateway/controlserver.go:161`. That type embeds `adapterv1.UnimplementedGatewayControlServer` (`pkg/gateway/mcpfabric/delegationtree/leasecontrol/leasecontrol.go:390-395`), so a handler built as a free-standing component and registered anywhere else would never be reached: the RPC would answer `codes.Unimplemented` in the composed binary and the agent-pod eviction trigger would silently never fire, while unit tests that construct the component directly stayed green. `NotifyPodTerminating` is therefore a method on `leasecontrol.Service`, delegating to a new eviction-checkpoint component (holding the session store, the lease store, the `Checkpointer`, and the eviction-fallback writer) that is passed into `newGatewayControlServer` as one more `leasecontrol` seam beside `scrubReports` and wired at `controlserver.go:161`. No new gRPC service, no new listener, and no new interceptor: the existing chain the server is built with (`main.go:690-693`) already covers the new unary RPC. `cmd/lenny-gateway/httpsurface.go` and `sessionsrv.go` carry no `GatewayControl` reference today and gain none; they wire the writer and the lookup alone.

**Change (the pod side).** `preStopDrainHook`'s exec command and its `--timeout` are unchanged (`podspec.go:1229-1245`) and it stays on the containers it is on today: the podspec already computes the timeout as the grace period minus the 10s drain margin, and the hook already signals the adapter and blocks until it exits. Three things change around it.

*The sidecar `runtime` container gets a wait-only hook of its own.* `preStopDrainHook` has exactly two call sites, and they are two pod layouts rather than two containers of one pod: the `adapter` container in the sidecar model (`podspec.go:560`) and the single container in the embedded model (`:645`). The sidecar `runtime` container (`:562-573`) carries no `Lifecycle`, so the kubelet SIGTERMs the runtime process at t=0, concurrently with the adapter's hook. A Full-level runtime is exactly the process the gateway-driven eviction `Checkpoint` must quiesce (`pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:203-206` routes it through `checkpointViaLifecycle`, and `:409-411` fails the whole RPC with `codes.Internal` when `RequestCheckpoint` does not complete), so an eviction checkpoint against a sidecar Full-level pod could never quiesce and would fail on the one path the trigger exists for, dropping every such session to the minimal-state fallback.

`preStopDrainHook` cannot be reused there, on two independent grounds, and each of them reproduces the failure it is meant to fix. Its exec command is `["lenny-adapter", "prestop", "--timeout=Ns"]` (`podspec.go:1229-1245`), and a preStop `exec` handler runs inside the container it is attached to, using that container's image. The `runtime` container runs `in.RuntimeImage` (`podspec.go:562-573`), which is the third-party image from `Runtime.spec.image` (`podspec.go:236-237`) whose only contract is the socket protocol (`spec/04:867`, `:875`, `:881`), and `runtimeMounts` (`podspec.go:509-518`) carries no volume holding a Lenny binary, so the kubelet's exec would fail with "executable file not found", emit `FailedPreStopHook`, and SIGTERM the container at t=0. Only the embedded model works today because "the embedded first-party runtime links the adapter and accepts the same CLI" (`podspec.go:642-645`). Second, `runPreStop` signals and polls **PID 1 of its own container** (`const preStopProcessID = 1`, `cmd/lenny-adapter/prestop.go:12-16`; `syscall.Kill(preStopProcessID, syscall.SIGTERM)` and `syscall.Kill(preStopProcessID, 0)` at `:99-101`), which in the `runtime` container is the runtime process, so even with the binary present the hook would SIGTERM the runtime at t=0 and then poll for the exit of the process it is supposed to hold open. It cannot reach the adapter's PID instead: `shareProcessNamespace` is deliberately unset (`podspec.go:485-487`, pinned by `podspec_test.go:287-288`) and setting it is prohibited (`spec/04:243`, `spec/13:16-23`).

C7 therefore gives the `runtime` container a **wait-only** hook that runs a command guaranteed to exist in an arbitrary image and waits on something observable across the container boundary:

- A new `drain-bin` `emptyDir` volume is added to the sidecar pod, appended to the sidecar builder's own volume slice at `podspec.go:493` rather than inside `podVolumes()` (`:823-859`), which takes no `Inputs` and is shared by both layouts (`:493`, `:592`), so a volume added there would render on the embedded pod as well. An initContainer running `in.AdapterImage` stages the adapter binary into it with a new `lenny-adapter install-drain-wait --dest=/lenny/drain/lenny-adapter` subcommand, which copies its own executable (`os.Executable`) with mode `0555`. The binary is statically linked (`Dockerfile:21` builds with `CGO_ENABLED=0` onto `gcr.io/distroless/static`), so it runs under any runtime image and needs no loader, no shell, and no package manager, which the distroless adapter image has none of either.
- The `runtime` container mounts `drain-bin` read-only at `/lenny/drain` and carries `Lifecycle.PreStop.Exec.Command = ["/lenny/drain/lenny-adapter", "prestop-wait", "--timeout=Ns"]`, with the same `terminationGrace(in) - preStopDrainMarginSeconds` timeout the adapter's hook uses.
- `prestop-wait` is a new `cmd/lenny-adapter` subcommand that **signals nothing**. It dials TCP `127.0.0.1:50051` on a poll interval and returns as soon as the connection is refused, which is when the adapter's gRPC listener is gone and therefore when the adapter process has exited. The two containers share the pod's network namespace, so the adapter's existing listener is the liveness signal and no new port, no new listener, and no new NetworkPolicy edge is introduced. It returns 0 on the timeout as well, deferring to the kubelet exactly as `runPreStop` does.

The embedded model is unchanged: there the adapter is the single container's PID 1, `preStopDrainHook` is correct as it stands, and no initContainer and no `drain-bin` volume are rendered. `preStopDeps` (`prestop.go:31-36`) and `runPreStop` (`:67-93`) are unchanged.

*The adapter is given its grace period.* The podspec renders `LENNY_TERMINATION_GRACE_SECONDS` (the value `terminationGrace(in)` already computes at `podspec.go:1193`) as a plain env var on the sidecar `adapter` container and on the embedded container, alongside the existing `podNameEnv()`. This is the same env var and the same pattern the gateway pod already uses for its own preStop budget (`charts/lenny/templates/gateway-deployment.yaml:742-743`, read at `cmd/lenny-gateway/main.go:2500`), so no new mechanism is introduced. The adapter computes `budget_ms` as that grace minus `preStopDrainMarginSeconds`, minus the demote-and-exit reserve (`LENNY_DEMOTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS + 5s`), minus the wall-clock it has spent since SIGTERM, and applies that same value as the RPC's deadline, so the ceiling it declares is the one it honours (P1).

*The notification is the first act of the SIGTERM path.* Today the handler runs `ShutdownDemoteSDK` (`cmd/lenny-adapter/main.go:397`), then `lifecycle.Close()` (`:398-400`), then `srv.GracefulStop()` (`:401`). Inserting the RPC anywhere "before `GracefulStop`" is not enough: `lifecycle.Close()` fails every pending lifecycle request and rejects every later one with `errLifecycleClosed` (`pkg/adapter/lifecyclechannel.go:558-561`, `:605-614`), so a `Checkpoint` the gateway opens afterwards cannot reach a Full-level runtime's quiesce even with the gRPC server still up. The adapter therefore calls `GatewayControl.NotifyPodTerminating` (P1) on the gateway client it already holds, passing its pod name and the remaining budget, and blocks on the response under its own bounded deadline **before** all three teardown steps. The lifecycle channel and the gRPC server are both live for the whole call, which is what lets the gateway open the `Checkpoint` stream back into this adapter and quiesce its runtime. When the RPC returns, fails, or its deadline fires, the adapter runs the existing teardown in its existing order and exits, `runPreStop`'s `alive()` poll observes the exit, and both hooks return. `preStopDeps` (`prestop.go:31-36`) is unchanged, because the notification lives in the adapter process rather than in the `prestop` subcommand: the subcommand has no gateway client, no mTLS identity, and no session state, and giving it one would duplicate the adapter's whole gateway channel in a short-lived exec.

The adapter's own deadline for the RPC is exactly the budget it declares, which already excludes the drain margin and the demote-and-exit reserve, so a gateway that hangs cannot consume the grace period the kubelet is counting down and cannot cost the SDK teardown its window. A `NotifyPodTerminating` that fails outright (the gateway is unreachable, which is the case a gateway outage overlapping a node drain produces) is logged and does not block the drain: the pod exits, the session keeps its last successful checkpoint, and the §7.2 retry mechanism resumes it on a replacement pod. This is the same degradation the pod has today, so the failure mode is not new.

**Change (the gateway side).** The RPC lands on an arbitrary replica. The adapter dials the load-balanced `lenny-gateway` ClusterIP Service once at startup (`pkg/adapter/gatewaylink.go:54`; the Service sets no `sessionAffinity`, `charts/lenny/templates/gateway-deployment.yaml:1601-1618`), the chart runs two replicas by default and up to 30 at Tier 3 (`charts/lenny/values.yaml:2074`), and `spec/10:22` makes sticky routing an optimization, so the receiving replica coordinates the terminating pod's sessions only by chance. Every surface the handler needs is otherwise per-replica: `podsession.Registry` is a process-local map (`pkg/gateway/podlifecycle/podsession/registry.go:8-17`) written only by the replica that ran start or resume (`start.go:2232`), `Checkpointer.snapshot` returns `ErrNoBinding` without a local binding (`checkpointer.go:236-239`), and the pod rejects any gateway RPC that does not carry its fenced `coordination_generation` (`spec/10:37`).

The handler therefore takes over coordination before it checkpoints, using the §10.1 handoff protocol that already exists for exactly this transition:

1. Resolve the sessions bound to the pod from `sessions.pod_assignment` (a `ListActiveByPod` selector on the `idx_sessions_active_by_pod` partial index the `GetActiveSlotsByPod` count query already uses, `migrations/0080_sessions_active_by_pod_index.up.sql`, `pkg/gateway/session/sessionstore/pgstore/pgstore.go:692`). This is a Postgres read and is correct on any replica; the process-local registry is not consulted.
2. For each session it holds no `podsession.Registry` binding for, open a connection to the terminating pod's adapter and bind the pod in this replica's registry; and for **every** session, run the §10.1 coordinator handoff over that connection: take the lease with `leasestore.Steal` when this replica does not already hold it, then, in one transaction, bump `coordination_generation` through the `spec/10:33` CAS and **reserve the slot's eviction checkpoint** through the C2 driver's reservation entry point (mint the `checkpoint_id`, supersede any prior active partial row under the S2 predicate, and commit the intent row at the post-CAS generation), and then fence the pod with `CoordinatorFence` (`spec/10:35`) over that connection. The `Steal` is the only step that is conditional. The CAS, the reservation, and the fence run for every session the handler checkpoints, whichever replica holds the lease.

   **The reservation runs here rather than at execution time, because `coordination_generation` cannot exclude the displaced replica's *next* attempt.** The generation is a property of the session row rather than of a replica, and every replica re-reads it: a displaced coordinator's next periodic tick reads the post-CAS value in the transaction that commits its own intent row (§3), so it writes at the handler's generation rather than under a superseded one. The pod's entry gate does not exclude it either. The gate C1 builds rejects a `coordination_generation` **below** the slot's `lastFenced` and admits one equal to it, which is what the shipped barrier gate does (`gen != fenced` → `coordinator_handoff_stale`, `pkg/adapter/coordination.go:157`), so a request carrying the post-CAS value is accepted. A handler that ran the handoff for every session first and only then checkpointed the slots serially would therefore leave a window, minutes wide on a `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` pod whose slots each run under a tier cap of up to 90s plus a 60s fallback, in which the replica holding the pod binding still drives that session's periodic checkpoints: `Checkpointer.Sweep` iterates the process-local registry and `snapshot` gates only on `Registry.Get` (`checkpointer.go:173-176`, `:236-239`), neither consults a lease, and nothing tells that replica the pod is terminating. A tick inside that window commits an active `partial = true` row at the post-CAS generation carrying no `manifest_reason` and inside its own deadline, which is exactly the row the supersede predicate refuses to displace, so the handler's own attempt for that slot would abort on the settled-row term and the slot would take the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback against a healthy object store and a live, quiescable pod, which is the loss the whole trigger exists to prevent; the peer's accepted `Checkpoint` stream would also hold the adapter's pod-global op-lock against the handler's next slot (`pkg/adapter/server.go:328`, `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:178-186`). Reserving the slot inside the handoff closes the window on the row rather than on the number: once the handler's intent row is the active row for `(session_id, slot_id)`, a peer's reservation meets it at equal generation, with no `manifest_reason`, inside its deadline, and aborts on the settled-row term, which is the arm S2 defines for exactly this state. Committing the reservation in the same transaction as the CAS is what leaves no read-then-write interleaving: a peer that read the pre-CAS generation and races the INSERT loses `partial_manifest_active_uniq` and, on the retry the racer already performs (§6), re-reads and aborts on the handler's row; a peer that reads the post-CAS generation aborts on the same row directly. The generation bump and the fence keep their own job, which is displacing the upload **already in flight** on another replica (that row is at a strictly lower generation, so the supersede admits it, and the fence cancels its stream and frees the op-lock). The single-flight lock cannot substitute for the reservation here: it is process-local and the peer is another replica.

   **The reserved row's `checkpoint_timeout_at` is the pod-declared budget deadline** (`reservation_time + budget_ms`, S2), rather than the slot's Stage 2 tier cap. The recorded deadline is what every peer's supersede predicate and every §12.5 backstop cycle read as the liveness marker of a running attempt, and a slot reserved at handoff time and executed several minutes later would otherwise carry a deadline that had already elapsed by the time the handler reached it, which is a row any peer may supersede and any backstop cycle may sweep out from under the running upload. The budget is the wall clock inside which the reserved attempt genuinely settles, because the handler cuts it at `budget_ms` and finalises it on the detached context (below). The slot's tier cap stays the ceiling on that slot's own upload, as the Go context deadline the driver runs the archive stream and the chunk `Put`s under.

   **The bind and the `Steal` are two gates, because the binding and the lease are held by uncorrelated replicas.** Nothing on the session-start path acquires the coordination lease, the sweeper races `Acquire` on every non-terminal session of every tenant (`coordination.go:109`, `:133-137`), and the binding is written by whichever replica served start or resume (`start.go:2232`, `:3150`), which is the same fact that keeps the sweeper's `ErrHeld` arm from carrying a registry unbind. A single gate keyed on the lease would skip the bind entirely in the state {replica A holds the lease, replica B holds the binding}, which is the majority of sessions at any replica count above one: A would never dial the pod and never bind it, and `Checkpointer.snapshot`'s first act (`binding, ok := c.Registry.Get(sessionID)`, `checkpointer.go:236-239`) would return `ErrNoBinding`, so every such slot would take the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback against a healthy object store and a live, quiescable pod, which is the outcome `leasestore.Steal` was added to prevent. A replica that already holds the lease but no binding therefore still dials and binds, and a replica that holds the binding but not the lease still runs the `Steal`.

   **The CAS and the fence are unconditional, because the lease does not gate the upload the eviction checkpoint has to displace.** `Checkpointer.Sweep` iterates the process-local registry and `snapshot` gates only on `Registry.Get(sessionID)` (`checkpointer.go:174-176`, `:236-239`); neither consults a lease, so the replica that drives a session's periodic checkpoints is the binding holder rather than the lease holder. In the state {replica A holds the lease and no binding, replica B holds the binding and has a chunk `Put` in flight}, a handler that skipped the CAS and the fence because it already held the lease would open its eviction attempt at B's own generation. Its supersede would then meet B's active row at equal generation with no `manifest_reason` and inside its deadline, which is the settled-row term's abort arm (§2, S2), and the attempt would abort; with no fence sent, B's stream would also keep the adapter's pod-global op-lock (`pkg/adapter/server.go:328`, `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:178-190`), so the eviction `Checkpoint` would queue behind it or return `codes.Aborted`. The slot would take the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state fallback against a healthy object store and a quiescable pod, which is the loss the whole trigger exists to prevent. Bumping the generation and fencing on every session makes every eviction attempt strictly newer than any upload in flight on any replica, so the supersede takes the `coordination_generation < $new_generation` arm and the accepted fence cancels the victim's stream and releases the op-lock before the eviction checkpoint contends for it. The single-flight lock cannot substitute: it is process-local, and A and B are different replicas.

   **The handler holds no adapter connection to the terminating pod, and none of its other dependencies produces one, so C7 gives it the seam.** Every gateway→pod act the handler performs needs a live `*adapterclient.Client` for that specific pod: `coordfence.Fencer.Fence` takes the client as a parameter (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordfence/coordfence.go:137`), and `Checkpointer.snapshot` reaches the pod only through the registry binding's client (`checkpointer.go:237-241`, `binding.Adapter.Checkpoint`). The only producers of a client in the tree are `Binder.connect` (`binder.go:1625-1631`) and `Binder.reconnect` (`:1096-1100`), both unexported, both of which resolve the Sandbox CR by name and dial `status.podIP` through the `DialAdapter` closure the composition root hands the `Binder` (`cmd/lenny-gateway/stores.go:1839`). The column the handler reads cannot substitute for one: `sessions.pod_assignment` carries the Sandbox name (`start.go:753`, `row.PodAssignment = bound.SandboxName`) rather than a pod address. C7 therefore adds one exported entry point to the existing producer rather than a second dialing path: `podsession.Binder.AdoptForCheckpoint(ctx, sandboxName, sessionID, slotID) (*BindResult, error)`, built on the same `resolveSandbox` (`binder.go:1908-1917`) and `DialAdapter` pair `reconnect` already uses, returning the `BindResult` the handler puts in its registry. It claims no pod, assigns no slot, mints no credentials, and starts no session, because the session is already running on that pod, and the gateway `ClusterRole` already grants the `get` on `sandboxes` it needs (`charts/lenny/templates/gateway-deployment.yaml:142-147`). The eviction-checkpoint component therefore holds the `*podsession.Binder` alongside the session store, the lease store, the `Checkpointer`, and the eviction-fallback writer, and `cmd/lenny-gateway` already carries the binder on its wiring struct (`wiring_fields.go:308`) at the `controlserver.go:161` composition site, so nothing new is constructed. A pod the handler cannot resolve or cannot dial is a failed slot and takes the minimal-state fallback below rather than a silent skip.

   **The handler removes every binding for the terminating pod from its own registry.** A binding it puts in its own registry has no other lifecycle owner: entries are removed only by `PodExecutor.Release` (`pkg/gateway/session/executor/pod.go:313-316`), which runs on the replica serving the session's teardown rather than on the arbitrary replica this RPC landed on. A binding left behind would name a destroyed pod forever, and `Checkpointer.Sweep`, which iterates `Registry.Snapshot()` and consults no lease (`checkpointer.go:173-176`), would keep driving a periodic checkpoint for that session on every tick. Under this change each such tick commits an intent row before it opens the `Checkpoint` RPC that cannot succeed, so it would churn manifest rows and hold an active same-generation row carrying no `manifest_reason` and inside its own deadline, which is exactly the row the supersede predicate refuses to displace, aborting the session's genuine checkpoint on its replacement pod for as long as the doomed RPC runs. The handler therefore removes **every** binding for the terminating pod from its own registry and closes every client it dialed in a `defer` on the way out, whether the slot settled or the budget elapsed. That includes a binding that already existed on this replica, which is the case where the RPC happened to land on the session's serving replica: `PodExecutor.Release` cannot be left as its owner, because it runs on the session's **teardown** and an evicted session is not terminating, it is losing its pod, so the binding would sit in the registry naming a destroyed pod for the rest of the session's life. The pod is gone by construction on this path, so no binding naming it can be valid afterwards.

   **Every other replica retires its own stale binding, because the handler cannot reach a registry it does not own.** `podsession.Registry` is a process-local map (`registry.go:14-17`) and the RPC lands on one replica, so the binding held by whichever replica served the session's start or resume survives the pod unless that replica retires it itself, and nothing retires it today: `Registry.Remove` has exactly one caller in the tree, `PodExecutor.Release` (`pkg/gateway/session/executor/pod.go:313`), the session stays non-terminal across the eviction, and the resume puts a **new** binding in the registry of whichever replica serves it (`start.go:3150`) without touching the old one. That leftover is inert before this change, because `Checkpoint` returns `codes.Unimplemented` and writes nothing. Under this change every tick of `Checkpointer.Sweep` on the stale replica reserves a checkpoint, which commits an intent row before the RPC that cannot succeed, and holds an active same-generation row carrying no `manifest_reason` and inside its own deadline, which is exactly the row the supersede predicate refuses to displace, so it would abort the session's genuine checkpoint on its replacement pod for as long as the doomed RPC runs. C7 therefore gives the binding a reclaimer inside the C2 reservation, where the read is already being made: the reservation transaction reads the session row for its authoritative `coordination_generation` (§3), so it also reads `pod_assignment`, which carries the Sandbox name (`start.go:753`), and compares it against the `SandboxName` on the registry binding it is about to checkpoint (`podsession.BindResult.SandboxName`, `binder.go:487-488`). A disagreement means the session has been re-bound to a different pod under this replica's binding: the driver drops the binding with `Registry.Remove`, returns `ErrNoBinding`, and commits nothing, so no intent row is written, no supersede runs, and no `Checkpoint` RPC is opened. The second arm covers the window before the session is resumed, in which `pod_assignment` still names the destroyed Sandbox: a `Checkpoint` RPC that fails because the pod is gone (the dial fails, or the binder can no longer resolve the Sandbox) drops the binding on the same abort path that already soft-deletes the attempt's zero-chunk row. A stale binding therefore costs at most one aborted attempt, which retires it, rather than one intent row per tick for the session's remaining life.

   **Taking the lease needs one new store primitive, because the shipped store cannot take a lease from a live holder.** `leasestore.Store.Acquire` "succeeds when the lease is unheld, and is idempotent when holder already owns it … Returns ErrHeld when a different holder owns the lease" (`pkg/gateway/storage/leasestore/leasestore.go:125-141`); the coordination sweeper skips a session on `ErrHeld` (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordination/coordination.go:132-137`) and takes over only when a lease lapses on its TTL (`coordination.go:4-8`); and the Postgres `coordination_lease` table is only a mirror of the Redis lease, with an interface carrying `Upsert`, `Release`, `ListHeldByReplica`, `DeleteByUser`, and `DeleteByTenant` and no compare-and-swap (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordlease/coordlease.go:5-9`, `:61-84`), so it is not the thing a takeover CASes. A live coordinator renews continuously, so on the termination path `Acquire` would return `ErrHeld` for roughly `(N − 1) / N` of sessions and every one of them would fall to the minimal-state fallback with a healthy object store and a live, quiescable pod, which is worse than the shipped behavior at every replica count above one.

   C7 therefore adds `Steal(ctx, tenantID, sessionID, holder, ttl) (Lease, string, error)` to the `LeaseStore` interface (`leasestore.go:58-66`), a takeover that overwrites the holder unconditionally and returns the displaced one, alongside `Acquire` rather than replacing it. It is called only by the `NotifyPodTerminating` handler; every other caller keeps `Acquire` and its `ErrHeld` stand-down.

   **It is implemented on all three of the interface's implementations, because the value production hands the handler is not the Redis store.** `LeaseStore` is a role interface with compile-time satisfaction assertions on `*leasestore.Store` (`leasestore.go:67`), `*leasestore.Failover` (`failover.go:39`), and `*pgstore.Store` (`pgstore/pgstore.go:50`), and `cmd/lenny-gateway/stores.go:1272` wraps the Redis store in `leasestore.NewFailover(...)` whenever Postgres is wired, so the handler holds a `*Failover`. A `Steal` that existed only as a Redis Lua script on `*Store` would not compile against the interface and would be unreachable through the wired value, which is the failure the primitive exists to prevent. It is also a §12.4 durable-fallback role: `spec/12:217` mandates a Postgres advisory-lock fallback for distributed session leases, and every other lease verb honours it through `Failover` plus `leasestore/pgstore`, so a Redis-only `Steal` would drop every terminating pod's sessions to the `workspace_lost = true` minimal-state record during a Redis outage. The three implementations are: a Lua compare-and-set on `*Store` (atomic read-and-overwrite of the holder key, returning the displaced holder); a `pg_advisory_xact_lock`-guarded unconditional `UPDATE session_leases SET holder = $new … RETURNING` on `pgstore.Store`, which is the same locking discipline its `Acquire` already uses; and primary-then-fallback routing on `Failover`, identical to its other verbs. The `leasestore` package doc's invariant that "a lease can never be stolen or renewed by a replica that does not hold it" (`leasestore.go:13-14`) is corrected in the same edit to name the §4.6.1 termination-path exception, which is the one caller that may.

   The steal is safe on the machinery the store and the spec already carry, and the mechanism is the sweeper's `Acquire` rather than `Renew`. `leasestore.Renew` has no caller anywhere in `pkg/` or `cmd/` outside `Failover`'s own forwarding (`failover.go:81`, `:86`); the coordination sweeper renews by calling `Acquire`. (`credrenewal` is not a caller: its `Renewer.Renew(ctx, lease)` (`pkg/gateway/credentials/credrenewal/credrenewal.go:57-61`, invoked at `:231`) is the §4.9 credential-lease surface, a package-local interface over a different `Lease` type, and the package imports no `leasestore`.) The coordination sweeper renews by calling `Acquire`, which "is idempotent for the current holder — it refreshes the TTL — so one `Acquire` call per session per sweep both claims new sessions and renews held ones" (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordination/coordination.go:10-14`), and it stands down on `ErrHeld` by skipping the session (`coordination.go:132-137`). After the steal, the displaced coordinator's next sweep therefore calls `Acquire`, receives `ErrHeld`, skips the session, and stops renewing and stops mirroring it into `coordination_lease`. `Release` is already a no-op for a non-holder (`leasestore.go:170-172`); the `coordlease` mirror is brought forward with the existing `Upsert`, whose own doc says "A cross-replica handoff overwrites coordinator_replica with the new holder" (`coordlease.go:61-67`); and `CoordinatorFence` is a hard precondition of every gateway→pod RPC (`spec/10:35`), enforced on the `Checkpoint` and `Resume` streams by the `coordination_generation` field P1 adds and the entry gate C1 builds. The entry gate does **not** cover the displaced coordinator's later requests, and nothing here claims it does: the gate rejects a generation below the slot's `lastFenced` and admits one equal to it, and a later attempt on the displaced replica reads the post-CAS generation from the session row before it opens the RPC, so it presents the fenced value. What excludes that attempt is the handler's own intent row, reserved for every slot inside the handoff transaction (step 2), which the peer's reservation meets at equal generation, inside its deadline, with no `manifest_reason`, and aborts on. The fence's job is the stream that is already running: S3 restates `spec/10:36` so an accepted `CoordinatorFence` cancels every in-flight RPC of a superseded generation before it acknowledges, and C1 has the handler register a cancel func the fence invokes, so the victim's `Checkpoint` returns and releases the adapter's pod-global op-lock (`pkg/adapter/server.go:328`, `pkg/adapter/checkpoint.go:178-190`) before the new coordinator's eviction `Checkpoint` contends for it. Without that cancellation the victim would hold the lock for its full 60-second deadline and the new coordinator's checkpoint would queue behind it or fail with `codes.Aborted`. S3 states both rules in `spec/10:30-37`. No new RPC, no new RBAC, and no new network edge appears.

   **The displaced replica's checkpointer keeps its binding, and the sweeper's `ErrHeld` arm is unchanged.** `Checkpointer.Sweep` iterates the process-local `podsession.Registry` (`checkpointer.go:174-176`) and `snapshot` gates only on `Registry.Get(sessionID)` (`:236-239`); neither consults a lease, so a replica that loses the lease keeps driving periodic checkpoints for the stolen session. Hanging the unbind off the sweeper's `ErrHeld` arm was considered and rejected, because that arm is not a displacement signal. `Sweeper.Sweep` enumerates every non-terminal session of every tenant and races to `Acquire` each one (`coordination.go:109`, `:133-137`), nothing on the session-start path acquires the coordination lease, and the binding is written by whichever replica served start or resume (`start.go:2232`, `:3150`), so the lease holder and the binding holder are uncorrelated and the arm fires on every sweep for every session another replica happens to have won the lease race for. Under that edit a replica serving a healthy session whose lease a peer's sweeper reached first would drop its own binding: the session would stop being checkpointed by anyone (the lease holder never bound the pod and returns `ErrNoBinding`), its pod and its concurrency slot would leak (`PodExecutor.Release` is the only `Registry.Remove` caller and returns nil when the binding is absent, `pod.go:313-316`, so neither `binder.Release` nor `ReleaseSlot` runs and §6.2 recycling never sees occupancy return to zero), and the pod would become unreachable for message dispatch, interrupt, upload-to-session, expiry warnings, and tree recovery, all of which resolve the adapter through `podRegistry.Get`. The wasted work the unbind was meant to save is bounded without it, and the binding has a reclaimer of its own. The displaced replica's next periodic attempt reads the post-CAS `sessions.coordination_generation` in the transaction where it would commit its intent row (§3), meets the handler's reserved row for that slot at **equal** generation with no `manifest_reason` and inside its deadline, and aborts on the settled-row term at the reservation, so it commits no row, opens no `Checkpoint` RPC, and contends for no op-lock. Its binding names a pod that is destroyed by construction on this path, and the pod's grace period does not retire it, because `Registry.Remove` has exactly one caller (`PodExecutor.Release`, `pkg/gateway/session/executor/pod.go:313-316`), which runs on the replica serving the session's teardown. The stale-binding reclaimer below is what retires it.

   The generation bump is what fences any upload still in flight for that session on any replica, whether that replica held the lease or only the binding, which is why it runs unconditionally. Such an upload's row carries no `manifest_reason` and sits inside its own deadline, which is the signature of an upload still running, so the S2 predicate's **equal-generation** arm would refuse it; the new attempt's generation is **strictly higher**, so the `coordination_generation < $new_generation` arm admits the supersede. That arm is exactly what the fence licenses: the fence ack cancels the victim's `Checkpoint` stream at the pod and frees the operation lock the new coordinator's checkpoint needs, any chunk `Put` the victim had already issued is wasted bytes the widened backstop sweeps from the soft-deleted row, and its next counter `UPDATE` or its finalising `UPDATE` affects zero rows under the `deleted_at IS NULL` guard, so it aborts and sweeps its own chunks (S3).
3. For each slot in slot-ID order, execute the attempt reserved for it in step 2 through `Checkpointer.snapshotWithTrigger(…, checkpoint.TriggerEviction)` (C5), which enters the C2 upload driver at its second, execution entry point with the already-minted `checkpoint_id` and its already-committed intent row, exactly as the barrier path does after its own reservation (C2). No slot mints, supersedes, or INSERTs at this step, so nothing here can meet a row a peer has committed in the meantime. Serialization across slots is the `spec/05:542` rule and it is what keeps a `maxConcurrentSessions: 8` pod from putting eight concurrent archive relays on one gateway replica during a node drain. Each slot's upload is bounded by that slot's §10.1 Stage 2 tier cap, selected from `last_checkpoint_workspace_bytes` exactly as `prestop.Hook.run` already selects it (`prestop.go:341-380`, `:88-90`), and the whole fan-out is bounded by the `budget_ms` the pod declared, which is also the reserved rows' `checkpoint_timeout_at`.

A gateway-to-gateway forwarding RPC is rejected. No peer surface exists (`GatewayControl` is adapter→gateway only, `schemas/lenny-adapter.proto:198-202`; the only cross-replica Service is the ops-facing headless `lenny-gateway-pods`, `gateway-deployment.yaml:1637-1652`), `coordination_lease.coordinator_replica` records an OTel `service.instance.id` rather than an address (`pkg/gateway/coordination/coordlease/coordlease.go:72-74`), and forwarding would need a new RPC, an addressing scheme, a NetworkPolicy edge, and spec text, where the handoff needs one Lua script on a store interface that already exists and whose `Renew` contract already anticipates being taken over. Skipping a slot the replica does not coordinate is not an option either: in a two-replica deployment it would make the trigger a no-op for roughly half of terminating pods, which is the loss T-4.4.14 and T-4.6.4 exist to close, and a single-replica kind test would not catch it.

A slot whose handoff cannot complete (Redis is unreachable so the `Steal` cannot commit, the `coordination_generation` CAS loses its race three times, the reservation that commits with it aborts on the supersede guard against an active row at a **higher** generation, or `CoordinatorFence` cannot be acked), and a slot that has not settled when `budget_ms` elapses, is a failed slot and takes the §4.4 minimal-state fallback below, because the pod and its workspace are destroyed either way. The higher-generation abort is the CPS-006 fence doing its job: another coordinator has already fenced this session at a newer generation, and this handler must not clobber its upload. A handoff failure is an infrastructure failure rather than the routine case: with `Steal` in place, a lease held by a live coordinator is taken rather than refused.

`checkpoint.RetryBudgetFor(TriggerEviction)` (`{500ms, 5s, 30s}`) governs each slot's MinIO retries, through the driver-owned retry ladder over the context-carrying store surface C2 adds. Without that surface the first chunk `Put` against an unreachable MinIO would block for `miniostore`'s uncancellable 36-second ladder (`miniostore.go:44`, `:259`) and neither the tier cap nor the retry budget could cut it, which on this path means the kubelet SIGKILLs the pod mid-upload. That is why the agent-pod trigger cannot ship without C2's `PutContext`.

**Change (the eviction-fallback writer's call site).** When a slot's eviction checkpoint exhausts its MinIO retry budget, when its coordination handoff cannot be taken, or when the pod-declared budget elapses with the slot unsettled, the gateway writes the §4.4 minimal-state record through `evictionfallback.Writer`, increments `lenny_checkpoint_eviction_fallback_total`, and logs the committed chunk keys (`spec/04:280`). All three arms leave the session with no fresh checkpoint and a workspace that dies with the pod, which is the state the record represents. Exhausting the Postgres budget as well takes the total-loss arm the writer already implements (`evictionfallback.go:26-30`): `session.lost` with `reason: "eviction_total_loss"` and `lenny_session_eviction_total_loss_total`.
